Rhen used the waiting time to walk the ship. Dev had found a hairline buckle near the forward truss wound, inherited from whichever version of Morrow Glass remained. The buckle did not threaten the current run, but it changed the vessel’s safe mass for sharp maneuvering.

“How much?” Rhen asked.

“Four percent off lateral load until we brace it.”

“Time?”

“Six hours with dock stock. Ten using ours.”

“We have three.”

“Then don’t throw the ship sideways.”

Rhen marked the limit on the command board. The next scheduled leg carried the remaining biological cargo to three orbital settlements. After that, Talia wanted the evidence packet delivered to Kest Civic Court before Continuity House could challenge Pelagos custody. The court lay on the opposite side of the gate complex from the settlement route. Taking it added two hours and risked missing the last feeder departure.

A clean schedule still existed on paper. Every real condition had begun cutting pieces from it.

At the end of inspection, Kest Control released Morrow Glass with a warning that unscheduled operations would void local priority. Rhen signed because arguing consumed the same hours everyone lacked.

The carrier left cradle six on Day 5 with nine containers and too little traffic around it. Settlement One accepted graft tissue and immune seed. Settlement Two took antivenom substrate. At each stop, clinic crews asked whether more carriers followed. Rhen answered with names only when a departure had actually committed.

Near Settlement Three, the feeder craft Low Field missed its check-in.

Yori caught the distress trace beneath local cargo chatter. “Weak beacon, forty thousand kilometers inward. They lost primary power.”

Rhen plotted the craft. Low Field ran food and dialysis patients between two small orbital communities. Its route should have kept it near rescue tugs. Rationing had reassigned those tugs to guard fuel depots.

“How many aboard?”

“Crew of four. Passenger register says eleven.”

“Life support?”

“Beacon estimates ninety minutes.”

Karo opened the local authority channel. “Settlement control has dispatched a response.”

The response craft showed on Rhen’s board. Its arrival estimate read two hours, twelve minutes.

“Response arrives after air,” Rhen said.

“Control orders us to maintain medical schedule.”

One container remained urgent: antifungal cultures for Settlement Three’s hydroponic beds. Delay beyond four hours risked losing half the inoculant. If the beds stayed untreated, food production would fall within weeks. Low Field carried fifteen people who would run out of breathable air in less than two.

Throughput did not become cruel because it used large numbers. It became cruel when someone pretended a large future erased a small present without choosing.

Rhen turned the plot toward Low Field.

Karo released his harness. “Captain.”

“Sit down.”

“Local priority forbids unscheduled operations.”

“Local priority also claims it sent a rescue.”

“You have structural restrictions.”

“Four percent lateral. We won’t use it.”

Yori had already issued hard-strap orders. Dev shifted power from cargo cold to maneuvering reserve. The remaining cultures could tolerate the draw for thirty minutes.

Karo stood behind Rhen’s couch. “If you lose this cargo, Settlement Three may lose a harvest.”

“I heard the same numbers you did.”

“Then follow them.”

Rhen looked back. “Numbers don’t follow themselves.”

Morrow Glass accelerated along a shallow curve. Direct pursuit would have required the lateral burn Dev had prohibited. Rhen used Kest’s gravity to bend the approach, trading eleven minutes for a safer load. Low Field appeared as a cold speck turning against the planet.

The feeder craft had vented attitude gas and could not stop its spin. Its beacon painted a broken circle. Through magnification, Rhen saw frost around the passenger section.

“Dock collar won’t hold that rotation,” Yori said.

“We don’t dock.” Rhen brought the heavy carrier alongside at matched spin. “Cargo sling from truss three. Catch their rescue ring and damp through winch.”

“The buckle sits six meters from that load path.”

“So keep tension under half.”

Yori and two riggers crossed the external spine in pressure suits. Their lamps moved over the hull in bright steps. The sling paid out toward Low Field, missed once, then wrapped the feeder’s rescue ring. Each rotation pulled against Morrow Glass. Rhen felt the buckle answer as a faint click through the frame.

Dev said, “Thirty-eight percent safe tension.”

“Hold it.”

“We need forty-four to damp before their air estimate.”

“Use the cargo tug.”

The tug that had carried the last container sat clamped beneath truss four. Yori released it remotely and fired its small thrusters against the feeder’s spin. The combined forces turned a dangerous oscillation into a slow roll.

Settlement control demanded Rhen abandon the attempt and wait for authorized rescue. Karo muted them this time.

“Record the order,” he said. “Do not answer.”

At matched rotation, the riggers pulled a temporary air line across. Pressure rose inside Low Field with twenty-three minutes remaining on its estimate. The feeder’s pilot came over the channel coughing and furious.

“You took your time.”

Rhen laughed once. Relief needed somewhere to go. “File a complaint.”

“Already drafting it.”

Passenger transfer took another forty minutes. Two dialysis patients crossed in rigid litters. A child in a yellow pressure hood refused to release a bag of dried beans until Yori promised the beans counted as medical cargo. The final passenger carried a case of station water records against his chest.

Their bodies added 1.1 tonnes with gear and litters. The empty tug subtracted half that. Rhen recalculated the Settlement Three approach twice.

The antifungal container reached its destination three hours and forty-one minutes late. Cold records stayed within viability. The hydroponic supervisor accepted it without gratitude because gratitude would not sterilize damaged beds.

Kest Control revoked Morrow Glass’s local priority before the last passenger disembarked.

Karo read the notice in the command blister. “You can appeal after the emergency review.”

“Can the review fly cargo?”

“No.”

“Then it can wait.”

Rhen opened the route board. Without priority, the carrier would miss its authorized return to Pelagos. Jun’s monitored run would expire. Continuity security could impound the ship at Kest Gate. A legal captain would seek a berth and preserve the asset.

Talia’s evidence packet remained sealed in the custody locker.

Rumor had already changed traffic. A verified record might change it harder, perhaps toward panic, perhaps toward truth. Rhen thought of the clinic gallery, the five insulin lots where seven had been expected, and the fifteen people aboard a feeder craft whose authorized rescue arrived too late. None of them could eat a principle. None could survive a system that hid whom its principles consumed.

They called Talia through the monitored channel.

Karo stayed at his couch. He no longer reached for the mute control.

“Kest Civic Court has opened an emergency receipt window,” Talia said. “It closes in fifty-two minutes.”

“We lost local priority.”

“I saw the notice.”

“Your court route gives Control grounds to seize us.”

“Yes.”

Rhen waited for persuasion. Talia offered none.

Yori brought the ship around Kest’s night side. The nearer moon rose over the northern burn. Cargo restraints clicked behind the blister as empty cradles settled into their locks.

“File our destination,” Rhen said. “Civic Court anchor, custody transfer under emergency evidence rule.”

Karo looked down at Jun’s release order. “That exceeds the monitored medical run.”

“The medical run ended when your system removed our priority for rescuing its passengers.”

“Heavy carriers do not have standing to interpret orders.”

“Good. This one has a captain.”

Rhen committed Morrow Glass to Talia’s evidence route. On the board, the authorized schedule went dark, and a new approach line carried the sealed census toward Kest.

Chapter 9 — Proof of Weather

Talia erased the answer before she allowed Isca to see the question.

The Pelagos weather room had never expected a meteorologist. Its main table still bore exclusion-field controls, and the wall displays treated stars as sources of instrument noise rather than weather. Talia had spent the last hour turning Maelin’s spare chamber into a test no sympathetic witness could rescue.

She removed every current-Kest archive after AY 60. She cut Pelagos from the Concord astronomy service. She sealed the receiver’s civic channels and left only six raw sensor bands available for delayed return. Finally, she placed three independent recorders in local custody, each with a different clock.

Isca watched through the glass partition. Her impatience had become exact. She did not pace. She counted each device Talia disconnected and each person allowed into the room.

Maelin leaned over the isolation board with a driver between her teeth. “If you remove another useful feed, our adversarial test will consist of staring at a dark screen and distrusting it.”

“Useful feeds carry contamination.”

“Everything carries contamination if you define usefulness broadly enough.”

“That objection goes in the method note.”

Maelin pulled the driver from her mouth. “You do know how to punish a joke.”

Talia checked the custody roster. Sal Varo stood inside the door. Two Ministry observers waited at separate consoles. Neither had participated in the civic relay. Isca would enter only after committing her forecasts. Talia herself would not touch the sensor banks once the test began.

The design needed to survive Jun Orra.

Population proofs established institutions, relationships, and a living count. They did not establish independent history. Jun could argue that Pelagos had assembled a coherent reconstruction from current-Kest records, Isca’s memories, and decades of leaked transit data. The argument required extraordinary machinery, but no more extraordinary than a world phase-locked around a reserve star.

Weather offered a harsher line.

At AY 61, a coronal storm struck current Kest. Charged particles damaged northern grids, burned the cloud forests, and forced cities underground. Kest-Blue claimed that storm had missed. After the divergence, its atmosphere, instruments, and solar records developed without access to current archives. A precise forecast from that branch could point to events no present record contained.

Talia opened the inner door. “State your understanding before we begin.”

Isca entered carrying nothing. “You want me to predict solar weather from home. You will compare my predictions against field readings that arrive through Pelagos after I commit.”

“What information have you received since the civic relay closed?”

“None.”

“Have you seen the raw arrays?”

“No.”

“Did Maelin describe current readings?”

“No. She complained about your wiring.”

“That statement matches my record,” Maelin said.

Talia pointed Isca toward a clean console. “You may request pre-AY 61 stellar baselines common to both Kests. You may use standard physical models stored locally before your arrival. You may not query any modern source.”

Isca sat. “What horizon?”

“Choose one that allows confirmation today.”

“Confirmation through which sensor geometry?”

Talia displayed six unlabeled vectors. “You may select four. Their identities remain sealed until commitment.”

For the first time since entering, Isca hesitated. A meteorologist did not predict an abstract star. She predicted measurements from instruments with locations, errors, and histories. Talia had removed the professional ground beneath her and called the fall independent proof.

“This biases failure,” Isca said.

“Yes.”

“Your sensors may not match ours.”

“Yes.”

“Then a wrong answer proves nothing.”

“Correct.”

“And a right answer?”

“Depends how right.”

Isca looked at the observers. “Does your office test every claim by making accuracy harder?”

“Only claims people plan to erase if the test fails.”

The anger left Isca’s face without becoming forgiveness. She chose four vectors.

Talia locked the selection. Maelin revealed the first shared baseline: current stellar rotation from AY 58, magnetic sector boundaries from AY 60, and a common archive of long-period coronal behavior. No observation after the great storm appeared. Isca read in silence.

Her fingers began moving over the model. She adjusted for sensor angles she could not know, entered ranges instead of points, and rejected the default plasma-density assumption. Professional habits changed her posture. The room and its custody disappeared from her attention until something resisted her calculation.

“Your model treats the northern heliographic band as quieter than ours does,” Isca said.

“Is that a question?” Talia asked.

“It means either your star behaved differently after divergence or your later instruments developed a bias.”

“You cannot use later current records.”

“I do not need them. The default coefficients reveal what your model builders observed.”

Talia had missed that channel. A standard model could smuggle modern weather into a supposedly clean test through its tuned constants.

She suspended the session.

One Ministry observer objected at once. “The subject has already seen the baseline.”

“Isca is not a subject. The baseline carried post-divergence inference.”

“You selected it.”

“Then the failure belongs to my method.”

Talia deleted the model package and opened the equations without coefficients. Isca would have to build every assumption from the common record. The change cost an hour and removed one argument Jun could use later.

During the reset, Sal brought food into the room under seal. Isca ignored hers. Maelin ate both portions of fruit and denied doing so when challenged.

Talia sat beside Isca without opening the channel. Through the partition, the red star filled one instrument window. Seven black array petals divided its disk.

“How often did you forecast this?” Talia asked.

“Solar weather? Every day for twelve years.”

“From Kest?”

“From the northern atmospheric office. We handled orbital hazard and ground-grid exposure together. Open cities cannot pretend the sky ends at a roof.”

Current Kest’s buried towns had learned the opposite habit. Their weather offices treated rock cover as a permanent coefficient.

“Did you lose people in solar events?”

“Of course. We lost pilots, repair crews, two school expeditions that ignored a particle warning. We did not lose the north in AY 61.” Isca looked toward the dark console. “Your test wants that difference to produce a prediction. It produced my whole profession.”

Talia wrote the sentence in her private notes, then deleted it. It carried emotional force but no evidentiary value. She disliked herself for making that distinction while Isca watched.

“What would satisfy you?” Isca asked.

“A result neither side could prepare after contact.”

“You think Nemi sent me a forecast during the civic relay.”

“I think any hostile review will claim she did.”

“She sent a census and asked whether I had eaten.”

“Hostile review will not care.”

Isca turned the sealed food packet in her hands. “Then choose an event too new for Nemi to know when we spoke.”

“The field delay varies.”

“So measure an eruption beginning after you closed the channel. I can use its early shape once your oldest raw sensor reaches home. Kest-Blue predicts the next stage. Your later readings confirm it here.”

Talia followed the proposed chain. Pelagos would transmit only a blind early vector through the exclusion field. Kest-Blue meteorologists would model the event using their independent solar history. Their prediction would return before Pelagos’s local sensors observed the later stage, if geometry and field delay cooperated. Neither branch could prepare the answer from a completed event.

Maelin listened from the board. “That requires sending a technical packet.”

“Not civic contact,” the Ministry observer said.

“Physics has always enjoyed exemptions people do not,” Isca replied.

Talia revised the protocol. All parties signed before they knew whether a usable event existed.

The first search found nothing. Pelagos’s red star mattered only to the field machinery; the weather under test came from Kest’s sun, carried through branch-linked exclusion. The array caught ordinary wind, weak magnetic noise, and one particle rise too diffuse for timing.

Three hours passed. Isca rebuilt common-history coefficients by hand. Talia audited the equations line by line. She found two places where current notation concealed later assumptions. Isca replaced them with wider uncertainty bands.

At local hour nineteen, raw sensor four registered a hard ultraviolet rise from the Kest-Blue geometry.

Maelin froze the first twelve seconds. Talia verified that the signal had entered after civic closure. Sal drew a random challenge key and encrypted the later portion so no one in the room could view it. Only the early slope, polarization, and source latitude crossed the field.

“Packet away,” Maelin said. “Assuming away has meaning here.”

The exclusion trace accepted it during a brief phase rise. A timer appeared on each custody recorder.

Before anyone waited for Kest-Blue, Talia required a present-world control. Pelagos had one solar analyst on staff, Orin Dase, assigned to protect array electronics from stellar interference. He had observed the early ultraviolet rise through a maintenance warning but had not seen the sealed later feed. Talia brought him into the room under separate custody and gave him the same twelve seconds.

Orin read the vector, then looked at Isca. “Does she remain here?”

“She has already committed no forecast,” Talia said. “You are not competing with her.”

“I am competing with a result everyone wants.”

“That concern belongs in your declaration.”

He recorded it. Then he requested current solar maps.

“Denied,” Talia said. “The branch cannot access them.”

“Then this does not resemble operational forecasting.”

“The branch also lacks our instrument calibration. Both conditions impair the comparison.”

“Impairment does not make bias equal.”

Isca spoke from the partition. “He is right. Give him his maps.”

Talia turned. “That advantages current Kest.”

“Of course it does. Your question asks whether our independent history contributes information your better-equipped forecaster lacks. Do not blind him for symmetry and call the result honest.”

Orin studied her through the glass. Professional attention replaced some of his suspicion. “What information do your forecasters have?”

“Full Kest-Blue solar maps through the packet departure. Their instruments differ from yours. Their weather record diverged after AY 61. They know their own star.”

Talia revised the control rule. Orin received every current feed except the sealed later interval. He built a forecast using the latest present-Kest source map. The map showed a complex magnetic region near the challenged latitude, but current models expected its strongest structure to open northward.

He committed a single eruption with a broad particle front. Arrival uncertainty covered twenty-six minutes. Polarization remained unremarkable.

“Confidence?” Talia asked.

“Sixty-four percent for eruption. Thirty-one for Earth-facing particle release.”

“Kest-facing,” Isca said.

Orin glanced at her. “Old habit.”

“From a planet neither of us lives on.”

“The equations traveled better than the people.”

Talia sealed his forecast. Next she asked the two Ministry observers to choose an automated Concord model. They disagreed over which package qualified. One preferred the current hazard service, which absorbed real-time reports from three systems. The other argued that its distributed inputs could contain Pelagos leakage.

Their dispute improved the design. Talia admitted both models under separate labels. The current service predicted a weak front. A clean historical package predicted no significant event. Neither received access to the Kest-Blue commitment.

Maelin watched custody seals multiply across the board. “At this rate the paperwork will become massive enough to stabilize the field.”

“Every control closes an excuse,” Talia said.

“No. It identifies which excuses remain available.”

That distinction mattered. Talia added it to the limitations note, though Maelin complained that sarcasm lost structural integrity when quoted.

Orin stayed in the room. He asked to inspect the Kest-Blue method only after both predictions opened. Isca agreed on one condition: Pelagos would send his present-world forecast back with the final results, including his objections. She would not let her colleagues receive a victory stripped of the strongest competing work.

“You expect them to publish our control?” he asked.

“I expect them to argue with it.”

“What if the branch forecast fails?”

“Then they argue about why.”

Orin signed that condition. Talia recognized a new custody problem at once. Scientific exchange had begun to acquire reciprocal obligations no Ministry order had authorized. She could either amputate those obligations and weaken the test, or preserve them and document the disobedience.

She preserved them.

They waited.

Talia used the delay to prepare failure language. If no answer came, the test remained inconclusive. If Kest-Blue predicted only a generic flare, coincidence would dominate. If the return arrived after local observation, contamination could not be excluded. She wrote each outcome before any result tempted her to soften it.

Isca stood at the partition. “You make disappointment look organized.”

“I make it reviewable.”

“Does that help?”

“Sometimes after the damage.”

A return carrier touched the array twenty-seven minutes later. It contained a Kest-Blue weather office seal, six forecaster keys, and a model commitment. Isca’s name did not appear among them.

Talia opened only the forecast statement. It predicted a confined eruption followed by a failed magnetic lift, then a second release from the southern edge of the source region. The second release should produce two particle fronts. Kest-Blue expected the faster front to carry a reversed polarization uncommon in current models. Arrival windows spanned nine minutes.

Maelin read over her shoulder. “Specific enough to die beautifully.”

“Or stand,” Isca said.

The observers requested immediate access to local sensors. Talia denied it until the predicted window closed. No one could adjust the commitment after seeing partial confirmation.

Minutes acquired weight through procedure. Each recorder advanced on its separate clock. The sealed local feed accumulated behind an unread counter. At the first predicted window, nothing visible changed in the room. At the second, an array alarm tried to announce particle hazard. Maelin silenced it without opening the measurement.

Isca watched Talia rather than the board. “If it matches, will you believe me?”

“Belief has too many exits.”

“Will you state what the evidence supports?”

“Yes.”

“Even if Jun orders otherwise?”

Talia thought of the old warning carrying her signature, still unopened in Jun’s classified file. Eleven years ago, she had accepted a closed review because panic could kill. Every careful omission after that choice had acquired descendants.

“Yes,” she said again.

The final window ended.

Sal entered three keys. Both Ministry observers added theirs. Maelin unlocked the sensor bank without touching its contents. The weather room filled with raw plots.

A confined eruption rose from the exact latitude Kest-Blue had identified. Its magnetic lift failed after eighty-one seconds. Plasma collapsed, struck the southern source edge, and released a second front. The first arriving particles carried reversed polarization.

No one celebrated.

Talia compared predicted windows against recorded arrivals. Four central estimates fell within uncertainty. One missed by twelve seconds but remained inside the declared band. The density range matched within six percent. Current-Kest models had predicted a single broad front arriving twenty-two minutes later.

“Run instrument-fault challenges,” she said.

Maelin split the readings by array petal. The signal appeared with expected geometric delays across five independent sensors. A sixth had saturated and contributed nothing. Talia removed it from analysis.

“Check whether the return packet could have read our later feed through field reflection.”

They compared packet closure time against each local clock. The Kest-Blue commitment arrived thirteen minutes before Pelagos received the first predicted particle front. Even the largest clock uncertainty left more than eleven minutes of separation.

One observer tried a different objection. “Isca may have known this region’s behavior from before her arrival.”

“She did not sign the forecast,” Talia said.

“She could have briefed the forecasters through earlier contact.”

Isca answered. “We did not know which region your random key would select.”

Talia added the objection anyway. Then she requested a second test.

Kest-Blue sent no answer for forty minutes. Field coherence dropped twice. When another packet arrived, it carried an objection from its weather office: Pelagos had provided too little calibration to distinguish instrument bias from actual polarization. They proposed three blind corrections, each committed before receiving more data.

A fabricated polity designed to please its discoverers might have claimed easy certainty. Kest-Blue’s forecasters had rejected part of the proof as badly measured.

Talia approved one correction by random selection. The resulting forecast concerned a smaller particle event and gave a broad window. Pelagos later confirmed it near the lower bound. Less dramatic, more useful. Independent history did not need perfection. It needed errors that belonged to a real method.

She assembled the finding while every witness remained in the room. Observable effect: post-AY 61 solar behavior unavailable in current archives. Procedure: blind randomized packet exchange under sealed timing. Measured contradiction: Kest-Blue forecasts outperformed present models and arrived before local confirmation. Interpretation: the source possessed independent observations, institutions, and causal continuity after branch divergence.

Talia signed.

The first Ministry observer refused. The second signed as witness to procedure without accepting interpretation. Sal signed the custody chain. Maelin pressed a grease-marked thumb to her key and left a dark crescent on the glass.

Isca read the finding twice. “You wrote independent polity.”

“The evidence supports it.”

“Not reconstruction. Not residual.”

“No.”

The wall chimed before Talia could transmit the certified packet. Continuity House had entered the Pelagos command layer.

Jun appeared only as text. Her order cited uncontained causal contamination, network panic, and unauthorized branch communication. It directed Sal to secure Isca and Maelin. A separate technical instruction carried Talia’s own audit classification.

DISCONNECT ALL RAW ARRAYS FROM LOCAL REVIEW. PRESERVE SUMMARIES UNDER CONTINUITY BLACK.

Talia looked through the partition at the live plots. The reversed particle front still crossed them in blue lines. Summary processing had already begun at the edge of the display, rounding intervals and combining sensor errors until the distinctive shape softened.

“Sal,” she said, “has your authority authenticated?”

He checked the command seal. “Yes.”

“Then witness my objection before you act.”

Talia copied the finding into three open recorders. Sal gave his officers the disconnection order. One by one, the raw arrays went dark, leaving Kest-Blue’s correct weather sealed behind Jun’s command.

Chapter 10 — The Other Kest’s Sky

Isca aligned the stolen camera by watching her own face arrive forty-one seconds late.

The image occupied a maintenance pane no wider than her hand. In the live side of the pane, she sat in a Pelagos equipment locker under flat blue light. In the delayed side, her earlier self leaned toward the lens, frowned, and reached for a focus control she had already released. Condensation blurred both versions at the edges.

“Again,” Maelin said through the wall speaker.

Isca turned the camera three degrees left. The device belonged to a hull-inspection crawler and still carried scratches from some old passage along the station exterior. Maelin had wired its optical feed into a diagnostic carrier that Jun’s disconnection order left untouched. The order named raw arrays. It did not name maintenance images reflected through the exclusion field.

Talia would have called that an omission with consequences. Maelin called it an available socket.

“Your face remains disappointingly singular,” the physicist said.

“Can the channel reach home?”

“It has already reached something. The return keeps failing color synchronization.”

“Sound?”

“Sound costs less than a face. We could speak cleanly if you abandoned the picture.”

Isca looked at her delayed reflection. Six days had changed it in small institutional ways. Her hair had been cut with clinic shears to clear a sensor patch. Pelagos clothes sat wrong at the shoulders. A custody band circled her wrist, gray under one light and blue under the other. None of those changes mattered as much as seeing who noticed them.

“Keep the picture,” she said.

Maelin sighed through the speaker. “Sentiment defeats bandwidth. A founding law of communication.”

Beyond the locked equipment room, security boots crossed the corridor. Sal Varo had placed Isca under protective station custody after Jun’s order. He had not called it arrest. He had assigned two officers, restricted her movement, and left her access to legal counsel that could not arrive before the next gate alignment. When she asked whether a narrowband family call violated custody, he said any contact required technical authorization.

Maelin had supplied technical authorization from herself.

The maintenance pane flashed green, then broke into blocks. Another room appeared inside them.

At first Isca recognized nothing. White ceiling strips crossed a broad public hall. Rain rattled on glass somewhere beyond the camera. People moved behind tables covered in civic wafers and paper lists. Several wore pale filter scarves around their necks, an old southern habit that had spread north during the plague years.

Then Nemi leaned into view.

Her face assembled from the outside inward. Hair first, tied high and badly. One cheek. The line beside her mouth. Her eyes arrived last and looked past the camera before finding it.

Isca forgot every prepared sentence.

Nemi touched two fingers to the screen at her end. Forty-one seconds later, Isca raised her own.

“You cut your hair,” Nemi said.

“They needed the sensor clear.”

“I dislike them.”

“That helps.”

“Are you in a cupboard?”

“Equipment locker.”

“Better.”

The delay put holes between their lines. Each waited for a reply already in transit, then guessed wrong and began speaking. Twice they stopped together. On the third collision, Nemi laughed. Isca heard it after the smile had vanished from the image.

Home had never required patience at this scale. Their worst delays came from mountain relays during storm season, seconds at most. Forty-one seconds gave every question time to acquire fear before an answer arrived.

“Where are you?” Isca asked.

“North Quarter School Five. The neighborhood council moved the civic relay here after the petition. Our regular chamber cannot handle the people who want access.”

“This doesn’t look like School Five.”

“We rebuilt the east hall after the flood.”

“What flood?”

Nemi stared at her. “Last spring.”

Isca knew last spring. She remembered rain failures in the north, smoke advisories, and a public argument over reservoir releases. No flood had reached School Five.

Nemi understood before Isca could ask. “Your last spring does not match ours anymore.”

“My last spring?”

“The people holding you have current-Kest records. You have started using their weather as if you lived through it.”

“I lived through ours.”

“Then remember the east drainage collapse. You complained for two weeks because council meetings ran in our kitchen.”

The memory appeared only after Nemi named it: wet shoes under the yellow table, maps spread beside Aru’s breakfast, neighbors arguing over pump access while rain struck the wall. Isca had not lost it. Current-Kest’s northern history had pressed against her own until the smaller domestic event slipped behind the dramatic burn scar.

“I remember,” she said.

Nemi’s face softened late. “Good.”

Someone ran past the camera carrying a roll of blue cable. Behind Nemi, the rebuilt hall displayed a mosaic made from storm-broken window glass. School Five had never owned a mosaic before Isca left. She wanted to ask who designed it, whether the east classrooms still smelled of damp insulation, if the old music room survived. Questions multiplied faster than the channel could carry them.

“Aru?” she asked instead.

“At home with Sena. He demanded to come. The council voted against children in the relay hall until we understand exposure.”

“He knows you can see me?”

“He knows I am trying.”

Nemi looked away. A woman in a green filter scarf placed a paper stack beside her. Isca recognized Councilor Davi, who had once spent four hours in their kitchen opposing a new bus shelter because its roof shed water toward a neighbor’s wall. Davi noticed the image and came close.

“Are you receiving full motion?” the councilor asked.

“Fragments,” Isca said.

“Can Pelagos record us?”

“Yes.”

“Can we record them?”

Maelin answered through Isca’s side. “You can preserve whatever reaches your receiver. Pelagos security may preserve this channel as evidence of my misconduct.”

Davi waited through the delay, then nodded as if misconduct had supplied a useful category. “The council authorized family contact for ten minutes. After that we need Isca’s account of custody conditions.”

Nemi’s expression sharpened. “She gets more than ten minutes.”

“She gets as long as the field holds. The council gets its ten before technical collapse.”

“You opposed bringing Aru because this counted as civic work. Now you want civic work to take his time.”

“I opposed placing a seven-year-old beneath an uncharacterized exclusion carrier.”

Their argument followed old grooves. Isca almost smiled. A fabricated world built from her needs would not have included Davi, bus-shelter drainage, or the particular way Nemi’s left hand opened when she prepared to overrule a committee.

“I can give the custody account now,” Isca said. “Keep the family channel afterward.”

Nemi looked toward the delayed image. “Do not bargain away your own minutes.”

“They belong to both.”

Councilor Davi opened the paper stack. Kest-Blue had begun printing critical records because digital signatures sometimes collapsed in the field. She asked Isca to state location, physical condition, restraints, access to counsel, medical treatment, and freedom to decline tests.

Isca answered each item. Pelagos had not injured her. Talia had refused sedation at North Anchor. Sal restricted her movement but had not forced scientific participation. Jun Orra’s authority remained sealed beyond meaningful appeal. The distinction mattered. Accurate testimony could not turn every person on this side into one jailer.

“Have they recognized your Kest citizenship?” Davi asked.

“Talia’s finding calls us an independent polity. No Concord authority has accepted it.”

“Do they claim you as a current-Kest citizen?”

“No.”

“Stateless?”

“Anomalous custody.”

Davi’s disgust survived compression. “They made grammar into a cell.”

Nemi wrote the line on a paper margin. Isca knew she would use it at the next meeting.

“What actions do you request?” Davi asked.

“Preserve contact. Demand public review of the asylum petition. Refuse any agreement that treats me as the only verified person.”

“Evacuation priority?”

“We do not have a safe evacuation method.”

“That answer differs from no.”

Isca felt Nemi watching. “If a method appears, use public criteria. Children, medical risk, field-collapse zones. No preference for my household.”

Davi marked the answer. Nemi said nothing until the councilor moved away.

“You rehearsed that,” she said.

“I signed it into the petition.”

“You signed our son out of a first place.”

The words reached Pelagos without anger. Their plainness cut deeper.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Isca stared at her.

Nemi’s image broke across the mouth, then reassembled. “Do not look relieved. I hate the answer. I would carry Aru through that field myself if anyone could show me where to step. But you do not get to turn him into the reason nine million people should trust your captors.”

“I am not asking them to trust anyone.”

“You asked a civic court for asylum.”

“I asked it to recognize an application.”

“The court belongs to a network built from us.”

“What alternative do you have?”

“None yet. That does not improve the court.”

A burst of interference replaced Nemi’s face with the school hall. Through the noise, Isca saw a memorial strip running above the rebuilt windows. Thousands of small names had been printed on dark glass.

“What is behind you?” she asked when sound returned.

Nemi followed her gaze. “Plague names.”

“Why did they move the district memorial?”

“They didn’t. School Five became a southern intake center during the third wave. Those names belong to people who died here.”

Isca knew the southern plague through briefings and weather restrictions, but the north had experienced it mostly as maps. The disease had begun in warm coastal water after a season of unusual bloom. It attacked lung tissue, spread through crowded cooling shelters, and resisted the first antifungal treatments. Kest-Blue lost cities current Kest had never built.

“How many at the school?” Isca asked.

“Three thousand, eight hundred and twelve recorded. More arrived without papers.”

“Our neighborhood never had that many beds.”

“We had floors.”

Nemi turned the camera toward the hall. Long white seams marked places where temporary partitions had once divided families. Ceiling ports carried fittings for emergency oxygen. A faded red line on the floor separated clean water from waste during quarantine. The mosaic used blue, green, and black glass from shattered isolation rooms.

Current-Kest broadcasts had begun calling the Other Kest abundant because its northern forests survived. They showed open cities, rainfall, green land where the present world carried a burn scar. None had shown School Five’s memorial.

“We did not avoid your disaster and keep everything,” Nemi said. “We had another one.”

“How long were you here?”

“Five months. Aru stayed with my sister outside the district. You forecast ventilation windows for the cooling shelters.”

Isca remembered those months as data first: humidity limits, wind reversals, salt aerosol moving inland. Then bodies entered the memory. Nemi asleep against a supply crate. Councilor Davi burning contaminated paper in a courtyard. Aru asking through a screen why both mothers could not come home.

“I told myself you were safer than the south,” Isca said.

“We were. Safety still had a count.”

The image held on the memorial names. Several ended at the edge of a broken pane. Repairs had preserved the fracture instead of replacing the whole strip.

Maelin warned that field coherence had fallen by half. “Five minutes if no one breathes ambitiously.”

Nemi turned the camera back. “Then we need policy.”

“I need to see Aru.”

“You will. First answer what Pelagos proposed.”

“No one has proposed rescue. They have containment.”

“Your messages mentioned prepared mass.”

“Maelin says branch matter needs equal exclusion margin. That means no simple transfer and no free duplication.”

“Could one person cross?”

“Maybe. Her models remain unstable.”

“Could three?”

Isca heard the trap. “Maybe.”

“Then say it before you decide.”

“A small transfer might become possible.”

Nemi leaned nearer. “You will not use it on us.”

“If the field starts failing and I can bring you through…”

“No.”

“Aru is seven.”

“So are thousands of children.”

“He is ours.”

“To us. Not to the neighborhood council, not to the southern districts, not to every family watching whether your contact turns public machinery into a private door.”

Isca stood so quickly the camera lost her face. The custody band struck the metal table. “You are not here.”

“Neither are you.”

“I wake under their lights. I do not know whether our house still stands until you show me a wall. Do not ask me to measure Aru against a district list.”

“I am asking you not to make me choose our family over that list.”

Delay left the argument open. Isca walked to the equipment-room door and back while her words crossed the field. Anger found every surface too small. Pelagos had already reduced her world to bandwidth, citizenship to a finding, marriage to alternating bursts. Now Nemi wanted discipline from inside the danger.

When the reply arrived, Nemi had moved closer to the lens.

“Isca, listen. The third plague wave taught us what priority without rules does. Officials moved their families north before they moved oxygen. They called it temporary continuity. By the time the council published names, the private transports had gone.”

“You think I would do that?”

“I think fear can make decent people name themselves exceptions.”

Isca looked again at the memorial strip. Nemi’s work had always turned private fear outward until it became a rule others could survive. Isca had loved that quality in ordinary years. Under Pelagos light, it felt like another locked door.

“What do you want me to promise?” she asked.

“No evacuation plan centered on us. If one place opens, publish the criteria before a name enters it. If you help design the method, include people from plague districts and places the civic relay barely reaches. Do not let them call my consent consent for Kest-Blue.”

“I cannot speak for Kest-Blue.”

“Good. Keep saying it.”

Maelin counted down from three minutes. The picture lost blue, leaving Nemi’s face in green and gray.

A second signal entered the maintenance carrier. Someone at the Ren apartment had found the home relay key. The pane divided. Aru appeared upside down.

Before the picture settled, Isca heard him arguing with Sena about homework. His class had been assigned a household water audit after the east-hall flood. Aru insisted bath steam should not count because it “went back into the air.” Sena insisted the meter lacked a category for philosophy.

The exchange delivered more home than the first sight of his face. Aru still negotiated rules by inventing larger ones. Sena still spoke to children as if they might prevail. Behind them, a kitchen timer chimed with the three-note pattern Kest-Blue adopted during plague quarantine, a sound current Kest used for gate warnings.

Aru thrust a marked worksheet toward the lens. His drawing showed rain collection from the roof feeding window planters and a public cistern. Red corrections covered his arithmetic.

“You added the same water twice,” Isca said.

“It can be in two places.”

“Not at the same time.”

He considered Pelagos, where two histories had occupied one instrument. “Are you sure?”

“On worksheets, yes.”

Nemi’s delayed laugh reached them from the school hall. For a few seconds the family inhabited the same joke despite the field, each response arriving after someone had already begun missing it.

Isca laughed before she could stop herself. “Turn the camera.”

“I did.”

“The other way.”

He turned it twice. Their ceiling, one ear, and the yellow kitchen table passed through view. Finally his face settled sideways. He had cut a front tooth since she left. A dark scab marked his chin.

“What happened?” Isca asked.

“I jumped from the pump rail.”

“You are not allowed on the pump rail.”

“I know now.”

Nemi said something from the school channel. Aru protested that Sena had approved the call. Their two images overlapped when the field delay shifted.

“Are you on the other Kest?” he asked.

“I am at Pelagos.”

“Does it have rain?”

“No.”

“Trees?”

“No.”

“Then why did they build it?”

Maelin answered, “Poor judgment across several generations.”

Aru accepted that more easily than any scientific explanation. He held up a sheet of paper. Two circles filled it, both meant to represent Kest. One carried green across the north. The other had a brown mark. Between them he had drawn a black star with seven petals.

“Which one are you?” he asked.

Isca touched the screen. “Neither right now.”

His mouth tightened. “That is not a place.”

“No. I am trying to leave it.”

She did not promise when. Nemi watched from the adjacent pane and gave one small nod.

Field coherence dropped below ten percent. The school hall dissolved into blocks. Aru carried his camera toward the apartment window despite both mothers telling him to stay still.

“I want to show you,” he said.

The view swung past the kitchen. Isca saw the repaired lamp, three cups beside the sink, and the weather notices Nemi had not taken down. Then the window filled the pane.

Kest-Blue night lay above the open northern city. Cloud-forest towers rose as dark columns beyond the neighborhood roofs. Rain moved through public lights. Both small moons hung above the trees.

Aru pointed at the nearer one. “Sena says your people think it got hit somewhere else.”

Current Kest’s nearer moon carried a bright impact streak along its eastern quarter, formed after debris from the AY 61 storm damaged an orbital defense platform. Isca had seen that streak through North Anchor’s cameras. Here, the moon’s eastern face remained smooth.

A dark crescent cut its southern rim instead.

“What made that?” Isca asked.

“A rock, before I was born. Mama, you taught me this.”

She had. The answer waited inside her memory with the flood, the plague shelter, and every ordinary difference pressure had buried.

Pelagos lost sound first. Aru kept pointing while his image broke into squares. Nemi vanished from the other pane. For one final second the stolen camera held the Kest-Blue sky: cloud towers under rain, two moons above them, and the moon scar in the wrong place.

Then the maintenance channel failed, leaving that other night frozen on Isca’s screen.

Chapter 11 — The Continuity Balance

Jun Orra arrived at Pelagos with the route mortality model open across her lap.

The courier’s restraint held her against a hard acceleration seat while the reserve station grew in the forward display. Seven exclusion petals ringed the red dwarf, black against a disk the color of banked coals. Pelagos Station hung below them in an orbit designed for instruments rather than comfort. Its habitat drums turned at mismatched rates. Service lights marked the receiver spur that Maelin Drave had added without appropriation, permission, or sufficient shame.

A second star field trembled across the display glass. The courier’s software labeled it reflection noise and erased it. Jun disabled the correction.

She had reviewed the model during every transit window from Arclight. Thirty-one hours without gate service killed first in refrigerated wards, then in farm belts dependent on imported enzymes. At three days, water membranes failed in four outer habitats. At nine days, the Sable curve overtook any relief plan the Concord could launch on slow drives. The names of the dead had changed since AY 17. Dependency had not.

The pilot announced docking clearance. Jun closed every branch except the one marked TOTAL NETWORK CLOSURE. She left that result visible: a median projection of 2.8 million deaths in the first month, with another 5.1 million exposed to severe rationing. No model could predict panic without pretending people behaved like valves. She had ordered the uncertainty displayed rather than hidden.

Pelagos took the courier with a jolt. Through the side port, the station acquired two shadows. One followed the docking truss toward the red dwarf. The other leaned six degrees away, cast by a light that did not exist in this system.

Jun counted both before she stood.

Sal Varo waited beyond decontamination in a plain security jacket. He had put two officers at the junction and none inside the reception lock. That choice told her he expected an argument about custody.

“Director,” he said.

“Commander. Current station condition?”

“Stable within the hour. Receiver load keeps moving. Drave calls that expected.”

“Drave has called several prosecutable events expected.”

Sal did not smile. “Talia Sorn requested that Isca Ren retain access to the civic relay during your visit.”

“Denied.”

“On what safety finding?”

Jun met his gaze. “A live connection to nine million frightened people creates an audience for every local dispute. Restrict the relay to scheduled proof exchanges until I finish the review.”

“That limits contact. It does not establish custody.”

“No. Your seal does.”

The distinction satisfied his office, if not the man. He transmitted the restriction while they walked. Pelagos carried cold through its handrails. Temporary cable bundles crossed the corridor overhead, each tagged in Maelin’s square writing. Scientists had turned three storage compartments into analysis rooms. Security had answered with portable locks. The entire station looked like an argument acquiring hardware.

Talia waited in the primary array chamber beside a table covered in print film. She wore her audit collar and a dark shoulder brace under her jacket. Isca stood at the far end with both hands flat on the table. Maelin occupied an open service well, visible from the knees up, fastening a sensor lead where no approved lead belonged.

Jun stopped at the threshold. “Commander, clear the chamber.”

Isca said, “You traveled here to discuss whether I count. I will remain for that part.”

Her directness carried no performance. Jun had watched six hours of interview records and found the same refusal in each. The woman did not ask current officials to solve personhood. She demanded that they stop using uncertainty as permission.

“Talia and I have a cabinet matter first,” Jun said. “You may hear any offer made concerning your legal position.”

“An offer concerning me without me present still concerns my legal position.”

Talia kept her attention on Jun. That restraint hurt more than accusation.

Jun set her case on the table. “Commander, escort Drave to medical inspection. No restraints unless she obstructs you. Isca Ren may wait in the adjacent relay room with visual access and muted audio until called.”

Maelin looked up from the service well. “You still make confinement sound like sensible furniture.”

“You still mistake contempt for a safety procedure.”

Isca lifted one hand from the table. “I’ll wait next door if Nemi’s scheduled packet remains in queue.”

Jun checked the relay notice on her wrist. The packet had already arrived and held authentication marks from Kest-Blue’s civic council. Denying it would change nothing except custody of the grievance.

“It remains,” she said.

Sal led Maelin out. Isca followed without waiting for an officer. At the connecting door, she looked back at Talia, not Jun. The door sealed.

Only then did Jun open the Continuity Balance.

Eleven years of cabinet evidence unfolded above the table. Biological periodicity from Pelagos. Language-like compression in reserve noise. Thermal structures too orderly for stochastic residue. Seven internal findings, each cautious enough to defend in court and clear enough to disturb anyone who read them together. Jun had brought the originals, including dissent notes and routing approvals. She had not come to win through selective disclosure.

Talia read the authorization column first.

“You continued Kest traffic after Pattern Six,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Pattern Six identified adaptive error correction.”

“It identified a signal that behaved as if it corrected errors. The cabinet did not have a sender, environment, or verified persistence.”

“You had weather-scale structure.”

“We had correlations around a reserve star under continuous exclusion load.”

Talia pulled one sheet closer. Her thumb stopped beside an operations order. “You increased traffic.”

Jun had known which line she would find. Knowledge did not reduce its force.

“Three Kest agricultural routes received priority after the AY 137 blight,” she said.

“This order adds discretionary crossings.”

“It added medical and seed reserve. Kest had lost two harvests.”

“The cabinet recommended lower exclusion loading.”

“The cabinet also projected forty thousand present deaths if we reduced supply to the recommended threshold.”

Talia turned the sheet around. “So you sent more structured material into Pelagos because current Kest needed throughput.”

“I sent food, cultures, machine parts, and field teams to current Kest. Exclusion followed every crossing. We had no evidence then that a coherent population received it.”

“Your own analysts used the phrase cultural recurrence.”

“Analysts use strong language when they want another year of instruments.”

Talia’s jaw tightened. “That sounds unworthy of you.”

“It sounds unworthy because it is. Some of those analysts understood the risk before I did. Others interpreted every new pattern as vindication. I chose among findings that did not agree.” Jun touched the routing approval. “I also chose among casualties who did not have the luxury of waiting for agreement.”

The array chamber vibrated as a petal adjusted outside. Across the polished table, Jun’s hand acquired a second outline. Talia saw it and moved the evidence away from both reflections.

“You buried their recommendation,” Talia said.

“I classified it.”

“You removed it from route review.”

“I kept an unverified reserve phenomenon from closing the network by rumor.”

“Eleven years later, Isca woke in a sealed bay.”

“Yes.”

The answer left no shelter. Jun watched Talia register that she would not call the woman residue or dispute her continuity. Precision had become expensive; Jun intended to pay for it.

She expanded the route mortality model between them.

Forty-seven systems appeared as dependency nodes. Green marked local reserves. Amber tracked losses after partial gate suspension. Red began with neonatal medicines on Oris Minor, protein vats on Deme, and coolant catalysts in the crowded habitats of Arclight. Jun advanced the clock one day. Supply corridors narrowed. On day four, an outer-system membrane plant failed for lack of a ceramic feedstock made two crossings away. Its failure contaminated water for seventy thousand people. By day nine, the display resembled an organ deprived of blood.

“Route mortality,” Jun said. “Not trade loss. Not inconvenience. These are the deaths associated with specific closures under conservative assumptions.”

Talia studied the nodes rather than Jun. “You built this before coming.”

“I have maintained versions since Sable.”

“Does it include deaths in Kest-Blue?”

“No reliable closure branch can include them yet. We lack distribution data, medical dependencies, and a stable account of what Pelagos failure does over time.”

“Then it presents one population as countable and the other as uncertainty.”

“It presents measured dependence and marks missing dependence.” Jun opened the empty Kest-Blue layer. “Give me defensible inputs. I will add them.”

“You already have a census.”

“A census gives people names. It does not tell me which relay failure kills whom on day six.”

The chamber door flashed a relay status warning. Isca’s scheduled packet had begun next door. No sound passed through, yet pale light moved over the connecting window as encoded weather bands crossed the field.

Talia pointed to the full-closure branch. “No one here proposed closing every route tonight.”

“Rumor has reduced voluntary gate commitments by fourteen percent in two days. Three captains refused Pelagos-adjacent routes this morning. Kest’s insulin cultures spent nine hours in a dock because a loader feared branch contamination.”

“You released Morrow Glass for that run.”

“I did.”

“Then you know disclosure and supply can coexist.”

“One successful run does not carry a system. Fear compounds. Every official admission changes what gate crews believe they risk by committing a crossing.”

Talia moved the model to day three and isolated a series of failures. “Your assumptions treat all current schedules as fixed.”

“They reflect present infrastructure.”

“They protect present priority, not only present life. Arclight ornament freight keeps two windows here. A private habitat expansion keeps another. Cut those before medicine.”

“Do it.”

The words stopped her.

Jun granted Talia editing custody of a duplicate model. “Reorder every crossing. Cancel leisure mass. Strip the inner systems to survival loads. You will still find dependencies beyond local manufacture. The network has spent a century making distance cheaper than redundancy.”

Talia began moving commitments. Jun let her. A decorative stone shipment vanished. So did three passenger windows, then a corporate habitat spine. Amber receded along two routes. Red remained around Deme, Oris Minor, Kest, and the Arclight water rings. Talia redirected slow-drive stocks. The model accounted for their arrival months after the projected deaths.

At last she rested both palms beside the display. “You should have shown this publicly years ago.”

Jun restored the schedules Talia had removed, then opened the model’s custody history. Each assumption carried an author, an approval date, and a range of dissent. One analyst had put the Oris Minor neonatal failure at twelve hours later than Jun’s public branch. Another had warned that the Deme water estimate relied on membrane stock reports submitted by the same contractor that benefited from emergency freight. Jun had kept both objections attached.

“Challenge them,” she said.

Talia selected Deme. “This catalyst route has an alternate through Lorn.”

“Lorn’s gate can accept the carrier mass. Its sterilization dock cannot certify the feedstock.”

“Move the certification crew.”

“Twenty-two days by slow drive. One crossing if Lorn gives up its fever shipment.”

Talia traced that shipment to a mining cylinder with forty-three thousand residents. Canceling it raised respiratory deaths there and bought Deme water capacity for another day. She tried a smaller load. The cylinder’s clinic could split the shipment, but its gate window needed a thermal buffer imported from Kest. Kest’s buffer allocation depended on the cultures delayed during the rumor.

“No single red node proves closure impossible,” Talia said.

“No. The links prove that every easy answer moves custody of a death.”

“That sentence can excuse any inherited arrangement.”

“Only if we stop checking it.” Jun opened the route committee’s authority layer. “Move what you choose. Put your name beside each change.”

Talia did. She cut an Arclight luxury water allotment and transferred two treatment modules outward. She delayed a military hull refit, freeing a gate window for membrane stock. She moved Kest seed reserve ahead of three contracted loads and assigned current losses to the firms that had purchased priority. The model improved. It did not clear.

A call entered from Pelagos cargo control while they worked. Eight refrigerated containers waited on the station’s outer truss. Rumor had convinced a Kest dock team to demand branch screening before unloading them. Their power cells held nine hours.

Talia answered before Jun could. “What cargo?”

“Insulin cultures, pulmonary seed, two tissue lots. Captain Tal’s declaration is clean.”

“Receiver exposure?”

“None above station background.”

“Then unload under ordinary biological seal. Record the crew objection and preserve their right to refuse direct handling. Use remotes.”

Cargo control hesitated. “Director authority?”

Jun leaned toward the channel. “Auditor Sorn’s order stands. Charge remote delay to Continuity emergency funds.”

The containers began moving on the station diagram. No principle had become cleaner. Nine hours of cold capacity had changed custody.

Talia watched the first container cross into the quarantine dock. “You could govern like this in public.”

“Sometimes. Public procedure moves slower than coolant failure.”

“Then publish the emergency decision afterward.”

“That part I should have done.”

Jun attached the cargo order to the incident record without classification. Talia noticed. She said nothing, but her shoulders lowered by a fraction, as if an old brace had shifted against a bruise.

“You should have shown this publicly years ago,” she said again, quieter now.

“And shown what beside it? A classified signal we could neither authenticate nor help?”

“The uncertainty.”

“Public uncertainty does not remain in the units you publish. It becomes a slogan, a market run, a gatekeeper refusing a commit while a ward waits for cultures.”

“That possibility does not grant you permanent custody of everyone’s moral judgment.”

Jun looked through the connecting window. Isca had received her packet. She sat before the relay glass with one hand covering her mouth, reading whatever Nemi had sent. Private grief moved under a security camera because Jun had restricted the channel.

“No,” Jun said. “It granted me custody of timing. I kept it too long.”

Talia drew back slightly.

Jun closed the model. She opened the oldest warning instead, a report signed by a junior auditor eleven years earlier. Talia’s name lay beneath the recommendation to contain Pelagos findings until route effects could be modeled. Jun did not turn it toward her as a weapon. She placed it alongside the traffic orders.

“You understood the first duty,” Jun said.

“I understood a temporary hold.”

“So did I.”

“Temporary measures require an end condition.”

“We wrote one. Verified agency in the signal, or a safe alternative to current routing.”

“You controlled the verification and failed to fund the alternative.”

That charge landed cleanly. Jun had no model to interpose.

“I funded observation,” she said.

“You funded watching.”

“Yes.”

Outside, one exclusion petal turned. The station’s two shadow systems slid across the chamber floor, separating around Jun’s shoes.

Talia closed her old report. “What happens now?”

“We preserve present traffic while Pelagos conducts bounded contact. We prohibit unsupervised receiver changes. We prepare a public statement that confirms an anomalous human arrival without asserting branch citizenship. The cabinet begins emergency redundancy planning.”

“Kest-Blue has already petitioned for asylum.”

“A petition does not create capacity.”

“It creates a legal duty to answer.”

“It creates several duties that conflict.” Jun summoned the draft order. “You will resume control of the evidentiary review under my authority. Your raw arrays remain sealed from public relay. Maelin’s receiver enters Ministry custody.”

“And Isca?”

Jun opened the adjacent door.

The relay room smelled of warm circuitry and the sharp mineral coolant Pelagos used in temporary loops. Isca stood when they entered. A still image of Nemi and Aru remained on the glass. Nemi wore a yellow work shirt. Aru held up a page covered with two differently scarred moons.

Jun did not look away from the family. “Isca Ren, I can grant you protected status under Concord emergency law. You would receive independent counsel, private housing at Arclight, unrestricted medical care, and communication with Kest-Blue under security review. No laboratory custody. No compelled testimony.”

Isca’s face did not change. “Price?”

“You refrain from public statements concerning exclusion until the Council establishes a classification. You submit technical communications for review. Your family channel remains private.”

“You would protect me from the category you assigned me.”

“I would protect you from detention, seizure by local authorities, and anyone who decides your body proves a claim they want to own.”

Talia said nothing. Jun needed her silence now because any rescue would make the offer dishonest.

Isca touched the weather card beside the relay. “Does protected status apply to Nemi?”

“No. She is not within Concord jurisdiction.”

“Aru?”

“No.”

“The other 9,412,804 citizens?”

“The order concerns the person physically present.”

“Then it concerns the wrong thing.” Isca lifted the stylus. “May I amend it?”

Jun passed her the document.

Isca wrote beneath the offer in a compact hand. She did not compose a declaration. She added a custody condition: ACCEPTANCE REQUIRES RECOGNITION THAT COMMUNICATION WITH KEST-BLUE CONSTITUTES CONTACT WITH PERSONS CAPABLE OF LEGAL CLAIM. Then she signed only as witness and returned the stylus.

“I refuse,” she said. “Send that refusal to your Council with Nemi’s authenticated census packet.”

Jun read the amendment twice. Accepting it would change the legal status of every relay exchange. Rejecting it left Isca exposed under emergency anomaly law. Isca had used the only custody available to her: custody of consent.

“Commander Varo,” Jun said.

Sal stepped in from the corridor.

“Keep Isca Ren under station protection. No sedation, transfer, or interview without her counsel or an immediate safety need. Preserve her amendment in the incident record.”

Talia’s expression shifted, not toward forgiveness. Toward recalculation.

Jun placed her seal beneath Isca’s refusal. The document locked. On the relay glass behind them, Aru’s drawing flickered as the field weakened, but both moons remained visible. One bore the scar Jun knew. The other carried a white line across unfamiliar blue.

The protection order had failed to purchase silence. It now preserved the refusal.

Chapter 12 — The Present Majority

The first occupiers came aboard Morrow Glass carrying lunch tins.

Rhen watched them cross the cargo lock in family groups while Pelagos security tried to decide whether a line of frightened civilians counted as a boarding action. Twenty-three people wore white strips tied around one sleeve. Four had children with them. None carried a visible weapon. Their leader, Eren Dax, held an insulated case against his chest as though it contained medicine.

It contained three thousand printed signatures and enough adhesive placards to cover a cargo bay.

“We paid passage,” Eren said when Rhen met him beyond the lock. “Your clerk issued receipts.”

“My clerk sold transfer seats to Kest. Pelagos has no civic arrivals.”

“The station has a public petition desk.”

“Inside a restricted reserve installation.”

Eren looked younger than his relay appearances. Twenty-eight, according to the passenger declaration. Lack of sleep had reddened his eyes. A healing pressure cut crossed one thumb. His coat carried the mineral dust of Kest’s buried northern towns.

“Captain Tal,” he said, “my sister’s clinic has four days of insulin cultures. My father maintains a town heat loop with parts shipped from Deme. If Continuity shuts the routes, neither of them can eat your distinction between public and restricted.”

Rhen glanced at the families behind him. A child leaned against a container brace, chewing a ration strip. Two older women had brought folding stools. These people had planned to wait, not fight.

“Security will remove you,” Rhen said.

“Then they can remove us from the petition desk.”

The argument had purchased him seven meters of deck.

Rhen called Sal Varo before anyone moved farther. Sal appeared on the cargo display already wearing a pressure collar, which meant station oscillation had worsened overnight.

“Keep them aboard,” Sal said.

“They hold valid passenger receipts.”

“They hold a coordinated occupation notice. Another hundred and forty are in the transfer hub. They entered on maintenance ferries and two contracted tenders.”

“Your station sold them docking access.”

“My station expected food technicians.”

Eren raised his voice toward the pickup. “Many of us are food technicians.”

Sal’s expression did not shift. “Captain, secure your engineering access and keep your cold trusses powered. I will send officers for the passengers.”

Rhen looked past Eren toward the lock. More white armbands waited inside the transfer tube. If officers pressed inward, the families had nowhere to give ground except among refrigerated medical containers.

“No officers in my bay,” Rhen said. “I’ll move them into the station without a crush.”

“You have ten minutes.”

“Give me twenty.”

“Twelve.”

The channel closed.

Rhen opened the personnel hatch beside the cargo lock and led the occupiers through Morrow Glass. The route passed warm machinery, patched insulation, and hand-lettered cautions accumulated across three decades. Eren kept watching the ship rather than the path. He noticed the emergency masks. He counted pressure doors. That attention could have belonged to a safety-minded passenger. It could also have belonged to someone choosing where to cause trouble.

“You came prepared,” Rhen said.

“We came expecting detention.”

“That case won’t stop a baton.”

“The signatures might stop the person holding it.”

Rhen doubted that and respected the attempt.

At the station collar, Pelagos gave them two shadows again. Their feet cast one set beneath the work lamps. A dimmer set fell toward the wall. The child with the ration strip jumped between them until his mother pulled him close.

The transfer hub held the rest of the occupation. White cloth marked railings and access ladders. Placards covered the closed shops: THE PRESENT ALSO COUNTS. NO FALL WITHOUT A VOTE. FEED THE LIVING HERE. Station staff had retreated behind service shutters. Sal’s officers formed a line across the array corridor with helmets clipped to their belts.

Eren climbed onto a baggage platform. His people turned toward him before he spoke.

“We submit our petition,” he said. “We block no medical cargo. We damage no station equipment. We stay until the Council guarantees that no route closure will be ordered without a public mortality accounting.”

It sounded practiced because it had to reach both the frightened and the armed.

A woman near the officers called, “What about the nine million at Pelagos?”

Eren gripped the petition case. “Count them if they are people. Count us too.”

No cheer followed. Several occupiers looked down. Rhen heard a better truth in that failure than in the placards. They had not come because they agreed on Kest-Blue. They had come because uncertainty had entered their kitchens.

Sal crossed the security line alone. “Eren Dax, you may deliver the petition. After delivery, this assembly must leave restricted compartments and return to the ferries.”

“Our ferries lost departure clearance.”

“You occupied their docking control.”

“We occupied a desk.”

“You placed two people inside the control room.”

Eren looked toward a cluster by the east hatch. The delay before his answer lasted half a second too long.

“They were told to secure our return access,” he said.

Sal touched his wrist channel. “East control, report.”

Static answered.

A station alarm cut across the hub. One hard tone. A pause. Three descending pulses.

Rhen knew the pattern from carrier refrigeration: primary coolant pressure loss.

The floor shifted beneath them.

Cold vapor struck through the east corridor grille. It arrived low and fast, a white sheet curling around ankles. Occupiers stumbled away. Security helmets came on. The station display changed from public notices to a compartment diagram, but three sections stayed gray because their local controllers had stopped reporting.

Sal shouted, “Seal east corridor.”

The pressure door began to close. It stopped thirty centimeters above the floor when a baggage cart jammed under its edge. Coolant fog continued through the gap.

Rhen pulled the nearest child onto the baggage platform and kicked the cart free. The door dropped. Its lower seal struck once, rebounded against a warped guide, then shut on the second attempt.

“Which loop?” Rhen asked.

Sal checked his display. “Receiver thermal loop. Rupture at junction E-four.”

“That loop shares return pressure with the array labs.”

“You know this station?”

“I delivered the replacement manifold you postponed installing.”

Another alarm joined the first. Oxygen displacement in east analysis. Then fire sensors reported a temperature drop so fast their software classified it as instrument failure.

A scientist’s voice broke through the emergency channel. “Array Lab Two. Six personnel. West hatch has no pressure. East hatch shows toxic coolant. We have two Kest-Blue relay researchers in the field room and cannot reach them.”

Rhen found the lab on the diagram. The rupture had emptied coolant into the east passage. Automated isolation closed the lab’s west hatch because the shared frame twisted when the cold pipe contracted. The field room behind it had no independent exit. Every failure followed the one before it with ugly clarity.

“Shut receiver pumps,” Rhen said.

Maelin answered from somewhere beyond the sealed corridor. “Already commanded. Local controller rejects remote stop because someone bridged its maintenance interlock.”

Sal looked at Eren.

Eren had gone pale. “The receiver was supposed to lose power.”

The hub seemed to contract around his words.

“What did you do?” Sal asked.

“Nothing to coolant. We put a bypass on the maintenance panel. It should have tripped the receiver breaker.”

“Who is we?”

“Two people in east control.”

Rhen seized the front of Eren’s coat and pulled him off the platform. The petition case hit the deck. “A receiver breaker carries fail-safe power through the thermal controller. You bridged the interlock, so the pumps kept fighting a closed valve.”

Eren’s breath came shallow. “The diagram showed an electrical isolation.”

“Which diagram?”

“Public maintenance filing. AY 139.”

Pelagos had added Maelin’s receiver after that filing. The old diagram showed a spare service branch. The current loop ran high-volume coolant through it. When Eren’s people cut power, a motorized valve had moved toward its safe position. Their bridge held the pumps at working pressure and suppressed the local shutdown. Fluid struck the closing valve until the aging junction split.

The coolant rupture had not emerged from hatred or impossible physics. It had come from obsolete knowledge applied with confidence.

Sal ordered Eren to the floor.

“No,” Rhen said.

Sal’s gaze sharpened.

“He knows what they installed. Arrest him after he tells us where every bridge sits.”

Eren opened his hands. “There are two. East maintenance controller and the receiver feed panel above Lab Two. We used insulated clamps. No explosives.”

“Who installed the second?” Sal asked.

“Lio Sare. Heat-loop engineer from Nacre Town.”

“Can he remove it?”

Eren stared at the sealed door. “He was in east control.”

The station diagram showed no biosigns there. Coolant obscured the sensors.

Rhen released Eren and turned to the hub. “Everyone who came with Dax, listen. This compartment may lose circulation when the emergency fans reverse. Children and anyone with breathing illness go first. Leave placards. Keep bags only if they contain medicine. Follow cargo crew instructions.”

An occupier demanded, “Where are we going?”

“Cargo Bay Six. It has independent air, heat, and two pressure exits.”

Sal said, “That bay lies inside security control.”

“It also fits them. You can guard both doors.”

A warning appeared on the display: coolant vapor approaching the hub return ducts.

Rhen keyed Morrow Glass. “Seli, open Bay Six through the lower cargo collar. Strip the cold racks from row three and rig temporary heat.”

Seli answered over engine noise. “How many?”

“One hundred sixty, plus station staff if circulation fails.”

“We don’t have masks for that count.”

“Pelagos does. Send a trolley.”

Rhen assigned four occupiers who knew crowd work to each passage lane. Sal added officers at intersections. The cargo-bay evacuation began without agreement on who commanded it. Families moved down the west ramp in groups of twenty. Station clerks followed carrying emergency cases. White armbands disappeared under pressure masks.

One man refused to leave without the petition sheets scattered beside the platform. Eren knelt, scooped the top bundle into its case, and shoved it into the man’s hands.

“Go,” he said. “Signatures don’t need all three copies.”

A pressure shudder traveled through the hub. The sealed east door bowed inward, then settled. Coolant had filled the service corridor to the door’s midpoint. Its temperature contracted the frame and pulled the lower hinge out of alignment.

Maelin came back on channel. “Pump one stopped. Pump two still running at forty percent. The bridge above Lab Two holds the control bus high.”

“Can you cut upstream power?” Rhen asked.

“Not without dropping the field room. We have live Kest-Blue contact in there.”

“People or equipment?”

“Two present researchers at the consoles. The branch participants exist across the field and will lose this window. I care more about the two with lungs.”

Rhen appreciated the answer. “Any route from cargo maintenance?”

Sal expanded the station plan. A narrow service chase ran from Bay Four around the back of Lab Two. It carried data cable and a manual coolant return. No walking clearance appeared on the approved diagram.

“Forty centimeters,” Sal said.

“Enough if the person doesn’t like breathing deeply.”

Eren stepped close. “Lio used that chase to reach the feed panel.”

“You sent him through a coolant chase?”

“He said it stayed dry.”

“It did before the pipe broke.”

Eren’s hands shook. He closed them against his coat. “The clamp sits on the upper bus behind a ceramic shield. Pulling it barehanded will arc.”

Rhen looked at the insulated cargo tongs clipped beside the emergency cart. “Then we take better hands.”

Sal blocked the cart. “You are not entering a contaminated chase.”

“My ship’s medical cargo includes coolant burns because farms use thermal loops. I have the suit, the tongs, and a crew that knows how to haul me back.”

“You also have passengers under your care.”

“Seli has Bay Six.” Rhen pointed to Eren. “He comes with me to the chase mouth. He describes the panel. He does not enter.”

Eren nodded too quickly.

Sal assigned Officer Cenn and took the rear position himself. Rhen did not waste time arguing with a commander who had chosen to share the risk.

They reached Bay Four through a stairwell already silvering with frost. Emergency fans pushed air west. Each landing grew colder. Eren spoke into the suit channel, giving directions from memory. His fear made the instructions exact.

“Red cable bundle at the second split. The chase hatch has four fasteners. We left the lower right loose because Lio dropped the nut.”

“You left evidence of entry.”

“We expected to claim responsibility after the receiver went dark.”

“That plan survives. The receiver did go dark.”

Eren flinched, but he kept walking.

Bay Four contained six empty freight cages and a bank of manual valves. Frost covered the chase hatch. A smear through it marked where someone had entered earlier. Lio’s tool sat beneath the lower brace.

Sal scanned the metal. “Coolant concentration beyond the hatch exceeds suit threshold in eleven minutes.”

Rhen clipped a retrieval line to the back of the pressure suit. “Then pull at nine.”

“Eight.”
