They opened the hatch. Vapor rolled out with enough force to rattle the freight cages. Beyond it, cable bundles crossed a cramped triangular space. A coolant pipe ran along the bottom, its insulation split and wet.

Rhen went in shoulders first.

The chase narrowed around the suit pack. Every movement drove an elbow into metal. Frost clouded the faceplate until the heaters cleared a small oval. Behind, Officer Cenn paid out the line. Eren’s voice guided Rhen past the first split.

“Upper branch. The panel sits above the pipe after two body lengths.”

“How did Lio turn around?”

“He didn’t. He backed out.”

“Good. I was worried your plan lacked discomfort.”

A breath that might have been a laugh broke in the channel, then Eren went quiet.

Rhen reached the panel. Its ceramic shield had cracked from thermal shock. Blue light pulsed through the seam. The insulated clamp held two contacts together, forcing the maintenance bus to report normal continuity.

“I see it.”

“Do not pull straight,” Eren said. “Rotate the grip clockwise first. Lio filed one jaw to clear the housing.”

Rhen set the cargo tongs around the clamp. The first rotation met resistance. The suit timer showed five minutes of safe exposure. Coolant pooled under one knee and began drawing heat through the outer layer.

“Clockwise,” Eren repeated. “If it catches, lift the left jaw.”

Rhen lifted. The clamp moved.

An arc struck the ceramic shield and turned the faceplate white. Rhen lost sight of the tongs but kept pressure on them. The clamp came free. Pump two wound down through the pipe with a falling vibration.

Maelin’s voice arrived at once. “Control bus clear. Closing upstream isolation. Lab Two west hatch should release when frame temperature rises.”

“Should?” Rhen asked.

“I can say will if you find comfort in lies.”

Sal ordered the line pulled. Rhen backed through the chase, pushing the removed clamp ahead. Twice the suit pack caught. Each time Eren described which cable brace to avoid as if he could see through the wall.

When Rhen reached Bay Four, Sal and Cenn dragged the suit clear. Eren helped close the hatch, then stepped away with both palms visible.

A new voice entered the channel. Array Lab Two reported its west hatch open by twelve centimeters. Staff had begun prying it wider. All six personnel remained alive. One researcher had a fractured hand. Another showed early coolant exposure. The two field-room staff were moving under their own power.

Rhen sat against a freight cage while Cenn stripped the freezing outer gloves from the suit.

Eren stared at the clamp on the deck. “That was ours.”

“Yes.”

“I wanted the receiver off for one Council cycle. Long enough to stop them treating closure as unthinkable.”

“You disabled a safeguard you didn’t know had changed.”

“I know.”

“You trapped six people.”

“I know their number too.”

Sal moved behind him with restraints.

The station alarm changed tone. Cargo Bay Six requested medical support. Two evacuees had collapsed from cold stress during the transfer, and circulation in the west ramp had begun to fail.

Rhen pushed upright. “We still need hands.”

“I am placing him under arrest,” Sal said.

Eren looked toward the west ramp. “Put an officer beside me. Bind one wrist if you need to. I know the families, and some won’t follow security into the bay.”

Sal considered him for one second. “One wrist. Officer Cenn retains the other.”

The restraint closed around Eren’s left hand. Cenn clipped it to a short control line rather than behind his back.

They came into Cargo Bay Six through its upper lock. Morrow Glass had turned the space into a rough shelter. Refrigerated racks stood powered down along one wall. Cargo blankets covered the deck. Children sat inside empty freight frames while station medics checked breathing. Seli had hung work lamps from the overhead rails and marked two clear lanes with yellow tie cord.

A group near the far pressure door began shouting when they saw Sal. Eren climbed onto a cargo brace with Cenn’s line still attached.

“The rupture came from our action,” he said.

Noise thinned across the bay.

“We meant to cut the receiver. We bridged a safety control using an old plan. The pumps ruptured the loop. People are injured.” His voice failed on the last word. He swallowed and continued. “Do what the cargo crew tells you. Clear the yellow lanes. Anyone with loop training reports to Captain Tal. I will give security every name and every device.”

No thesis followed. A heat technician raised her hand. Two nurses moved toward the yellow lane. The shouting family let a medic pass.

Rhen sent volunteers to carry portable heaters from the ship. Eren remained on the brace, directing parents toward available blankets and translating Pelagos compartment numbers into the ferry groups he had organized. He did not ask anyone to forgive him. When one man spat at his boots, he looked down and gave the next instruction.

An hour later, Lab Two’s survivors entered the cargo bay. Frost crusted their pressure collars. The researcher with the broken hand cradled it against her chest. Eren stepped off the brace as they passed.

Sal said, “Transport is ready.”

Eren looked at the occupied deck, then at the west ramp alarm still pulsing amber. “The last ferry group has three people missing. Let me finish the count.”

“You have admitted sabotage.”

“Yes.” His frightened gaze found Rhen, then returned to Sal. “That does not make the missing people less missing.”

Rhen checked the manifest board. Three names lacked shelter marks. One belonged to Lio Sare. The other two belonged to an elderly couple last seen near east control.

Sal transferred Eren’s restraint line from Cenn to himself. “Five minutes.”

They found the couple in an equipment alcove beyond the west ramp, sharing one emergency mask. Eren knew the woman’s Kest district and got her moving by naming the clinic where her granddaughter worked. Lio remained unaccounted for.

At the cargo-bay threshold, Eren stopped beside the rack of borrowed pressure suits. The station behind him still vented white coolant into isolated pipes. Ahead, the families he had brought occupied a ship captain’s freight lanes under security guard.

“Take me to east control,” he told Sal. “Lio may have used the manual return.”

Sal tightened his hold on the line. “You stay where I can see you.”

Eren nodded. He was still shaking.

Together they turned back toward the cold.

Chapter 13 — A Station with Two Shadows

Talia knew the corridor had changed because the casualty board appeared on the wrong wall.

She stopped before the west junction and checked the station plan on her wrist. Array Spine C should have opened ahead. Its near bulkhead carried a red medical cache and three manual seal wheels. Instead, a pale green corridor extended twenty meters toward a window that Pelagos had never possessed. Rain moved across the glass. Beyond it, cloud forest rose under a blue-white sun.

Her wrist still placed her inside Pelagos.

Isca came up behind her wearing a pressure hood folded at the neck. “That corridor belongs to the Kest-Blue receiver ministry. Nemi showed me its public tour.”

“Does it connect to a habitat?”

“Not physically. It stands in their Pelagos analogue, if their instruments modeled this station around the field.”

The green corridor flickered. Pelagos returned with frost along its deck and a casualty board bolted to the left wall. Two names remained amber. Eren Dax had gone back with Sal to search east control. Lio Sare had not emerged.

A warning tone sounded through both geometries. In Pelagos, it came from ceiling speakers. In the alternate corridor, a chime traveled from the window frames. The frequencies beat against each other until Talia’s teeth hurt.

Maelin spoke through Talia’s earpiece. “The receiver lost thermal stability during the rupture. We are phase-searching across compatible local structures. Do not cross any threshold unless its frame remains continuous for eight seconds.”

“People remain in Spine C.”

“I know. Three biosigns in your station, perhaps more from the branch facility. The array cannot keep the two plans separated.”

Talia watched the junction alternate. Pelagos for six seconds. Green corridor for four. Pelagos again, but the far pressure door had shifted half a meter relative to its frame.

“What causes the movement?” she asked.

“Neither structure moves. Your accessible geometry switches between local solutions. Position only remains conserved where both solutions contain compatible mass.”

“Then a person in an incompatible section?”

“Gets cut off from whichever exit does not recur. At worst, part of them lacks a compatible return.”

Isca fastened her pressure hood. “Say torn apart when you mean torn apart.”

“I meant unavailable to our instruments,” Maelin said. “The outcome for flesh converges.”

Talia opened the emergency kit. She took two guide lines, seal chalk, a hand lamp, and a mechanical timer. Electronics inherited whichever corridor held them. A wound spring would continue counting if its casing remained materially compatible.

Sal’s channel broke through under static. “Sorn, east control reached. One survivor. Dax with me. West route disappeared.”

“Your position?”

“Spine C, section four. Manual seal S-C-seven. Coolant return ruptured through the floor aftershock.”

Talia located the section. It lay beyond three alternating junctions. “Stay inside compatible mass. Mark your surfaces.”

“Already marked.” A pause followed. “Some marks come back in different places.”

The green corridor replaced Pelagos again. On its wall, someone had drawn a chalk arrow in Talia’s hand.

She had not crossed the junction.

Isca saw it. “That says field room.”

“It says return route.”

“In my script, field room.”

The same strokes carried two instructions because the surfaces had converged around shape rather than meaning. Talia rubbed the chalk with one gloved thumb. It smeared. Whatever version of her had made it, the mark now occupied shared material.

She tied a guide line around the Pelagos handrail and waited for eight continuous seconds. The timer reached nine. Talia crossed. Isca followed before the corridor switched.

Their two shadows traveled ahead of them. One came from Pelagos emergency lamps. The other angled toward the Kest-Blue window, faint but distinct. At each oscillation, one sharpened while its partner thinned.

Section two contained the first trapped worker. Pelagos showed an equipment alcove with its hatch bowed inward by coolant pressure. Kest-Blue showed an open records nook at the same coordinates. Through the overlap, Talia could see a man curled behind the damaged hatch and a branch clerk standing knee-deep inside his outline. Neither seemed able to see the other.

Isca pressed both palms to the frame. “Kest-Blue relay, can you hear me?”

The clerk turned. Her face appeared through frost on the Pelagos hatch.

“Yes. We have a structural alarm and no access to the east stair.”

“Move away from this threshold,” Isca said. “Three meters toward the window. Keep your hands visible.”

The clerk obeyed. Talia waited for the records nook to dominate, stepped through its open frame, then braced one boot against shared flooring. She reached into the Pelagos alcove as the geometry swung back. Her arm appeared to pass through a shelf of blue record tiles.

The trapped man lifted his head. Blood covered one side of his face.

“Can you move?” Talia asked.

“My leg is pinned.”

A fallen equipment rack trapped his boot. In the branch geometry, no rack occupied that location. Talia could not lift an object when its corridor vanished, but she could shift it during each Pelagos interval. Isca counted the oscillation. Eight seconds present. Five seconds branch. Seven present. Six branch.

Talia pulled during the present intervals and held position when the rack disappeared. Her hands closed around empty air in the branch corridor, yet the strain remained in her shoulders through the guide line. On the fourth return, the rack moved enough for the man to drag his foot free.

“Now,” Isca said.

They hauled him across during a nine-second Pelagos interval. The corridor changed before his injured foot cleared. His boot vanished at the ankle.

The man screamed.

Talia held him still. Three seconds later Pelagos returned, and the boot came with it. Blood did not. The matter had remained compatible even when inaccessible.

“You keep that foot inside my chalk marks,” she said. “Do not chase any door.”

A rescue team waited at section one. Talia sent him back along the line and recorded one survivor transferred. The act mattered because the casualty board had begun duplicating names. She trusted custody she could trace, not the station’s count.

The branch clerk remained in the records nook. When green geometry returned, she held up a slate showing seven names and a room number. Isca read them aloud while Talia entered each as branch personnel observed, location unverified. Two names matched signatures from the authenticated census. Five did not appear in Pelagos records at all.

“Ask who has physical contact with them,” Talia said.

Isca relayed the question. The clerk pointed upstairs, then drew a square around three names. Her supervisor had seen those three enter the lower ward. Four had answered only through local audio after the oscillation began.

Talia separated the groups. Witnessed. Heard. Listed. The distinctions felt narrow beside a corridor that could remove matter, yet rescue teams needed to know what they were searching for. She assigned the branch clerk custody of the four audio contacts and gave Isca the witnessed three. Neither woman objected to the burden.

A Pelagos medic reached through the overlap and passed the clerk a mechanical timer. It became visible in her hand only when both geometries shared the alcove floor. She set it beside her slate and began calling intervals. Her count reached them through Isca’s relay two seconds late.

“We can use that,” Talia said. “Keep her on our rhythm.”

“She has a name,” Isca replied. “Veya Morn.”

Talia corrected the rescue log. Veya Morn, branch records clerk, held timing custody at section two. A minute earlier, Pelagos instruments had treated the woman as reflection noise. Now three rescue teams waited for her count before crossing.

Farther east, coolant fog filled the present corridor. Kest-Blue’s geometry offered clear air, but its pressure differed enough to pull at Talia’s hood seal. The oscillation changed airflow each time. Vapor advanced during Pelagos intervals, stopped when the branch corridor returned, then condensed on surfaces shared by both. Rainwater on the alternate windows froze into branching white veins.

Sal called again. “We have Lio Sare. Unconscious, breathing. Dax found a manual return chase.”

“Can you move west?”

“Door S-C-seven opens into a wall every other interval. Its hinge frame has begun shifting.”

“Hold position. We are two sections away.”

Isca touched Talia’s arm. At the next junction, the alternate corridor split around a circular stair that Pelagos lacked. A branch responder stood above them waving both hands.

“Six people in our lower analysis ward,” he called. “The stair exits into your cold pipe passage when the field swings.”

Talia projected both plans. The branch ward overlapped Pelagos section five, where coolant had displaced breathable air. If branch staff descended at the wrong phase, they would enter toxic fog with no suits.

“Keep them upstairs,” she said.

“Our fire system orders evacuation.”

“Override it.”

“We have no authority.”

Isca spoke in Kest-Blue civic shorthand, fast and sharp. The responder answered. Their exchange lasted one interval.

“He can lock the stair if a recognized relay accepts custody,” Isca said.

“Do it.”

“That makes me liable for anyone trapped above.”

“Yes.”

Isca keyed her civic seal against the branch panel when the stair returned. The panel recognized her after two tries. A red bar crossed its green display. Six branch names transferred to Isca Ren, emergency relay custodian.

The action did not solve their fire. It kept six people out of Pelagos coolant. Permission had changed before safety could.

They reached section four with the guide line nearly spent. Sal stood inside a maintenance recess, holding Lio upright against his shoulder. Eren knelt at the manual seal. Frost silvered his hair and coat. His left wrist still carried the open half of a restraint.

The seal separated two pressure zones. Beyond it, a broken coolant return vented into section five. Each Pelagos interval forced cold vapor against the door. Each branch interval replaced the far corridor with a records chamber at lower pressure. The alternating pressure pulled the seal in opposite directions, working its damaged latch loose.

Eren had both hands on the wheel.

“Talia,” Sal said. “Take Lio. I’ll relieve Dax.”

“No,” Eren said without looking around. “The spindle slips when the corridor changes. You need the angle.”

His right boot pressed against a chalk line on the deck. The line marked where the wheel existed in both geometries. Outside that narrow overlap, his hands would lose contact every few seconds.

Talia checked the door frame. Two hinge bolts had drawn halfway out. A third had sheared. If the seal opened during the branch interval, lower pressure would drag coolant fog through the records chamber and down toward the trapped ward. If it opened during Pelagos, the vapor would flood the route behind them and cut off the cargo shelter.

“Can we brace it?” she asked.

Sal pointed to a folded support bar. “Tried. The branch geometry has a cabinet where the foot must seat.”

Isca studied the alternate records room. “Move the cabinet.”

“The people there cannot see us,” Talia said.

“They can see me.”

During the next branch interval, Isca stepped to the windowed side and struck the cabinet with the hand lamp. A clerk appeared behind its translucent doors. Isca shouted instructions. The clerk pulled. Sal pushed the folded support through Pelagos space when it returned. For four cycles, each side moved an object the other could not continuously touch.

The cabinet cleared. Sal planted the support foot. Talia set its head beneath the Pelagos latch housing.

The station lurched.

A phase warning rose to a continuous scream. The green corridor slid sideways by almost a meter. The support foot lost shared purchase and punched through a shelf of branch record tiles. Its head tore free from the latch.

The seal opened a handspan.

Coolant vapor blasted through. Eren hauled the wheel down and closed the gap before Talia reached it. His gloves froze to the metal. The pressure drove him against the frame.

Maelin entered the channel. “Oscillation amplitude doubled. Receiver petal three has lost alignment. I can reduce the swing, but I must shut down local field support in Spine C for ninety seconds.”

“What remains during shutdown?” Talia asked.

“One geometry will dominate. I cannot choose which.”

Sal shifted Lio’s weight. “We need sixty seconds to move him west.”

“The seal will not hold ninety,” Talia said.

Eren looked at the wheel. “It will if someone keeps the spindle against the lower tooth.”

“From which side?”

“This side. The wheel drops out of reach in the other corridor, but the spindle stays under my boot.”

Talia saw the arrangement. Eren could hold the damaged latch only by keeping one foot and both hands across the shared strip. If Kest-Blue geometry dominated, the door frame around him would become a records wall. He might remain inaccessible until the field returned. If Pelagos dominated, coolant pressure would keep loading the seal.

“We can secure you to the line,” she said.

“The line pulls my boot off the tooth.”

“Then Sal holds you.”

Sal shook his head. “I need both arms for Lio.”

“Leave me,” Lio murmured.

Eren flinched at the voice. “No.”

He looked frightened. Talia wanted that entered into any later record. Not calm courage. Not redemption made easy by certainty. His mouth trembled behind the pressure hood, and his eyes kept measuring the corridor that might remove him.

“I broke the loop,” he said. “I know the load on this wheel. Move them.”

Talia took one step toward him.

Eren tightened his grip. “Auditor, if you change custody now, the seal opens.”

He had learned her language and used it correctly.

Talia attached the remaining guide line to his belt without drawing it taut. “Ninety seconds. If the wheel releases, step west. Do not reach after it.”

“Understood.”

She marked the line in the incident log: Eren's seal, manual custody, section S-C-seven. The possessive looked improper in a technical field. No approved component bore his name. Talia left it.

Maelin began shutdown.

Sal carried Lio. Isca took the man’s legs. Talia moved ahead, choosing each threshold by the mechanical timer. The corridor settled toward Kest-Blue. Pelagos frost faded. Rain brightened on the windows. Their guide line continued through the alternate wall behind them, visible as a black thread embedded in pale paneling.

At thirty seconds, Eren’s breathing filled the channel.

At forty-eight, metal struck once.

“Status?” Talia asked.

“Wheel gone. Spindle under my boot.”

“Step west if it moves.”

“I heard you.”

They passed section three. The Kest-Blue clerk had cleared the records nook and now held both hands against the far wall, giving them room even though Lio’s body moved through shelves she could not touch.

At sixty-seven seconds, the black guide line snapped tight.

Eren made a sound without words.

Talia turned. Through two alternating partitions, she saw him for one clear interval. The seal frame had shifted. His left leg remained in the shared strip. The rest of him occupied Pelagos geometry behind the branch wall. Coolant fog pressed around his shoulders. Both hands still held the spindle housing.

“Release it,” she said.

“If I release, it opens.”

“Sal is clear.”

“The ward isn’t.”

Isca shouted toward the branch stair. The six people remained above, locked under her civic custody. If the seal opened now, coolant would cross through the lower records chamber when Pelagos returned.

Seventy-nine seconds.

Talia keyed Maelin. “Bring the field back early.”

“Petal three has not seated.”

“Bring it back.”

“I restore now, we may trap both geometries across the rupture.”

Eren heard. “Don’t.”

At eighty-four seconds, the spindle tore through his glove. Blood darkened the metal. His boot did not move.

Talia could reach him only by abandoning Lio in an unstable threshold and crossing without eight seconds of continuity. The calculation took less than a breath. It remained a calculation she would remember.

“Eren,” she said. “The ward lock releases at ninety. After that, let go.”

“Tell them I admitted it.”

“Your admission is recorded.”

“Not just the sabotage. Tell them I was afraid.”

Eighty-nine.

The branch stair lock changed from red to green. Isca ordered the six staff upward toward a safe roof corridor.

Ninety.

Pelagos returned all at once.

The pressure seal slammed open six centimeters. Coolant filled section five. Eren’s body struck the frame as the spindle spun free. The guide line snapped, then recoiled through the corridor.

Talia started back.

Sal caught her shoulder. “You cannot reach him through that flow.”

On the display, Eren’s biosign fell below readable pulse. The seal door hammered against its remaining hinges. His body had lodged beside the lower frame, narrowing the opening he could no longer hold.

Maelin restored field support. The alternate geometry came back in broken reflections. Kest-Blue responders moved their ward staff away from the poisoned overlap. Pelagos rescue foam flooded section five from the ceiling, sealing coolant to the deck.

Eren did not move again.

They carried Lio into Spine B. Medics took him behind an intact pressure door. Isca stood with one hand against the wall while the branch responders confirmed six staff safe. Sal removed his helmet and entered Eren Dax among the dead.

Talia opened the incident log. The station had attached a sealed Ministry packet during the ninety-second field shutdown, routed through Continuity priority while public channels failed. Its header carried Jun’s cabinet seal and an execution schedule.

FINAL FALL PREPARATORY AUTHORITY.

Pelagos decoherence arrays would receive armature power within thirty days of Council approval. The packet ordered security to preserve the receiver only until testimony concluded. It listed Isca as recoverable anomalous matter and Kest-Blue’s citizens as coherent exclusion structure.

Beside Talia, the wall held two shadows again. One belonged to the emergency lamp. The other crossed the place where Eren’s guide line had vanished.

She copied the Ministry packet into his open casualty record before Continuity could withdraw it.

Chapter 14 — The Order to Fall

Jun entered the Concord chamber through the witness door because the director’s entrance had filled with cameras.

The emergency session occupied Arclight’s oldest assembly room, a circular hall built before accordant transit made distance cheap. Forty-seven system delegations sat in rising tiers. Seven reserve custodians occupied the lower arc. Above them, public galleries had reached capacity before dawn. Rain moved down the exterior glass in controlled silver lines.

Pelagos appeared on a suspended display at the center. Its station image carried a nine-second delay. Repair crews crossed Array Spine C in white suits. A black square marked the compartment where Eren Dax had died.

Jun placed the Continuity Balance on the witness table and checked its custody seal. Talia’s leaked copy of the Final Fall preparation packet had reached every delegate overnight. The chamber no longer needed Jun to reveal what she proposed. It needed her to attach authority to it.

Council Speaker Oren Vale called the session. “Director Orra, state the emergency.”

“Pelagos Reserve has entered unstable phase overlap with a coherent exclusion structure. The overlap has already changed accessible station geometry, transferred pressure between incompatible compartments, and contributed to one confirmed death. Receiver failure models show escalating risk to Pelagos personnel and to any gate using the reserve balance.”

“Requested authority?”

“Forced decoherence of the Pelagos exclusion field by Day 40, thirty days from this vote. Immediate armature preparation. Controlled reduction of Kest-route commitments during the interval. Civil evacuation of the station before execution.”

Noise moved through the galleries. The Speaker did not call for silence until it threatened the record.

Delegate Sava of Kest requested the floor first. Her system seal illuminated beside the image of a planet split between green south and burned north.

“Does forced decoherence kill Isca Ren?”

“No. Isca Ren occupies present matter and will remain under protection.”

“Does it kill the people communicating through the receiver?”

Jun kept both hands on the witness rail. “It ends the coherent field supporting Kest-Blue. Available evidence indicates that citizens there experience continuous biological lives. I expect their lives would end.”

Several delegates looked away. Sava did not.

“How many?”

“Verified census at first contact: 9,412,806.”

The number entered the chamber record under Jun’s voice.

Sava said, “Then your request authorizes killing them.”

“My request prevents an uncontrolled reserve collapse while preserving the transit network that supports current populations. Those consequences do not cancel each other.”

“You selected which consequence receives the verb prevent.”

“I selected the consequence this Council can still govern.”

Speaker Vale gave the next question to Oris Minor. Its delegate appeared by gate relay with hospital light behind him.

“Director, our neonatal wards hold eighteen days of imported cultures. If the Kest routes close during this procedure, what mortality do you project?”

“Under the reduced schedule before you, no closure exceeds nine hours. Oris receives two priority shipments and a third held at Lorn. Expected excess mortality remains below twelve, with a high uncertainty range of forty-one.”

“Under no Final Fall?”

“If Pelagos fails without control and contaminates the balancing ledgers, we cannot predict which routes remain available. Full precautionary suspension produces a median 2.8 million deaths during the first month.”

The delegate sat back. His question had changed custody of the room. Nine million unseen citizens now faced millions whose clinics appeared in the delegates’ own reports.

Talia waited at the opposing witness table. Jun had permitted her to retain audit collar and title for the session, despite three cabinet requests for immediate suspension. Procedure required a recognized officer to authenticate classified evidence. Keeping Talia in office gave that evidence legal force. Removing her would only advertise fear.

Speaker Vale called her.

Talia rose with a stack of transparent record panes. “I submit the Continuity cabinet archive from AY 132 through AY 143. It contains eleven years of structured biological and cultural signals, seven findings of increasing organization, and route orders that increased Kest exclusion loading after Pattern Six demonstrated adaptive correction.”

Jun accepted the archive’s authenticity when asked.

The Speaker said, “Director, did you conceal evidence of persons?”

“I concealed evidence of structure that might indicate agency. The cabinet did not certify personhood before Isca Ren’s arrival and authenticated contact.”

“Why did traffic increase?”

“Kest suffered agricultural losses. Increased transit prevented present deaths.”

Talia activated a graph. Signal complexity rose after each traffic priority. “The increase also strengthened Kest-Blue’s phase-lock. The Ministry lacked proof of personhood because it restricted contact while adding the physical information that made coherent contact more likely.”

Jun opened her own copy. “Correct. Traffic contributed to coherence. No cabinet model predicted convergence into a city, a family, or a polity.”

Talia looked at her across the circular floor. “Your uncertainty protected action in one direction.”

“Yes. Continuing supply preserved identifiable people. Restriction might have killed them to avoid an unverified harm.”

“And after verification?”

“After verification, every available action kills identifiable people.”

No one applauded. Jun had not come to collect agreement through cadence.

Delegate Peret of Deme requested operational detail. “Can Pelagos remain stable while the Council develops another protocol?”

Maelin Drave appeared by secure relay from a station infirmary. A thermal patch covered one side of her neck. “Not under present loading. The sabotage broke a coolant junction. Repairs can restore hardware, but the contact event changed the exclusion field. Pelagos now searches between two coherent geometries. Each search broadens the overlap.”

“How long?”

“Days before more local incidents. Weeks before coherence loss reaches population scale. Those estimates overlap because the machine does not care about your agenda.”

Speaker Vale warned her about decorum.

Maelin ignored him. “Final Fall will make the loss immediate and controlled. Doing nothing distributes it unpredictably. Neither option rescues Kest-Blue.”

“Is there a rescue option?” Peret asked.

“Not one I can certify today.”

Talia said, “Certification today cannot define the limit of what the Council permits us to attempt.”

Jun turned toward her. “Attempt with which crossings?”

“Prepared mass. Distributed sites. A reciprocal exclusion protocol rather than a single reserve dump.”

The chamber screens filled with requests to speak.

Speaker Vale struck the control. “Auditor Sorn, have you submitted an engineering plan?”

“No. The principle requires modeling.”

“Capacity effect?”

“Severe.”

“Gatekeeper commitments?”

“Not yet sought.”

“Success probability?”

“Unknown.”

Jun opened the Pelagos repair feed. A pressure seal moved under remote tools. “Thirty days provides time to model alternatives. It does not provide permission to keep adding load while we hope. My order schedules Final Fall. It does not forbid a certified replacement before execution.”

Talia’s attention sharpened. “Then include an automatic stay if a non-overwrite rescue reaches technical sufficiency.”

“Define sufficiency.”

“Independent review. Material conservation. No erasure of current Kest. A survivable distribution model.”

“Add demonstrated gate synchronization and no greater current mortality than Final Fall operations.”

“That last condition rewards the infrastructure that made one population dependent.”

“It recognizes that dependency exists.”

Their exchange narrowed the dispute into permissions the chamber could amend. Jun had expected Talia to demand delay. Instead, she demanded a stay condition that might redirect engineers without leaving Pelagos unmanaged.

The reserve custodians requested a closed operational caucus before accepting either text. Speaker Vale kept the public feed open but muted the gallery microphones. Seven custodians moved to the floor carrying local gate keys in black cases. Each key could refuse a balance instruction even after Council authorization. Jun had taught younger directors to treat those keys as safety equipment rather than ceremonial assent.

Custodian Ral of Ember Reserve spoke first. “Final Fall shifts the unresolved load before decoherence. Where does it go?”

“Into the six remaining reserves under temporary margins,” Jun said. “Pelagos takes no new exclusions after day twenty.”

“Ember cannot absorb its assigned share without canceling two heavy routes.”

“Your current schedule lists one ore carrier and one habitat shell.”

“The shell carries four thousand pressure doors. Orison’s new ring cannot open residential sectors without them.”

Talia consulted the freight record. “Delay the shell eight days and split its doors among medical-priority modules.”

Ral shook his head. “The carrier frame cannot split. Move it to Lumen Reserve.”

Lumen’s custodian opened a thermal graph. Her star had entered a flare season three weeks earlier than forecast. Accepting Pelagos load would force a safety margin that displaced seed shipments to three agricultural moons.

Jun adjusted the order. Ember would accept less exclusion during the first ten days. Lumen would carry none until its flare probability fell. Two quiet reserves took the difference, but their custodians demanded authority to suspend assignment if local noise exceeded an explicit threshold.

“Put the threshold in veils per thermal interval,” Maelin said over relay. “Do not write ‘unsafe conditions’ and let frightened operators invent a number during execution.”

The custodians supplied instrument ranges. Jun entered them as automatic holds. Each hold extended the schedule by six hours and triggered review rather than director override.

Talia read the new text. “You have given seven operators power to pause an order the Council already passed.”

“Not yet passed,” Jun said. “And yes. Political authority cannot improve a star’s thermal margin.”

Ral placed his gate key on the witness table. “It also cannot make my crews participate in a killing without naming what they do.”

Jun changed the task language. DECOHERENCE SERVICE became KEST-BLUE TERMINATION ARMATURE. No engineer could sign the work log under a euphemism.

The revision cost her Arclight’s immediate support. Its delegate objected that operational labels should remain neutral. Kest answered that concealment had already worn neutral language for eleven years. Speaker Vale ruled the explicit label admissible because it described the expected effect.

Next came evacuation custody. Sal Varo refused remote authority to empty Pelagos before replacement thermal loops passed pressure test. Jun wanted nonessential staff gone by day twelve. Sal said day sixteen. Maelin insisted researchers needed access through day twenty-four if they hoped to certify any alternative.

They settled on staged permission. Families and civilian observers would leave first. Damaged-spine crews would remain until repairs passed. Receiver researchers could stay by individual consent, with daily withdrawal windows guaranteed. Security could not compel scientific staff to operate Final Fall equipment. The order gained four departure dates instead of one.

None of those amendments settled whether the Council could erase Kest-Blue. They determined who could be ordered into a cold corridor, which cargo lost a window, and how a gatekeeper could stop the sequence. Jun trusted those clauses more than any declaration of necessity.

Speaker Vale ordered both texts displayed. The proposed Final Fall order gained a review clause. Talia’s automatic stay appeared beneath it. Delegates began submitting edits.

Arclight moved to require cabinet approval of any alternative. Outer systems rejected the phrase. Kest moved to place Isca on the review panel. The reserve custodians demanded protection from civil liability if they refused unsafe gate commands. Oris Minor requested priority guarantees for medical transit throughout the thirty-day period.

Jun answered each amendment through operational effect. Cabinet veto would make the review politically empty, so she opposed it. Isca possessed relevant knowledge but no current engineering credential, so Jun supported witness standing rather than a technical vote. Gatekeepers retained existing safety authority. Medical priority required cancellation of passenger and private construction windows beginning immediately.

Every answer gave someone less than they wanted. The order acquired shape.

During a recess, Talia crossed the floor under guard. She placed a sealed pane beside Jun’s model.

“Eren Dax’s casualty record,” she said.

“I read it.”

“You classified his sabotage admission but left his final rescue action public.”

“Security requested both sealed.”

“Why refuse?”

“His movement will turn him into a martyr if we hide the failure and a monster if we hide the rescue. The record should carry both.”

Talia glanced at the public gallery. Several white armbands showed among the seats. “That standard would improve your cabinet archive.”

“Yes.”

The answer displeased her more than defense might have.

Jun opened the casualty pane. Eren’s last request remained attached: tell them I was afraid. Talia had entered it as an authenticated statement, not a moral conclusion.

“His fear resembled mine,” Jun said.

“No. He damaged one receiver because he feared closure. You prepared to erase a world because you fear losing custody of the network.”

“I fear preventable deaths.”

“So did he.”

The recess bell sounded.

Jun closed the pane. “Then judge the scale of our authority, not the purity of our fear.”

When testimony resumed, the Council took up legal recognition. Kest proposed emergency citizenship for every authenticated Kest-Blue resident. Arclight moved to recognize only Isca under anomaly protection. The legal engine beneath the language mattered immediately. Citizenship would grant standing to seek injunction, trigger habitat obligations across the Concord, and require representation before any destructive order. Individual anomaly protection would safeguard Isca’s body while leaving Kest-Blue outside current jurisdiction.

Nemi Ren appeared on the civic relay when the field permitted. The image broke into colored bands every few seconds. She wore a teacher’s yellow shirt and held a neighborhood registry pad.

Speaker Vale asked, “Do you claim Concord citizenship?”

“No,” Nemi said. “I claim that your action reaches us. If your law can kill us, your court can hear us.”

“That does not answer the category.”

“It answers the permission.”

Jun watched delay move through the channel. Nemi’s next words arrived four seconds after her mouth formed them.

“We request standing as affected persons. Citizenship can remain disputed without making death undisputed.”

Kest accepted the narrower motion. Arclight did not.

Delegate Hara of Arclight addressed Jun. “Would standing for Kest-Blue citizens prevent Final Fall?”

“It would permit an injunction petition. The court could stay armature preparation while reviewing jurisdiction.”

“What duration?”

“No honest estimate exists for a first-contact claim of this scale.”

“Could Pelagos fail during review?”

“Yes.”

The Arclight delegation called the uncertainty unacceptable. Kest called its rejection a procedural killing. The reserve custodians split.

Talia requested the witness floor. “The Council need not grant citizenship to grant standing. It can recognize Kest-Blue as a contacted polity under emergency exterior-relations law.”

Speaker Vale consulted counsel. “Exterior-relations law applies to settlements physically independent of Concord infrastructure.”

“They are physically dependent on our field because our crossings made them so.”

“Dependence does not satisfy independence.”

“It should not erase personhood either.”

Counsel offered a compromise. Isca Ren would receive protected anomalous person status, including counsel, movement, privacy, and a nonvoting place in technical review. Authenticated Kest-Blue testimony would enter the Council record as evidentiary communication. No finding on citizenship, polity, or jurisdiction would issue before a later session.

Jun read the compromise. It protected the body present in the chamber’s reach. It denied legal hands to everyone the order targeted.

Speaker Vale asked for her position.

“I support Isca Ren’s protection. I oppose using that protection as evidence that the Council has answered Kest-Blue’s status.”

The gallery stirred at her qualification.

Talia said, “Will you support affected-person standing?”

“Not without a time-bounded emergency court able to decide before Pelagos fails.”

“Then create the court.”

“The Council has not agreed that it holds jurisdiction.”

“You invoke jurisdiction to order Final Fall.”

The contradiction entered the record. Jun could have answered that physical control and legal membership differed. The distinction remained valid and inadequate.

“Put the question to vote,” she said.

Affected-person standing failed by four systems. Kest-Blue citizenship failed by eleven. Protected anomalous person status for Isca passed with only two abstentions.

On the central display, Isca received the decision at Pelagos. She stood beside Talia’s empty remote station, arms folded against a borrowed gray jacket. No gratitude appeared on her face.

The Council moved to Final Fall authority.

Jun presented the final operational sequence. For twenty days, crews would install decoherence armatures and evacuate nonessential staff. Ten days remained for alignment, route reduction, and final review. Execution required Jun’s seal plus confirmation from three reserve custodians. The automatic stay would activate if an alternative passed independent technical review, conserved matter, preserved current Kest, demonstrated synchronized gate control, and met the order’s current-life safety ceiling.

Talia objected to the safety ceiling one last time. “A rescue may impose rationing without imposing equivalent death. Your model must not treat reduced comfort as mortality.”

“Agreed.” Jun amended the clause. “Only projected deaths and irreversible medical harm count against the ceiling.”

The change won two outer-system votes. It lost one inner delegation worried about unrest. Timing shifted. Permission narrowed.

The roll began.

Each system seal lit on the chamber wall. Kest voted no. Deme voted yes after securing membrane priority. Oris Minor voted yes. Pelagos Reserve’s custodian abstained because her staff faced the order’s physical risk. Arclight voted yes. Outer agricultural systems divided according to stock on hand and trust in Maelin’s estimates.

Jun did not watch the total until the Speaker closed the roll.

Twenty-six yes. Nineteen no. Two abstentions.

“The Final Fall order carries,” Speaker Vale said.

The chamber display requested the director’s execution seal.

Talia stood behind the opposing witness rail. “You can refuse.”

“Then the deputy signs by noon, without the stay clause we forced into the text.”

“You do not know that.”

“I know the succession order.”

Jun placed her hand over the seal plate. The system required pulse, print, and spoken acceptance. Around the chamber, delegates began transmitting medical priorities, evacuation objections, and armature staffing limits. Law had already become cargo.

“Jun Orra,” she said. “Director of Stellar Continuity. I accept execution custody under the adopted conditions.”

The plate warmed beneath her palm.

At Pelagos, DAY 40 / THIRTY DAYS appeared on every station clock. Repair workers paused beneath the number. Isca looked up at it. Behind her, a reflection showed a different room filled with people who had no vote in the chamber.

Jun pressed her hand down. Her seal entered the order, and the first armature shipment received departure clearance.

Interlude II — Voices Without a Present

PELAGOS CIVIC RELAY: TESTIMONY BATCH 44-B

Authentication notice: The following records arrived through the Pelagos exclusion receiver between 03:12 and 05:47 station time. Civic seals were checked against the Kest-Blue census root transmitted on Day 3. Voice continuity, household registry, and local witness countersigns met current evidentiary standards. “Authenticated” denotes source integrity. It does not reflect a Concord finding on citizenship, jurisdiction, or legal personhood.

Editorial action: Compression failures appear in brackets. No testimony has been reordered within its local sequence. Profanity remains where intelligible. Domestic addresses have been reduced to district level at the speakers’ request.

Record 44-B-01

Name: Lessa Om

Occupation: Municipal lift mechanic, North Cloud District

Local witnesses: Naro Pell, shift steward. Beya Om, household member.

I maintain six public lift towers between the lower fog line and the east canopy roads. Two towers carry school traffic. One carries produce from the hillside terraces. The older three still use Kest Standard bearings, which your archives may recognize, though we changed the brake housing after the AY 88 quake.

Yesterday my crew argued for four hours about whether to keep doing scheduled maintenance. If Pelagos fails, nobody knows whether our tower remains to need a brake. Our steward said work without a future wastes parts. I said a child can fall tomorrow even if officials erase us next month. We replaced the cable.

My request concerns a shipment already contracted through our local council: eight ceramic bearing sleeves, dimensions attached. Do not send them if another place needs the mass for medicine. Do not call the request proof that we expect rescue. It proves only that the east school lift still opens at seven.

Record 44-B-02

Name: Harun Vale and Omi Sere

Occupations: Restaurant owner. Refrigeration technician.

Dispute file: Blue Market Commercial Mediation 7711

HARUN VALE: Omi owes me two months of cold-room service. He says the receiver emergency suspends ordinary debts.

OMI SERE: I said the emergency suspends collection penalties. I serviced his compressor twice.

HARUN: You cleaned a filter once and drank broth for an hour.

OMI: The second visit happened while you were at the Council queue.

MEDIATOR: This relay slot was allocated for testimony concerning material effects of Final Fall.

HARUN: My material effect includes spoiled fish.

OMI: Mine includes clients who think the end of the world cancels invoices but not repairs.

MEDIATOR: State the remedy requested.

HARUN: He finishes the service.

OMI: He pays the base invoice. I waive penalties until the Pelagos decision.

HARUN: Accepted if he returns my wrench.

OMI: The wrench belongs to me.

MEDIATOR: Tool ownership transferred to ordinary commercial docket. Agreement on service and payment entered. Both parties requested that the Concord receive the docket without commentary.

Record 44-B-03

Issuing office: Rainward Birth Registry, Northern Region

Event: Live birth, 02:41 local time

Child: Sio Meret An

Parents: Davi An. Teren Meret.

Attending clinician: Asha Morn, license 18-44-N

Birth occurred during a six-minute receiver interruption. Infant presented feet first and required assisted breathing for eighty seconds. Parent Davi An declined evacuation from the registry clinic because flood warnings had closed the lower road. Parent Teren Meret authorized public transmission of the following statement:

Sio breathed before the signal returned. No official from either Kest gave permission. We request no ceremonial message and no place ahead of children already waiting. Enter the time accurately. If your Council keeps a record of us, begin by spelling the name correctly.

Registry note: Household declined to name the child “Pelagos,” “Future,” or “Defiance,” despite suggestions from two neighbors. The child bears a grandmother’s name.

Record 44-B-04

Name: Peren Tu

Occupation: Debt assessor, Southern Recovery Cooperative

Our southern districts lost twenty-two thousand businesses during the plague years. The cooperative bought household debt at reduced value and extended payment across twelve harvest cycles. Current balance records affect access to seed, housing, and apprenticeship. If instantiation occurs without financial ledgers, families with paid accounts may arrive owing money they already repaid. Families with unpaid accounts may arrive free of obligations their neighbors carried for years.

I am not asking the Concord to enforce our debt schedule. I ask you to preserve it long enough for us to argue among ourselves. Rescue that strips ownership records from poor households and keeps professional credentials from wealthy ones would create a convenient equality for exactly one morning.

Attached are verification roots for 408,212 accounts. The attachment does not contain private balances. It permits later comparison against records held in district courts. Please acknowledge custody, not validity.

Record 44-B-05

Name: Ulan Iri

Occupation: Classroom aide, temporary plague memorial school

Today fourteen children drew the two Kests. Seven drew yours underground because the archive pictures show buried northern towns. Five drew ours inside trees. One drew both planets sharing a kitchen. The last child refused the assignment and drew a pump because our washroom drain had backed up.

Our lesson concerned the Council vote. I was instructed to explain that Isca Ren received protection as an anomalous person. A student asked whether “anomalous” means no one has to know your neighborhood. Another asked if Isca becomes less protected when she sleeps because she stops giving evidence.

I did not answer with law. I told them Isca keeps her name while adults dispute categories. Please send a better answer before tomorrow’s class. We begin at nine, unless the drain floods again.

Record 44-B-06

Name: Renna Vos

Occupation: Water allocation clerk, Cinder Shore Municipality

My office receives 2,300 complaints each week during dry season. Most concern neighbors washing roofs outside assigned hours. The attached petition contains sixty-one requests to punish one apartment block for filling a decorative pool. Inspection found no pool. A broken main had filled the excavation beside their building.

I include this because your public channels describe Kest-Blue as speaking with one voice. We do not agree about roof water. We do not agree about the southern debt compact, rescue priority, school closures, or whether Nemi Ren should serve on the emergency council. Yesterday someone threw red dye on our municipal door because we reserved transmitter time for hospital logs rather than farewell messages.

My request: when you authenticate testimony, preserve objections attached to it. Consensus inferred from survival fear will produce false records. We are asking together not to be killed. That does not make us agreed about anything else.

Record 44-B-07

Name: Tol Nesh

Occupation: Apprenticeship examiner, western turbine works

I certify mechanics. Eleven candidates completed practical exams this month. Their certificates require my seal and two shop witnesses. The field took one witness signature from each file during yesterday’s oscillation. We repeated the signatures on paper and placed copies in separate buildings.

If only credentialed people receive early transport, the candidates may lose places because their records look incomplete. I request provisional equivalence for anyone whose examination began before the Council order. I also request that rescue planners stop asking for “essential occupations” without stating who decides. Turbine repair sounds essential. Child care sounds ordinary until the turbine crew has nowhere safe to leave children.

Candidate list follows under privacy seal.

Record 44-B-08

Name: Jori Pell

Occupation: Tram operator, Capital Rain Line

The rain line still runs. Passenger count has doubled because people go across the city to settle accounts in person. Half carry storage boxes. The rest carry food to relatives who accuse them of giving up.

At 16:20, a passenger refused fare and said no court would collect tomorrow. Another passenger paid for him. They then fought about whether payment had insulted him. I stopped the tram for seven minutes, separated them, and issued both a delay citation. The citations remain appealable for thirty days. I recognize that number may interest you.

I request no answer. I want the record to show that on the day your Council scheduled our death, Tram 14 finished its route eleven minutes late because two living men disagreed about a fare.

Record 44-B-09

Name: Mera Dax

Occupation: Heat-loop dispatcher, Nacre Town

Relationship to present-world casualty Eren Dax: sister, branch genealogy match unconfirmed.

I have been told a man with my brother’s name damaged your station and died holding a pressure seal. Our Eren works in a seed warehouse and dislikes enclosed transit. I do not claim the dead man as family. Shared ancestry does not grant me his grief, and your records cannot tell me whether he laughed the same way.

If you release his final statement, send it without cutting the sabotage from the rescue. Fear explains an action only after responsibility names it. We learned that during the plague, when neighbors broke quarantine to bring medicine through southern checkpoints. Some saved lives. Some spread infection. Several did both.

My municipal heat stock supports twelve days under reduced service. This note does not request priority. It requests the maintenance diagram that caused the Pelagos failure, so our crews can check whether any copied systems carry the same obsolete control.

Record 44-B-10

Name: Aven Sar

Occupation: Orchard pollination coordinator

We released the northern moths this morning. Their emergence followed a warm front by three days, later than forecast. I have attached temperature logs because your meteorologists asked for local measurements after the AY 61 divergence.

My daughter says measurements make us easier to believe. I told her belief should not depend on useful weather. She asked what does make a person count. I said birth, work, hunger, promises, and someone noticing when you fail to come home. She asked which item your Council lacks.

I had an answer, but the field alarm started. If this reaches Isca, tell her the cloud forest bloom began along the upper road. Tell Nemi that the district council approved the school shelter repairs. Tell Aru the pale moth he named landed on the kitchen glass this morning and I kept the wing scale because

[TRANSMISSION LOSS: carrier dropped at 05:47:13. No completion recovered. Source seal remained valid through final packet. Receiver retry returned thermal noise.]

PELAGOS CUSTODY RECEIPT

Ten records received. Nine complete. One incomplete after transmission loss. All source seals preserved.

Legal routing copied the batch to the Concord Council under “evidentiary communication from unclassified external structure.” The citizenship petition remained pending without a hearing date.

At 06:03 station time, Rainward Birth Registry requested confirmation that the name Sio Meret An had arrived intact.

Pelagos returned the spelling.

Part III — The Division

Chapter 15 — The Auditor Without an Office

Talia released the sealed ledger at 06:14 Arclight civil time, while Continuity House changed shifts and half its security staff faced the rain court.

She did not copy the cabinet archive. Copying would have given Jun a clean charge and the public a contaminated fact. Instead, Talia opened the emergency review she had signed eleven years earlier, attached the original Pelagos receipts already admitted before the Concord Council, and invoked the disclosure clause for an imminent act against a recognized protected person. Isca’s narrow legal status made the clause available. Nine million unrecognized petitioners made it necessary.

The submission window asked whether disclosure risked route confidence.

YES, Talia entered.

A second field requested mitigation.

PUBLIC VERIFICATION OF SOURCE CHAINS. NO OPERATIONAL GATE KEYS INCLUDED.

Her audit key trembled against the reader because her hand had begun to shake. She pressed harder. The reader accepted bone pattern, blood warmth, and sixteen years of authority. A final warning filled the wall: Continuity Black material would leave cabinet custody and enter the public evidence queue. Revocation could follow before review. Criminal referral remained possible.

Behind the warning lay a choice Jun had taught her to identify in other people’s accounts. A cost hidden in procedure still belonged to the person who authorized it.

Talia signed.

For two seconds nothing changed. Artificial rain crossed the court beyond her office glass, silver under the morning lamps. Clerks arrived with folded coats over their arms. A maintenance worker guided a polish rig along the dark floor. The quiet air of Continuity House held no alarm, no shout, no outward recognition that its oldest secret had entered a queue visible to every accredited civic archive in the Concord.

Then her wall went blank.

The desk followed. Cabinet panes collapsed into gray rectangles. Her door seal changed from white to amber, and a line appeared across the remaining local display.

CLEARANCE REVOKED: TALIA SORN.

She watched the wording settle. No allegation accompanied it. Revocation protected systems while accusations required reasons, and reasons could be challenged.

Someone knocked once before entering on an emergency override.

Sal Varo came in without a security team. Pelagos had left a gray fatigue in the skin around his eyes. He wore civilian transit black under his custody harness, as if he had traveled directly from the reserve station and changed only what regulations required. A small recorder blinked at his collar.

“Step away from the desk,” he said.

“I no longer have a desk.”

“Furniture remains under Ministry inventory.”

“That sounds like you.”

“It sounds lawful. Please move.”

Talia rose and placed both hands where his recorder could see them. Sal crossed to the wall, checked the dead cabinet panes, then inspected the reader slot. He did not touch her coat or ask for her personal slate.

“You submitted through emergency review,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You attached restricted receipts.”

“Receipts entered into Council evidence yesterday. Their classification survived only because the Council closed the annex.”

“That question will occupy people with larger tables.” Sal looked toward the rain court. “Did you remove data by any other route?”

“No.”

“Did you conceal a cabinet device on your person?”

“No.”

“Will a search prove those answers?”

“Yes.”

His mouth tightened. “Director Orra expected theft.”

“Jun expected me to choose the charge she prepared.”

Sal held her gaze long enough to acknowledge the distinction without approving it. He opened a custody form on his wrist and read each authority aloud. Talia could remain in personal possession of clothing, medical devices, private correspondence, and any record predating Ministry classification. He could hold her for questioning during the integrity inspection. He could not seize unclassified personal property without a separate order.

“Your apartment access has been suspended,” he said.

The sentence reached her more cleanly than the revoked clearance. Her rooms occupied the inner curve of Arclight’s old centrifugal city, fourteen minutes on foot from Continuity House. Ministry tenancy came with her post. Every book, every coat, and the chipped blue bowl her mother had fired during an engineering apprenticeship now sat behind an amber seal.

“On what grounds?”

“Credential-linked residential security. You can request escorted retrieval after inventory.”

“Today?”

“Not today.”

Outside her office, feet slowed. Staff had begun receiving the disclosure alert. Talia saw faces angle toward the glass and away again. One junior analyst raised a hand to the wall, perhaps opening the public queue, perhaps closing it before a supervisor noticed.

Sal gestured toward the door. “We are leaving by the west service corridor.”

“I want to walk through the audit floor.”

“That route increases disruption.”

“Revocation has already disrupted it.”

“My authority concerns custody, not symbolism.”

“Then you have no lawful reason to choose my corridor.”

For a moment he looked almost tired enough to laugh. “You argue like a person who still has appeals staff.”

“I trained them well.”

Sal checked his form. “The central corridor is permissible.”

They walked through Continuity House together.

No one stopped Talia. That hurt more than accusation would have. The audit floor contained sixty-four stations arranged in shallow arcs, each facing outward toward maintenance access and inward toward a shared incident wall. She had helped design the arrangement after a gate fire proved that auditors staring at private panes missed physical trouble around them. Now every station held the disclosure notice.

At the nearest desk, Peren Ai stood when she passed. He had worked three famine reviews under Talia and once slept beneath his console during a nine-system contamination recall.

“You included the casualty estimates?” he asked.

“I included both closure models.”

“Did you include your old signature?”

“Yes.”

Peren sat down again. He did not thank her. His attention returned to the ledger, which meant more.

Across the aisle, an allocator named Senn closed her display. “My sister’s insulin crosses twice,” she said. “If this shuts Kest routes, I hope your evidence knows how to keep her alive.”

“It does not,” Talia answered.

The honesty sharpened Senn’s face. “Then what did you release?”

“A record of what continued traffic has done. Supply decisions remain ours.”

“Easy word, ours. You do not have a route console now.”

Sal shifted one step closer but did not interrupt.

Talia looked over the floor she had expected to use for reform. Some clerks read with both hands pressed against their mouths. Others copied route forecasts into private work panes, already calculating which systems would suffer under voluntary slowdowns. Two supervisors ordered staff away from public channels. Near the incident wall, someone had pinned Kest-Blue’s verified population beside the projected mortality from immediate closure. The figures did not cancel each other. At least the wall refused that lie.

“Keep the models paired,” Talia told Peren.

Her former subordinate nodded once.

Security met them at the lift. Sal refused the offered restraints. “Personal custody,” he said to the officer. “No arrest order.”

By the time they reached street level, the rain court had opened to real morning. Arclight’s white star shone through city filters as a pale disk. Walkways crossed the centrifugal city’s central void in layered curves, crowded with workers stopping beside public panels. The sealed ledger had propagated through local civic archives faster than commentary could form around it.

One panel showed a cabinet receipt from AY 132. Structured biological recurrence: confidence sixty-eight percent. Continue observation under load.

Another displayed Talia’s old warning. The words looked younger than she felt: NO CLASSIFICATION CAN SUBSTITUTE FOR A PERSONHOOD TEST WHEN THE SIGNAL RETAINS ERROR, PREFERENCE, AND MEMORY.

Beneath it appeared her signature approving continued secrecy for ninety days.

The ninety days had become eleven years.

A woman in a food-union jacket noticed Talia beside the panel. Recognition moved through the walkway in small turns of the head. Someone called her murderer. Someone else began applauding. The applause spread for six beats, collided with shouts about closed routes, and broke into separate rhythms.

Sal guided her toward a transit alcove before the crowd could choose one response.

“You cannot hold me for public discomfort,” Talia said.

“I am moving you away from a probable assault.”

“Whose?”

“Several people have not decided.”

A boy stood on a bench to see over adult shoulders. His school coat carried the blue lamp of a gate family. He shouted, “Did they know?”

Talia turned before Sal could block her.

“For eleven years,” she said.

“Did you?”

“Not enough at first. Enough later to have acted sooner.”

The admission reached every recorder in the alcove. Sal closed his eyes briefly, then ushered her into a local shuttle.

Continuity House held her for seven hours in a records interview room that lacked cabinet access and offered excellent tea. Three integrity officers reconstructed her submission from system logs. They asked the same questions in changing order: whether Maelin had supplied hidden keys, whether Isca had coerced her, whether foreign gatekeepers possessed raw cabinet tables. Talia answered from the audit trail. Every fact had traveled through a lawful opening, though not one Jun had expected her to use.

During the fourth hour, public archive mirrors began failing under demand. The officers tried to blame the release. A civic clerk joined by local channel and explained that the queue remained intact; access nodes had throttled popular documents to protect emergency traffic. The distinction mattered. Talia insisted they record it.

At 15:03, Sal entered with her coat and personal slate.

“No data charges today,” he said.

“Other charges?”

“Director Orra has referred breach of duty and interference with Final Fall preparation. Neither grants detention without a magistrate.”

“Jun knows that.”

“She also knows where you will sleep?”

Talia checked her slate. Apartment access denied. Ministry accounts frozen pending separation inventory. Her private funds remained available, but three nearby lodgings had already canceled automated bookings after her credentials changed. The city required a stable identity token for every room, and institutional revocation had made hers look uncertain.

Sal placed a paper claim strip on the table. “This permits escorted retrieval tomorrow. Two bags. No Ministry devices.”

“You could authorize access now.”

“I could exceed the order now. That would help you and weaken the rule I may need later.”

His refusal landed without cruelty. Talia folded the strip into her pocket.

“Did you read it?” she asked.

“The ledger?”

“Yes.”

“I read the custody sections. I do not need field equations to know when someone has entered my protection.”

“Kest-Blue has not entered it.”

“No recognized jurisdiction has placed them there.”

“Would you obey an arrest order for Isca?”

Sal picked up his recorder. “If lawful.”

“And if Final Fall makes the appeal meaningless?”

“Then someone should challenge the order before I am asked to turn custody into execution.”

The words did not offer alliance. They marked a limit, which Talia trusted more.

He escorted her to the public side of Continuity House and left her beneath the rain court. The shift lamps had dimmed toward evening. Water struck the black stone in measured rows. At the far end, people had placed printed pages from the sealed ledger under the covered walk. Some bore names of Kest-Blue witnesses from the asylum petition. Others carried route-mortality forecasts or photographs of current-system clinics.

Two vigils occupied the same court without facing each other.

Talia walked between them. Citizens from the disclosure vigil thanked her with an intensity that made her step back. A route-family organizer asked who would accept responsibility for refrigerated cargo if captains refused Pelagos assignments. Talia said she did not know. Someone threw a paper cup. It missed and burst against a column, spreading bitter tea across a page listing branch births.

Her slate received ninety-three requests for interview, six threats, two offers of legal counsel, and one message from Peren: AUDIT FLOOR PRESERVING SOURCE CHAINS. SUPERVISION CONTESTED.

No message came from Jun.

A civic archivist caught Talia before she left the court. The woman introduced herself as Daro Mev and held out a portable verification plate scarred by years of public-record work. Three source chains had arrived with cabinet signatures intact. A fourth contained a missing witness hash between Pelagos receipt and Continuity review.

“Your release named the gap,” Daro said. “It did not fill it.”

“I never held that segment.”

“People online say omission proves fabrication.”

“People online have had six hours.” Talia took the plate and studied the chain. The absent hash fell on the day Jun’s cabinet had moved a structured signal from scientific review to route-confidence exposure. “Publish the gap as a gap. Do not infer the missing record in either direction.”

Daro frowned. “That gives both vigils a weapon.”

“It gives them the same uncertainty.”

A route-family organizer came under the shelter with rain on his sleeves. Talia recognized him from the opposite side of the court. He carried a clinic allocation showing four days of immune seed at Lorn Reach and no local manufacturing stock. The voluntary slowdown after public rumor had already delayed its next culture shipment.

“You want paired models,” he said. “Pair this.”

Talia set the clinic record beside the missing hash. One described a known shortage under current traffic conditions. The other exposed a decision concealed for eleven years. Her old office would have routed them to different desks, ensuring no analyst had to hold both claims in one field of view.

“Has the shipment missed a committed window?” she asked.

“Captain declined after the Final Fall vote. Insurance called Pelagos instability a system hazard.”

“Then disclosure did not close the route.”

“It made fear expensive enough to close it for us.”

She recorded his distinction. “Give the allocation to Daro with patient identities removed. The public packet needs consequences from rumor as well as consequences from exclusion.”

The organizer looked offended by her willingness to include him. “You think making the ledger larger fixes what you did?”

“No. It prevents my side from choosing the margin.”

Daro accepted the clinic record and generated a receipt for both of them. Around the rain court, the vigils kept their distance. Yet their documents now shared one verification plate. Talia watched the two source chains settle beside each other, neither granted power to erase its rival.

A young clerk from Continuity House arrived carrying a sealed envelope. Talia expected a private note from Jun and hated the quick hope that entered her before she could audit it. The envelope held only formal notice of separation, effective at 06:15, one minute after disclosure. Her pension remained under review. Her duty of secrecy survived termination.

“Who asked you to bring this?” Talia said.

“Automatic service failed because your residence credential would not accept delivery.” The clerk swallowed. “I volunteered.”

“Why?”

“My mother lives at Lorn Reach.” He glanced toward the route organizer. “I read both models.”

Talia signed the receipt. The clerk took it back without offering judgment, which left her standing with something harder to dismiss: a person who depended on present traffic and still wanted the concealed record opened.

Night in the centrifugal city never grew fully dark. Window bands shifted toward amber while maintenance lamps retained their white. Talia spent two hours in a civic advocate’s office signing separation challenges. The advocate found a temporary room across the city, then lost it when the building insurer flagged protest traffic. A university offered a guest berth and withdrew after its trustees objected.

At 22:18, a narrow information packet reached her through a Kest transit window. Pelagos contact had been unavailable since the previous route closed, so the packet carried a timestamp from six hours earlier. Isca’s note contained nine words: I saw the ledger. You included your signature. Good.

Maelin added a diagnostic attachment and no greeting. Final Fall calibration had begun around the outer petals.

Talia forwarded the attachment to her counsel and found a bench under the west transfer lamps. She could afford a capsule hotel farther out, if one accepted a manual identity review. Her shoulder ached from carrying the bag Sal had allowed her to keep. Inside lay one change of clothes, an audit notebook, and nothing suited to becoming a life.

A cargo announcement rolled across the concourse. Morrow Glass, heavy carrier, Kest registration, had cleared inner customs after a medical delivery. Departure awaited cargo assignment.

Talia read it twice.

Rhen found her before she reached the dock.

The captain wore a patched outer-route coat and carried two cups of machine coffee. “You look like Arclight has attempted to digest you.”

“It revoked my tenant status before trying.”

“I heard. Your capital has efficient priorities.” Rhen handed her a cup. “Are you under arrest?”

“Not tonight.”

“Are you carrying stolen cabinet hardware?”

“No.”

“Explosives?”

“No.”

“Unlicensed livestock?”

Talia looked at them.

“Dock rules,” Rhen said. “I like clarity.”

They crossed into the carrier berth. Beyond the pressure glass, Morrow Glass rested among polished inner-system couriers like a repaired tool left on a formal table. New plates covered the forward truss damage. Refrigeration vapor drifted beneath the cargo spine. Two crew members guided an empty pallet toward customs while arguing about whose turn it was to clean a coolant sieve.

“I cannot pay passage until my accounts separate,” Talia said.

“I am not selling you passage.”

“Then what are you offering?”

Rhen pointed through the glass toward the carrier’s command blister. “A berth. Small one. Ventilator complains every nineteen minutes. The door sticks if truss four runs cold.”

“Where are you going?”

“Wherever empty cargo rooms need counting.”

“That sounds like evidence work.”

“It sounds like mass.” Rhen’s humor left their face. “You put the cabinet’s numbers where my crew could read them. I can put you somewhere apartment credentials do not reach.”

Talia checked the local departure board. No route window stood open to Pelagos. A Kest run would commit at dawn, carrying only stored packets onward until another physical window connected the reserve. She would not speak to Isca in real time. She would not reach Maelin before Final Fall crews completed another day of preparation.

None of that changed the offer.

At the dock seal, Rhen pressed a crew token into her hand. The cheap metal carried scratches from older assignments. Her revoked audit key remained in her pocket, still warm from failed readers.

The carrier hatch opened on worn paint, warm machine air, and a narrow passage leading away from Continuity House.

Talia stepped aboard before Arclight could revoke that too.

Chapter 16 — A Fleet of Empty Rooms

Rhen’s first rescue inventory contained seven hundred and twelve empty beds and no air to fill them.

The figure floated above Morrow Glass’s galley table while the carrier waited for an Orison transit window. Talia had taken the narrow end of the bench, where the ventilation complaint arrived every nineteen minutes as promised. Two cargo union delegates occupied the other side. Between them sat a projection of the unfinished habitat: sleeping shells, pressure sectors, water loops, agricultural bays, and enough blank gray volume to make generosity look easy.

“Beds are furniture,” Rhen said. “Show me sealed rooms.”

Orison’s construction steward, Fal Iven, appeared by delayed local relay from the far side of Arclight’s gate. His image carried a timestamp nine minutes old because no physical window had opened yet. “Four residential arcs hold pressure. Two passed occupancy inspection last year.”

“How many people?”

“Under design standards, eighty-one thousand.”

“Under current installed oxygen?”

Fal looked away to consult someone off camera. The answer reached them after another delay. “Nine thousand, short term.”

“Water?”

“Eleven thousand if reclamation holds.”

“Heat rejection?”

“Seven thousand during full daylight load.”

One of the union delegates, Mora Sen, crossed her arms. “Your seven hundred beds have become seven thousand spaces.”

“They have become three different limits,” Rhen said. “Write all three.”

Talia entered the numbers into a public planning sheet. She had spent two nights aboard and still moved like someone expecting doors to challenge her credentials. The carrier gave her none worth challenging. A strip of tape labeled the galley heater. Crew notices covered the wall beside maintenance debt and birthdays. No cabinet pane waited behind the paint.

Mora tapped the habitat diagram. “The public asked what carriers can offer. If we publish seven thousand, they will hear a promise.”

“Then publish zero,” said Pell Or, the second delegate. “We are not authorized to receive anybody.”

Rhen turned the projection until Orison’s two pressurized arcs faced them like incomplete parentheses. “A hypothetical reception effort needs numbers before authorization. Otherwise every official who opposes it gets to call it impossible without finding the impossible part.”

The galley speaker clicked. Origin commitment had begun at Arclight. For the next four minutes the prepared transit window would carry narrow information packets between gates. Rhen pushed Orison’s latest construction records into the packet queue, then added requests to carrier registries at Kest, Lorn Reach, Sable, and six food systems. Voice conversations would have to wait for open windows or for the people involved to meet.

“Do not call this rescue,” Talia said.

Mora stared at her. “That is an interesting restraint from the person who released the ledger.”

“The physical model remains unfinished. Call it reception planning.”

“People donate to rescue.”

“People also hold you to it.”

Rhen liked the distinction because it carried a cargo smell. Promise less than the deck could bear. Deliver more only after checking the locks.

The crossing warning sounded. Everyone secured loose cups. Morrow Glass settled into origin geometry with a vibration Rhen felt through the bench before the console confirmed it. A thirty-year-old carrier did not conceal preparation beneath silent surfaces. Pumps changed pitch. Bulkheads tightened. Somewhere aft, a coolant line knocked twice against its bracket.

Rhen reached the command blister as the first horizon formed.

Orison arrived around them in pieces. Dark construction trusses resolved against a white star.
