The unfinished ring crossed half the viewport, its completed sectors bright with work lamps and its empty span open to vacuum. Two carriers waited in exterior berths. Neither carried passengers. Both held structural foam and water tanks meant for a future expansion whose funding had been postponed three times.

The arrival completed without a second horizon.

Rhen still counted eleven seconds before releasing their breath.

Talia noticed from the observer couch. She said nothing.

Dock crews met them in a sector built for thirty thousand residents and occupied by four hundred construction workers. The concourse lights activated in sequence ahead of their party, then darkened behind them to conserve power. Direction signs pointed toward schools, clinics, and civic kitchens that had never opened. Each sealed storefront contained dust on the inside.

Fal Iven waited beside an inspection lock. He proved shorter than his relay image suggested and wore pressure webbing over formal clothes. “You wanted sealed rooms.”

“I want the difference between sealed and survivable,” Rhen said.

Fal opened the lock.

Cold air entered the corridor with a mineral smell from unused filters. Beyond lay a residential arc lined with identical doors. Construction crews had installed plumbing trunks and emergency lamps, but no wall panels covered the service conduits. Rhen checked the nearest room. Four bunk frames folded from one wall. A wash alcove occupied the other. Air moved through the ceiling grille at a rate meant to keep seals conditioned, not lungs alive.

Mora swept a hand across the corridor. “People will see this and understand.”

“They will see rooms,” Rhen answered. “We need to understand everything those rooms consume.”

For six hours they walked the systems. Orison had volume. It had partial pressure integrity, water storage, and enough radiation shielding in the completed arcs. It lacked filter media, protein stock, medical waste capacity, childcare equipment, and trained crews for occupancy above seven thousand. One heat radiator contained a manufacturing flaw that limited the sunward sector during high load. Agricultural bays held soil racks without soil.

Rhen recorded each deficiency beside a supplier rather than beside a hope. Filter media came from Varin. Protein vats could ship from Kest if coastal fever allocations remained protected. Shielding patches occupied metal mass that three mining habitats had already promised to repair their own docks. Every answer displaced another use.

At the central hub, Fal showed them a transparent wall overlooking the incomplete span. Sunlight crossed the ring’s inner curve and stopped at a raw truss end. “If you count unfinished sectors, Orison could take three hundred thousand.”

“No,” Rhen said.

“That came from your public planning sheet.”

“That came from design capacity. Design does not breathe.”

Fal’s shoulders settled. “Seven thousand, then.”

“Seven thousand with heat repairs, intake supplies, and crew. Nine thousand during a short emergency if arrivals distribute across both arcs. Put three hundred thousand in an appendix marked unbuilt.”

Talia revised the sheet. “What does it cost to reach fifty thousand?”

Fal named a construction schedule of fourteen months.

“We have weeks,” Mora said.

“Then fifty thousand does not exist yet.”

Outside the wall, a maintenance cart crawled along the open truss. Its lamps illuminated docking sockets for modules never fabricated. Rhen had hauled enough deferred projects to recognize the politics of an empty frame. Every official liked capacity that lived in future tense.

That evening, three captains came aboard Morrow Glass for a cargo conference. Two more joined through the next open Kest packet window, sending recorded statements because conversational delay across closed routes would have turned argument into theater.

Captain Jes Aro of Kindly Weight spoke first. “My lower passenger deck has been empty since the Sable contract ended. Four hundred couches, independent air for three days. I can commit it after the seed run.”

“How much reserve mass?” Rhen asked.

“Six hundred tonnes if I drop commercial ballast.”

“Commercial ballast pays your crew.”

Jes shrugged. “Crew voted.”

Captain Ulan Per of Saffron Debt shook his head. “Votes do not feed families. My carrier has rooms because the mining route collapsed. I sign nothing that lets capital call those rooms donated.”

“Then name your operating cost,” Mora said.

“Ministry seizure rates plus hazard.”

“The Ministry may charge us for interference next week.”

“Then find another carrier.”

Rhen let the argument run until both sides had spent their cleanest principles. “We are not pricing morality. We are pricing thrust, food, maintenance, and wages. Put your cost beside your capacity. Publicly.”

Ulan leaned back. “You released an audit woman and discovered invoices.”

“I was born with invoices.”

The room loosened by half a degree.

On Day 14, the cargo unions built the first mass budget. It began with prepared matter because branch matter could not arrive for free. Empty habitat volume offered location, not substance. Every person required a released exclusion margin matched to material at reception: water, carbon stock, construction feed, stored food, and nonliving ballast certified clean enough for controlled instantiation.

Rhen rejected three versions before breakfast.

One counted active cargo already assigned to current-system clinics. Another treated carrier reaction mass as available matter, leaving no way to move after reception. A third assumed mining tailings could arrive at Orison through routes that would close during synchronized operations.

“Count matter where it will physically sit,” Rhen told Pell. “Do not spend it at origin and destination.”

Pell rubbed both eyes. “That removes forty percent.”

“It removes an imaginary forty percent.”

They marked every tonne by custody. Orison held structural feed. Kest unions offered agricultural carbon after protecting four harvest cycles. Sable habitats listed radiation shielding, though only if their local councils approved. Carriers identified ballast they could discharge without compromising return legs. Nothing entered the total until an engineer and a cargo representative signed the same line.

By local midnight, the verified mass budget supported reception for 112,000 people across five sites. Against Kest-Blue’s population, the figure looked insulting.

Before anyone published it, Rhen ordered a physical loading test. Spreadsheets made tonnes obedient. Cargo decks had more particular opinions.

Orison crews brought three blocks of certified structural feed through the berth lock, each wrapped in impact mesh and marked for a hypothetical reception sector. Rhen assigned one block to Morrow Glass, one to Kindly Weight, and one to the station reserve. The first crane lift exposed an error before the block cleared its cradle.

“Stop,” Rhen called.

The loadmaster froze the hoist. A thin red line on the deck display showed the carrier’s center of mass moving outside arrival tolerance.

Pell leaned toward the console. “The total remains under your ballast allowance.”

“Total does not tell me where it sits.” Rhen walked him aft and tapped the truss diagram. Medical refrigeration occupied the balanced socket their planning sheet had assigned to feedstock. Moving the block forward would place it beside a thermal line that could not tolerate instantiation cooling loads. Moving medicine would violate the protected cargo rule.

“Then the carrier contribution drops by four hundred tonnes,” Pell said.

“Or we change the frame.”

Two riggers fetched an unused lattice cradle from Orison storage. It had been manufactured for habitat glass, abandoned after the third expansion delay, and left with inspection seals intact. They spent ninety minutes fitting adapters to Morrow Glass’s scarred outer truss. Nothing about the work resembled a rescue poster. Bolts resisted. One adapter arrived three millimeters short. A rigger named Vesh filed the mating surface by hand because sending it back through fabrication would lose the test window.

Talia watched from behind the deck safety line. “How much of the public plan depends on solving problems this small?”

“All of it,” Rhen said.

The second lift seated cleanly. Mass distribution settled within tolerance, but the truss sensors reported a harmonic at crossing frequency. Rhen refused the load again. Vesh looked ready to throw the file.

“We carry heavier blocks every month,” she said.

“Not while arrival geometry tries to treat that block as available margin.”

Fal called an Orison structural engineer. The answer required a local walk from the far arc because station communication had failed in two unfinished sectors. When the engineer arrived, she found a brace omitted from the old cradle’s certification drawing. Installation took another hour and consumed six kilograms the mass budget had not listed.

Pell opened the planning sheet. “Do you want six kilograms removed from reception matter?”

“I want support hardware entered as operational mass.”

“That level of accounting will bury us.”

“That level keeps the block attached.”

They revised every carrier line to include cradles, hoses, thermal barriers, restraint crews, and return reserve. The verified total fell to 106,400 people. Mora stared at the reduction as if honesty had performed an injury.

Vesh signed the loading test with grease across one thumb. “Run it again under emergency power.”

They cut normal deck service. Backup motors lifted the block at half speed. Lamps dimmed along the truss. This time the cradle held without harmonic drift, and the carrier retained enough reserve to separate from a damaged berth.

Rhen entered the test beside the compact. Empty rooms now had a route from certified matter to a physically balanced deck. The number had grown smaller and more useful.

Mora wanted it withheld.

“Publish,” Rhen said.

“It tells nine million people we found room for one in eighty-four.”

“It tells them where the next room comes from.”

Talia stood over the projection. “It also prevents officials from treating nine million as a single impossible unit.”

Pell sent the budget during an outbound Kest window. A return answer could not arrive until another physical transit opened. The public would argue for hours before the planners heard any result.

Work continued without applause.

Day 15 began with survival cargo. Rumor after the Final Fall order had pushed captains away from Pelagos-linked routes and overloaded the direct Kest lanes. Morrow Glass carried filter rolls from Orison to Lorn Reach, then took immune seed toward a feeder exchange. Talia worked in the galley with three union schedulers while Rhen handled the crossing and cargo shift.

At Lorn Reach, a dock coordinator tried to divert two filter pallets into reception storage.

“Those are clinic allocation,” Rhen said.

“They would support two hundred branch arrivals.”

“They support six thousand current residents now.”

“You claim to be building rescue.”

“I claim empty rooms need honest supply.” Rhen locked the pallets under the original manifest. “Stealing from a clinic makes the rooms emptier.”

The coordinator called them compromised. Rhen accepted the word and watched the filters enter the clinic tug.

A packet from Kest arrived during departure commitment. Public reaction to the mass budget had split along predictable wounds. One civic assembly demanded every habitat list empty volume. Two gate families called the compact a disguised closure campaign. Kest-Blue’s neighborhood council sent a delayed statement through Pelagos: Do not reserve beds by status, profession, or resemblance to present citizens. Nemi Ren had signed beneath the council seal.

Rhen read the line twice. “They think we are going to choose useful people.”

Talia looked up from the scheduler table. “Some planners are already proposing it.”

“Engineers first?”

“Children first. Medical cases first. Families of verified witnesses first. Every category sounds humane until it becomes a border.”

Rhen thought of cargo priority. Medicine before furniture. Food before decorative metal. People resisted such ordering because no person belonged in a crate class, yet a reception operation would still face limited air, mass, and time.

“We need a compact that commits capacity without pretending to own the list,” they said.

During the Lorn Reach crossing, Rhen dictated six rules. No carrier could claim a passenger allocation before a joint civic process existed. Current survival cargo retained protected status. Costs and donated capacity remained public. Empty volume did not count without air, water, heat, and crew. Prepared mass could not be counted twice. Captains could withdraw before final commitment, but every withdrawal had to enter the same public ledger as every promise.

Talia added a seventh. “No reception plan may depend on overwriting current inhabited matter.”

“That seems obvious.”

“So did preserving the only world we could see.”

Rhen kept it.

They called the document the Empty Rooms compact because Mora’s proposed title, Accordant Humanitarian Reception Framework, sounded like a committee trying not to touch a bunk.

Signatures came slowly.

Jes Aro signed at once. Ulan Per demanded wage guarantees and a limit on Ministry requisition. The Kest food captains waited until their union confirmed protected cargo floors. Two inner-system courier owners offered speed but almost no mass; Rhen accepted their relay capacity instead. A water hauler from Sable refused public disclosure of maintenance debt, then changed her mind after her crew published it without her.

Each signature had to cross during an open transit window. By Day 16, Rhen carried confirmations with timestamps scattered across twenty-one hours. Some captains had signed before seeing later amendments. The legal slate marked their assent conditional until the revised compact reached them and came back.

At Orison, workers rolled shielding into the first residential arc. Heat crews replaced the flawed radiator manifold. The station’s occupancy ceiling rose from seven thousand to eight thousand four hundred, assuming filter deliveries held. Rhen trusted that figure more than the original three hundred thousand because people had bruised their hands making it true.

The final packet window opened at 19:40.

Morrow Glass sat docked beside Kindly Weight while captains crowded its galley, corridor, and command channel. Others appeared as recordings queued from distant gates. No one could answer across a closed route, so Rhen read each final condition aloud before accepting the signature.

“Protected food floor remains binding.”

The Kest captains confirmed in their recorded response.

“Crew wages enter reception cost before charitable credit.”

Ulan’s signature validated.

“No captain controls selection.”

Jes pressed her key to the slate.

Mora watched the count rise. Nineteen. Twenty. Twenty-one.

The penultimate packet came from a mining carrier with only forty pressure couches and eighteen hundred tonnes of certified feedstock. Its captain apologized for offering so few rooms. Rhen entered the mass first and the apology nowhere.

Twenty-two.

Static crossed the last open channel as the window approached closure. A packet from Sable had failed its first relay and arrived with three seconds remaining. Talia caught the header, verified its civic seal, and pushed it into local custody before the route shut.

The galley fell quiet. Across the table, the compact showed twenty-three captains, each attached to real cabins, real costs, and matter located on an actual deck.

Rhen signed last as carrier host.

Outside, Orison’s unfinished ring turned through the white light. A row of residential windows brightened along one completed arc, not because anyone had arrived, but because workers had connected the repaired heat loop and needed to test every room under load.

The Empty Rooms compact entered the public queue with twenty-three signatures and no promise larger than its air.

Chapter 17 — The Overwrite Problem

Maelin’s first rescue model gave Isca her kitchen back.

The image formed inside Pelagos control at 04:11, after six failed runs and one field collapse that burned out a sensor petal. Morning light crossed the floor tiles in her Kest-Blue apartment. Nemi’s yellow cup sat beside the sink. Aru’s school coat hung from the wrong hook because he never used the lower one assigned to him. Through the window, cloud forest climbed the northern ridge under a rain front Isca remembered forecasting.

No static crossed the walls. No second reflections disagreed with the room. The apartment held depth, weather, and ordinary untidiness with such precision that Isca reached toward the table before she saw current Kest underneath it.

A buried-town ventilation shaft occupied the same coordinates as her sink.

“Stop the run,” she said.

Maelin did not. She stood at the receiver console with one hand braced against its damaged rim, watching two matter solutions converge. Her gray hair had escaped its tie. A red field warning painted her face without changing her expression.

“Maelin.”

“I heard you.”

“Then stop.”

“I need the boundary.”

The kitchen brightened. Its southern wall passed through three meters of current-Kest shelter concrete. Aru’s coat became transparent where a water pipe crossed it. Outside, the cloud forest and the ashland ridge occupied one terrain solution in alternating sheets.

A human figure appeared near the apartment door.

Isca struck the emergency break.

Pelagos control lost every projected surface at once. Dark instrument glass replaced the kitchen. The seven exclusion petals around the reserve star dropped into safe alignment. Cooling pumps surged beneath the floor. Maelin’s console emitted a tone usually reserved for uncommitted transit mass.

She looked at Isca’s hand on the break. “Another nine seconds would have shown whether the conflict resolves at cellular scale.”

“It showed enough at a wall.”

“We cannot infer a person from architecture.”

“You put someone in the door.”

“A low-detail boundary probe.”

“It had Nemi’s height.”

Maelin removed her hand from the console. “Yes.”

Isca wanted anger to simplify her. Her carelessness with her body, the unfastened collar, and the burn scar left untreated across one wrist all made her look capable of treating anyone as material. Yet she had spent years building the receiver that allowed Nemi to speak. She had hidden records at the cost of her career. Moral repair had not cured her appetite for one more measurement.

“Explain what you expected,” she said.

“I expected incompatible matter to reject at the local boundary. We could route around inhabited structures and instantiate only branch citizens in prepared zones.”

“What happened?”

Maelin restored the run as a static comparison. Her apartment returned beside a map of current northern Kest. “The branch arrives as a coherent physical history. Its local states depend on surrounding matter. Remove your wall, and the field tries to restore the support behind it. Remove the ridge, and weather records no longer match the living systems they shaped.”

“So it brings everything.”

“Not everything. It selects enough compatible matter to sustain continuity.”

The distinction made her want to break the screen. “Whose continuity?”

She enlarged the buried town. Population tags appeared beneath her street. Current families slept in chambers carved after the AY 61 coronal storm killed the cloud forests. Kest-Blue had no chambers there. Its open city needed none.

Maelin initiated a diagnostic pass using nonliving matter only. The model laid her apartment block across the shelter district. Where solutions conflicted, current concrete vanished from the output. Branch soil replaced sealed corridors. Water tanks became root systems. No explosion marked the exchange. Replacement looked clean because the rejected history never remained to display damage.

“The model preserves the incoming branch,” she said. “Current matter supplies the released margin.”

Isca searched the output for the buried-town tags. “Where do these people go?”

“They do not enter the selected solution.”

“Say the word you used in the private notes.”

Maelin’s jaw moved once. “Overwrite.”

Pelagos had spent eleven years receiving exclusions from crossings elsewhere. Now its instruments proposed sending Kest-Blue back by excluding the people who had benefited from those crossings. Symmetry made an elegant diagram. It did not make a moral answer.

“How fast?” Isca asked.

“If we hold phase reference, a full Kest substitution could complete inside two transit cycles.”

“Days?”

“Three, perhaps four.”

Nemi and Aru could stand under a solid sky before the end of the week.

The thought arrived complete, indecent in its hope. Isca saw Aru running up the apartment stairs, furious that his school coat still hung from the wrong hook. She saw Nemi sleeping without a relay alarm beside the bed. Her body answered before judgment did: breath shortened, hands warmed, knees weakened as if home exerted gravity through the screen.

Maelin watched the diagnostic instead of her. That restraint let her suspect she understood the temptation.

“What happens to current Kest?” she asked.

“The selected branch occupies its compatible matter.”

“You have said that.”

“Current biological structures with no branch-compatible continuation fall outside the solution.”

“Nine million of us arrive by making how many disappear?”

She opened the present census. Current Kest held more than thirty-eight million residents, including buried northern cities and coastal settlements shared in different forms by both histories. Compatibility confidence varied. The model could not promise which current citizens might map into similar branch lives and which would simply end. Even a compatible body would not preserve the present person’s continuous experience.

“Stop calling any of them compatible,” Isca said.

“It describes matter relation.”

“It invites you to count resemblance as survival.”

Maelin closed the census pane. “Agreed.”

No defense. No argument. Her agreement left the choice with Isca.

Isca did not trust agreement obtained through a diagram. “Run a live boundary test.”

“We have no branch organism in storage.”

“You have my clothing.”

The sealed evidence cabinet still held pollen from three Kest-Blue plants, collected from the shirt she wore during materialization. Maelin objected to consuming any sample before a full rescue protocol existed. Isca overrode her under the same protected-person authority Talia had used to expose the ledger.

They chose one cloud-fern pollen grain and a patch of current-Kest lichen grown on an inspection tile. Both descended from northern species separated by the AY 61 storm. Neither organism carried a nervous system. No test subject could confuse replacement with pain, which made the result easier to observe and harder to excuse.

Maelin placed the tile inside a palm-sized field cradle. “This cannot model person continuity.”

“It can model your verbs.”

She fed the pollen’s measured state into the rescue solution and assigned the lichen tile as compatible matter. At one percent coherence, the pollen formed a translucent shell over the living patch. Its proteins drew carbon from the lichen’s outer cells. Instrument labels described the exchange as margin release.

“Hold there,” Isca said.

The field stabilized. Under magnification, lichen cells nearest the pollen had lost membrane structure. Their chemical constituents persisted, rearranged into the branch organism’s incomplete envelope.

“Can you restore them if we reverse?”

“Possibly at this stage.”

“Possibly belongs in a report. Reverse it.”

Maelin reduced coherence. The pollen shell collapsed. Most lichen cells recovered their prior shape, but three remained chemically intact without resuming metabolism. A yellow boundary appeared around them.

DEAD STATE, the instrument reported.

Isca wrote the words into the run log before automatic summary could rename them.

A second pass raised branch coherence to six percent. The cloud-fern grain germinated. Its first root extended through the lichen patch, using current proteins as feed. When Maelin reversed the field, no prior lichen pattern remained under the root. Reconstruction software offered a statistical approximation from neighboring cells.

“That would look like recovery on a low-resolution scan,” she said.

“Would it continue the same organism?”

“No.”

“Mark the difference.”

She added a red continuity break across the reconstructed region.

For the final pass, Isca moved the pollen to a sterile carbon wafer beside the lichen. Prepared matter had no living pattern to defend. The grain formed, rooted, and consumed the wafer’s surface without touching the adjacent organism. Field coherence remained weaker because the wafer supplied no matching local history. Yet both life signs continued.

Maelin increased support. Power demand tripled. The receiver’s cooling loop approached its safe limit. A full city attempted this way would require mass distributed across many prepared sites plus phase support beyond Pelagos.

“Worse efficiency,” she said.

“Better survival.”

“The network cannot supply this at population scale under present routing.”

“Present routing creates the margin by throwing something else away.” Isca sealed the lichen tile and branch root inside separate sample fields. “Every future model carries this control. One occupied target, one prepared target. Report both continuity outcomes before you report speed.”

Maelin attached her protocol to the rescue file. Her first estimate changed from three days to unavailable.

A field carrier rose from the receiver. Kest-Blue requested civic contact. Pelagos could support a visual channel while the petals remained in safe alignment, though delay had widened after the diagnostic run. The console predicted between two and five minutes each direction.

Isca opened the channel.

Nemi did not appear. A neighborhood council chamber resolved instead, crowded beyond its wall markings. Isca recognized the school gym where Nemi organized plague supply during the southern closures. Adults filled the bleachers. Children sat on the floor under emergency lamps. A weather clock above the door showed six hours later than Pelagos local time.

Councilor Veyan stood at the camera. “We received the successful interior image.”

Maelin muttered, “Successful has escaped its enclosure.”

Isca leaned toward the pickup. “The image concealed a matter conflict. Do not circulate it without the current-Kest layer.”

Her warning entered the field. While they waited for its arrival, the chamber continued in the past. Veyan described rumors already moving through Kest-Blue: an intact apartment, a full northern district, a rapid return. People behind him held printed stills from Maelin’s run. Someone had enlarged the kitchen window until the forest filled the page.

Aru had eaten breakfast in that room.

The delayed reply reached the council. Veyan’s expression changed. He listened to someone outside frame, then faced the camera again.

“What conflict?” she asked.

Isca sent the overlay. “The procedure replaces occupied matter. It cannot limit restoration to empty regions. Current Kest’s buried towns intersect our northern cities.”

A woman came down from the bleachers and took the council microphone. She gave her name as Teren Mal, a transit medic from the southern coast. “Current Kest has spent one hundred forty-three years purchasing its continuity with ours. Why does their occupancy veto our return?”

The question crossed to Pelagos and waited inside Isca for an answer.

“Because the people there did not choose the Ministry’s accounting,” she said at last. “They are not reserve load. We cannot make them into it.”

Again the field carried her words away without immediate consequence.

Maelin opened the receiver’s stability report. The diagnostic had shortened Pelagos’s safe coherence estimate by eleven hours. She highlighted the loss but did not speak.

In the council chamber, Teren continued before hearing Isca. “My brother vanished from the south clinic yesterday. His room lost phase between one breath and the next. We have weeks if the Ministry leaves us alone, less if Final Fall begins. How much patience do you ask from people disappearing while another Kest keeps our ground?”

Behind her, the crowd reacted in separate waves. Some demanded the model. Others called for Nemi. A man near the back shouted that Isca had become attached to her custodians. The field clipped the next sentence and restored only its end: easy to defend strangers when you are already solid.

That reached her cleanly.

Isca looked down at her hands. Pelagos light reflected from skin that no longer lost phase when the receiver fluctuated. She slept in a real bunk. She ate current-system food. Every refusal she made occurred from the safe side of the glass.

Teren received Isca’s answer. “Choice did not protect us either,” she said. “Their gates made us. Their secrecy kept making us. Why must innocence count only in the present?”

“It does not.” Isca recorded the reply knowing Teren would wait minutes to hear it. “Your brother counts. My family counts. Current children sleeping above the coordinates of my home count. If the only operation saves us by removing them, the operation extends the same crime with custody reversed.”

Veyan asked whether Isca had another method.

She had none.

The channel delay forced that absence to remain visible. No quick argument covered it. Isca stood at the console while the council watched an earlier version of her face. Pelagos pumps cycled. A second reflection moved across the black instrument petals, showing the gym crowded under a different angle of light.

Nemi entered the council chamber at last. She wore her emergency-teacher vest and carried Aru asleep against one shoulder. He had grown too tall for that carry, but exhaustion had folded him around her. Nemi did not approach the microphone until Teren yielded it.

“Isca,” she said. “Show us what happens to a person.”

The request arrived three minutes later.

Maelin shook her head. “We have boundary inference, not a biological run.”

“Then show the inference.”

“It will look painless.”

“That makes it more dangerous to hide.”

She loaded two anonymized body maps. One came from Kest-Blue’s civic medical archive. The other represented a living current-Kest resident at the same coordinates with no equivalent branch history. The model began at low resolution. Bone states conflicted. Blood chemistry diverged. Neural patterns shared no viable mapping. As branch coherence rose, current markers dropped from the selected output one organ system at a time.

Nothing screamed. No sensor retained a subject who could scream after exclusion.

Maelin sent the run with its uncertainty labels intact.

Nemi watched the result in the council’s delayed present. Aru woke against her shoulder and asked a question too softly for Pelagos to receive. She answered him before returning to the camera.

“You told them no,” Nemi said.

“I am telling all of us no.”

“We may lose districts while you look for better.”

“I know.”

“You do not know from here anymore.”

Isca took the blow because it came from the person entitled to deliver it.

When her reply reached Kest-Blue, she said, “Then do not let me decide alone. Publish the full model. Hold a civic vote on whether to reject overwrite. But no current-system authority gets to offer it as rescue, and no branch official gets to conceal its cost.”

Veyan conferred with the council. Teren returned to the bleachers. Anger did not dissolve. Several people left before the next transmission, carrying their kitchen images with them.

Nemi remained at the microphone. “Aru wants to know if our apartment still has his coat.”

“The model remembered the upper hook.”

“He says that means it remembered wrong. He put it there on purpose.”

Isca laughed once and covered her mouth. The sound hurt more than crying would have.

Field loss crossed the channel. Nemi’s image divided at the shoulders, restored, then froze. A delay notice climbed from three minutes to five.

Maelin lowered transmission quality to preserve voice. “I can keep this open for another exchange.”

Isca looked at the apartment still beside the body maps. The model offered a perfect room by treating everyone outside its remembered continuity as removable support. She deleted the still from the public summary but preserved it in evidence with both overlays attached.

“What else do we have?” she asked.

“Fragments. Habitat instantiation works at small scale if prepared matter has no competing biological state. The field can place an isolated tissue pattern inside certified feed.”

“Scale?”

“Grams.”

“People require more than grams.”

“So do cities.”

Isca opened the public planning packet that had arrived from Orison during the previous transit window. Empty Rooms listed pressure volume, prepared mass, air, heat, and twenty-three carrier commitments. The plan could receive only a fraction of Kest-Blue, yet it offered one property Maelin’s fast model lacked: no one currently lived inside the matter assigned to arrival.

“Run one person across multiple reception sites,” she said.

“That destroys local coherence.”

“Do not divide a person. Divide the operation.”

Maelin turned toward her.

“Stop asking one world to supply every compatible state,” Isca continued. “Use prepared matter at Orison, empty habitats, underused worlds, carrier feedstock. Restore neighborhoods as distributed structures. Keep families together where possible, but do not make geography sacred enough to kill for it.”

“Every gate would need to preserve its own excluded branch during routing. Otherwise the operation frees Kest-Blue by sending new displacement into another reserve.”

“Then every gate participates.”

“Forty-seven independent gate authorities do not synchronize because we need them to.”

“Make the need measurable.”

Maelin brought up exclusion ledgers from the public release. “Rotating preserved margin across the network might hold coherence, but capacity would collapse. Half, perhaps worse. Transit windows would require reciprocal commitments rather than one-sided dumping.”

“Can it avoid replacement?”

“I do not know.”

“Find out.”

The field channel recovered long enough to carry Nemi’s last response. Her voice arrived without image.

“The council will vote on rejecting overwrite,” she said. “People will hate you for asking. I may hate you during part of it. Do not mistake that for permission to stop.”

Aru spoke close to the pickup. “Bring the coat too.”

Then Kest-Blue dropped into static.

Isca stood with the lost voice until Maelin closed the carrier. Through Pelagos control glass, the red dwarf hung behind seven black petals. Final Fall calibration lights moved along their rims in cold increments.

She entered her rejection into the rescue file under her own name, attached the body-map run, and gave Maelin a new requirement rather than a consolation.

DISTRIBUTED SOLUTION. NO OVERWRITE OF CURRENT KEST. PRESERVE PERSON CONTINUITY AT PREPARED SITES.

Maelin read it, opened a clean model, and divided the network into forty-seven gates.

Chapter 18 — Reciprocal Routing

The first distributed rescue attempt killed a square of lichen and moved the harm to Pelagos.

Talia watched both failures occur on separate instruments. At Orison, a camera showed branch cloud-fern tissue forming on a sterile carbon tile beside a current-Kest lichen patch. The organisms never touched. At Pelagos, exclusion load climbed across the receiver petals as the gate discarded the unselected arrival state into its usual reserve.

“Abort Orison,” she said.

Her order entered the packet queue. No route window stood open. Light-speed relay between Pelagos and Orison would arrive too late to matter, and gate relays carried narrow packets only during physical transit preparation.

Maelin reached past her and broke the local phase reference.

The branch tissue at Orison vanished. Its carbon tile remained sterile. Pelagos load dropped, but not before the receiver lost one coherent strip of the Kest-Blue field. A district census in the southern branch flickered by thirty-one citizens, restored twenty-eight, and left three unresolved.

Talia marked them as people, not signal loss.

Across Pelagos control, warning lamps crawled along the seven black petals. Final Fall crews had installed new decoherence charges under Council authority. Sal’s security officers guarded the access spine outside, allowing Maelin to work only because the order still granted a technical review period. Every failed attempt consumed part of that period and part of Kest-Blue.

Maelin replayed the Orison test. “Prepared matter solved the occupied-target conflict. Ordinary exclusion recreated it at reserve scale.”

“The gate selected a branch arrival and dumped the no-arrival solution here.”

“Yes.”

“Then prepared matter changes who arrives, not what transit discards.”

“You wanted consequence before mechanism.”

“I wanted the consequence to remain a tile.”

A delayed packet arrived from Orison. The gatekeeper’s response had been recorded before Talia ordered the abort. SUCCESSFUL MICRO-INSTANTIATION. REQUEST SCALE PARAMETERS.

She returned the complete Pelagos loss report during the next transit commitment. Until Orison received it, the remote team would believe the test had succeeded.

Talia opened a clean incident tree. Observable effect: branch tissue formed at a prepared site. Attempted procedure: ordinary arrival selection using prepared carbon. Measured contradiction: reserve exclusion increased by the same margin. Interpretation: the rescue path preserved local life by spending remote coherence.

The tree left no place for the word rescue.

Maelin drew forty-seven gate symbols around the central display. “Every crossing prepares two destination histories. Current practice selects one, discards one, and sends the displaced information to a reserve. We need the nonselected state to remain available after commitment.”

“Show me an attempt.”

She chose Pelagos, Orison, and an unused maintenance gate at Cinder. Pelagos could not perform ordinary inhabited transit, but its field array could imitate a gate commitment at microscopic mass. Orison held the carbon tile. Cinder’s gate could receive a rotating exclusion margin without affecting a population.

Attempt two began during an open construction-cargo window on Day 18.

A gram of certified carbon sat at Orison. Pelagos held the cloud-fern pattern. Cinder accepted the nonselected Orison state for twelve milliseconds, then handed it back after branch tissue formed. The plan treated exclusion like a borrowed tool: move it away during commitment, return it before the reserve accumulated load.

At first, every trace stayed green.

The branch root appeared on Orison’s tile. Cinder registered a temporary veil. Pelagos coherence held.

Then the cargo carrier completed arrival.

Cinder tried to return the preserved state through a window that no longer existed. Its gate had only enough local field memory to hold the margin for forty milliseconds. At forty-one, protection collapsed. The exclusion followed the oldest available reserve assignment and struck Pelagos in a sharp pulse.

A control relay cracked. Cooling vapor filled the lower console trench. Maelin dropped to one knee and pulled a manual isolation bar before Talia reached her. Pelagos lost no verified citizens, but the southern district dimmed for four seconds.

“Your borrowed tool missed its return transport,” Talia said.

Maelin examined the burned relay. “Sequential routing requires a window to remain open after arrival. That doubles exposure time and still fails when the next gate closes early.”

Cinder’s gatekeeper sent an angry demand for disposal instructions. The preserved margin had left a residual state in his maintenance buffers, reducing the gate’s next safe cargo allowance by six percent. Talia issued a hold on Cinder transit until engineers verified full release.

One feeder shipment missed its connection because of that hold.

Rhen’s recorded message arrived three hours later through a Kest window. “Your microscopic test has delayed two tonnes of antifungal substrate. Lorn Reach can absorb six hours. After that, tell me whether to charter a courier.”

Talia added the substrate to the incident tree. Technical alternatives did not receive moral exemption while they remained alternatives.

She and Maelin slept in shifts. Talia took the floor beside the main display for eighty-three minutes and woke to a sheet placed over her by someone who denied having done it. Maelin had built a third configuration while she slept.

Instead of moving exclusion after selection, she paired two selections at the same commitment boundary. Orison would form branch tissue from prepared carbon. Cinder would select its own unchanged local state while preserving Orison’s nonselected state inside the reciprocal field. At the next commitment, Orison would preserve Cinder’s unused alternative. Neither gate could dump the other’s margin because each crossing remained contingent on the partner retaining it.

“Mutual custody,” Talia said.

“Mutual obstruction, if one side defects.”

“What happens when Cinder refuses the second commitment?”

“Orison cannot safely route again. Its preserved margin becomes part of the next arrival pair.”

“So one gate can freeze another.”

“One gate can prevent another from externalizing its cost.”

Talia revised the incident tree. Interpretation: reciprocity converted hidden disposal into visible dependency. That did not make the system kind. It made refusal expensive to the refuser and partner alike.

Attempt three used no branch tissue. Talia required two inert patterns with distinct isotope marks. Cinder held a carbon grain labeled red. Orison held one labeled blue. Each gate prepared an arrival with the other grain and a nonarrival retaining its local grain. At a synchronized count, both selected arrival while preserving the excluded alternatives inside paired buffers.

The first count failed before commitment.

Orison’s clock reached zero nineteen microseconds ahead of Cinder. Automatic safety rejected both selections. No matter moved. A construction carrier waiting behind the test lost its window and had to remain in origin geometry until crews bled the field.

Fal Iven’s delayed complaint arrived as text: HABITAT HEAT MANIFOLD ABOARD THAT CARRIER.

“Capacity cost,” Talia said.

Maelin reset the clocks using Morrow Glass’s preserved raw arrival data as a common physical reference. Rhen had sent a copy from the carrier’s isolated clock, not through cabinet normalization. Its eleven-second fracture provided two traces from one event, precise enough to expose timing errors gate instruments usually averaged away.

The second count reached commitment.

Red carbon appeared at Orison. Blue arrived at Cinder. Both local grains remained present. Exclusion buffers held the nonselected states instead of forwarding them to Pelagos.

For sixty milliseconds, four valid material records existed across two gates without four physical grains. The buffers preserved information, not duplicate matter. Each site could account for one present grain and one protected alternative.

Then Orison opened its next test arrival. Cinder reciprocated. The buffered alternatives rotated into the new arrival pairs, releasing the prior commitment without reserve discharge.

Pelagos load did not move.

Success lasted until Talia asked for a third route.

“Send Orison’s next arrival toward a different destination,” she said.

Maelin kept red and blue on the display. “The pair already preserves both alternatives.”

“The Concord does not run one shuttle between two gates. Orison sends food to Lorn Reach, receives machinery from Arclight, and takes habitat modules from Kest. Change its partner.”

She added a simulated Lorn Reach gate using live clock behavior from the public archive. No matter would move there. Its emulator could still show whether a preserved state survived a partner change.

Cycle six began with Orison carrying Cinder’s unused blue alternative. Cinder held Orison’s red. When Orison prepared a new arrival pair with simulated Lorn Reach, its buffer now contained three obligations: local carbon, incoming test matter, and the prior Cinder state. The gate found compatible geometry but lacked a certified recipient for Cinder’s alternative.

Automatic routing proposed Pelagos.

Talia canceled before selection. “That path returns to disposal.”

“Then Cinder must follow Orison into every later pairing.”

“Which turns two independent gates into one route.”

Maelin tried to transfer Cinder’s preserved alternative to Lorn Reach before Orison committed. The emulator accepted the record, but Cinder lost proof of custody during the handoff. For seven microseconds, neither gate could demonstrate preservation. Orison’s safety layer rejected its arrival.

On a live route, a food carrier would have remained at origin.

“Overlap custody,” Talia said. “Cinder retains proof until Lorn Reach confirms receipt.”

“That creates a brief duplicate record.”

“A record can have two custodians. Matter still occupies one state.”

She rewrote the handoff. Cinder held its buffer while Lorn Reach received a verified image. Orison waited. Once both custodians agreed on the same alternative, Cinder released its copy and Orison committed with Lorn Reach.

The route completed without Pelagos load.

Talia added a fourth gate, then a fifth. Each partner change required a preserved-state handoff before matter selection. The buffers did not move as one great burden. They rotated among gates whose upcoming arrivals offered compatible storage. A gate with no transit still held its last obligation. A busy gate carried more handoffs and therefore surrendered more capacity.

“Now remove Arclight,” she said.

Maelin marked the simulated inner-system gate unavailable.

Three preserved states had expected Arclight custody during the next cycle. Their source gates refused commitment. Two cargo movements halted before arrival geometry formed. A medical courier between Kest and Lorn Reach continued because its reciprocal proof used Orison instead.

“No cascade,” Talia said.

“A partial stop.”

“Keep removing gates.”

At thirty-nine active participants, six outer systems could not find reciprocal custody inside their scheduled windows. Food routes froze. At twenty-eight, the network separated into local clusters whose buffers could circulate internally but could not support branch instantiation across all prepared sites. Every active cluster avoided reserve discharge, yet Kest-Blue’s arrival patterns lost common phase between reception zones.

Maelin restored all forty-seven inhabited gates in the simulation.

Preserved states rotated around the Concord. No single gate held every exclusion. Partner failure stopped only routes dependent on that custody path until schedulers found another. Distributed branch patterns retained one common reference because each gate proved its obligation against the same commitment clock.

“Seven reserves?” Talia asked.

“Monitoring and emergency field support. No routine disposal.”

“Could forty-six gates perform Kest-Blue instantiation?”

Maelin removed the smallest outer gate. Reception patterns reaching its system remained coherent elsewhere, but the missing gate’s ordinary crossings continued to produce exclusion unless shut down completely. If it stayed open under old rules, Pelagos began accumulating load again. If it closed, that system lost food export and medical import during the operation.

“So participation means reciprocal routing or shutdown,” Talia said.

“For the synchronized period, yes.”

“That condition goes public.”

She did not object.

They ran a schedule using the actual previous week of Concord traffic. The protocol rejected forty-eight percent of crossings before commitment. No carrier entered a bad arrival. No reserve received hidden margin. Delays struck unevenly: inner manufacturers lost redundant courier windows, while outer food systems lost fewer but longer routes. One Kest clinic shipment arrived eighteen hours late in the model despite priority status.

Talia called Rhen during the next available local link to Morrow Glass, then waited while the carrier captain examined the schedule. Rhen’s response came back in the same open window with seconds to spare.

“Eighteen hours spoils this culture,” they said. “Pair it through Orison instead of Arclight. You lose a construction module and save the medicine.”

Maelin changed the custody path. The culture arrived inside tolerance. Orison’s module slipped two days.

On the habitat camera, workers stood beside an empty radiator cradle waiting for that module. Talia attached the image to the routing choice. Every recovered shipment created another visible delay. The method supplied no clean column where planners could hide inconvenience.

“Run one defection after matter commitment,” she said.

Maelin made Cinder drop its buffer proof midway through an Orison cycle. Reciprocal locks prevented either gate from selecting arrival. The physical carrier remained at origin, but both fields retained enough stress to consume their next windows. Recovery took six hours and required public allocation of replacement cooling capacity.

“If locks failed?”

“Then one gate could complete by exporting exclusion to Pelagos.”

“Remove that fallback from the code.”

“Operators will demand an emergency path.”

“Give them abort, delay, and local shutdown. Do not label disposal an emergency.”

She deleted the reserve fallback. The next simulated defection stopped two gates and spared the unselected state.

Maelin ran the result again. Talia made her disconnect the Pelagos feed entirely. Red and blue crossed in four cycles, preserving each unused state through the paired gate rather than hiding it in the reserve.

On the fifth cycle, Cinder’s cooling limit forced a late commitment.

Orison detected the delay and refused selection. Both grains stayed where they were. Nothing vanished. Nothing arrived. The protocol chose missed transit over unaccounted exclusion.

Talia stared at the stable traces. “Name that refusal condition.”

“Reciprocal Routing requires proof of preserved alternative before either gate commits.”

“That describes the condition. Name the consequence.”

Maelin glanced at the waiting cargo schedules. “Capacity falls.”

They modeled a full day. Each ordinary crossing now required paired buffer space, a reciprocal gate commitment, and time to rotate the preserved state into another arrival pair. Gates could no longer use every compatible window. Safety margins widened. Cooling loads doubled during handoff. Under ideal coordination, the Concord retained fifty-five percent of present throughput. When they added maintenance variance and honest refusal, safe capacity settled near fifty-two percent during the first year.

Roughly half.

The figure went into the public model without euphemism.

By Day 19, the method had a name and enemies. Reciprocal Routing appeared in the civic archive beside the three failed attempts, the lichen loss, Cinder’s delayed substrate, and Orison’s missed heat manifold. Talia included every consequence because any protocol built through omission would inherit the Ministry before its first crossing.

Responses arrived only when routes opened. Inner-system gatekeepers objected first. Arclight called synchronized custody an unlawful transfer of sovereign control. Two manufacturing systems warned that half capacity would close medical fabrication lines. Outer food systems demanded priority guarantees before testing. Seven gatekeepers refused to place local buffers under a protocol designed by a dismissed auditor and an unauthorized physicist.

The public ledger showed forty-seven gatekeepers because forty-seven inhabited systems held authority to commit their gates. Reserve systems could support testing, but they could not decide for populations whose food and medicine crossed the network.

Talia created a commitment matrix with no neutral column. Agree to test. Refuse. Request conditions. Silence. Every cell carried the next available packet window, so the absence of a reply could not be confused with a no.

Maelin read the first returns. “At this rate Pelagos fails before enough of them finish objecting.”

“Then reduce what they must trust.”

“They must trust synchronization.”

“They must verify it. Trust belongs to closed ledgers.”

They published the clock code, buffer proofs, abort conditions, and all raw runs. Any gate could reproduce the inert-grain exchange with a partner. No test required branch tissue. No commitment authorized later participation.

Still, no inhabited gatekeeper volunteered.

On Day 20, Final Fall technicians activated the first decoherence charge for a dry calibration. The receiver field tightened around Kest-Blue. Isca reported that visual delays had reached six minutes in the southern districts. A school clinic vanished from one reflection and came back missing its supply room.

Talia watched the charge’s timing trace overlap the empty commitment matrix.

“Can we test without an inhabited gate?” she asked.

“We already did.”

“Cinder lacks the traffic constraints they fear. We need a micro-transfer on a working gate with cargo waiting behind it.”

“That gatekeeper risks a missed schedule, buffer contamination, and prosecution under the Final Fall order.”

“Yes.”

Maelin gave her a look that returned sixteen years of Jun’s criticism in one line. “Your recruitment language needs polishing.”

A packet indicator flashed at Orison’s cell.

The message could not be current. No live route connected Pelagos and Orison. It had entered through a construction carrier that left Orison five hours earlier, crossed Kest, and forwarded its sealed payload during Kest’s Pelagos maintenance window. Talia verified each timestamp before opening it.

The sender identified herself as Gatekeeper Anja Vey, senior commitment operator at Orison. Fal Iven appeared beside her in the recording, still wearing pressure webbing. Behind them, workers moved heat-manifold crates from the delayed carrier.

“I have reviewed the failed runs,” Anja said. “Your first attempt exported harm. Your second left Cinder holding margin it could not return. Your third rejected transit when reciprocity failed. That rejection cost us nine hours on residential heat installation.”

She paused and looked off camera, perhaps at a local counsel, perhaps at a crew asking her not to continue.

“Our occupancy ceiling lost a day,” Anja said. “No one disappeared. I consider that difference operationally meaningful.”

Talia let out a breath she had not authorized herself to hold.

The recording continued. Orison would not commit passenger capacity, branch matter, or a permanent buffer. Anja offered one working gate, one sterile carbon wafer, and a paired test during an actual cargo schedule. She required a public abort authority shared between both gatekeepers. If either proof failed, the cargo would wait.

“Who pairs with her?” Maelin asked.

Talia checked the matrix. Forty-six cells remained red, conditional, or silent.

A second attachment answered. Anja had persuaded the gatekeeper at Kest’s agricultural South Anchor to receive the reciprocal proof, but not to move matter. Orison would perform the micro-transfer; Kest would hold the preserved alternative and return it during the next open window. The test remained asymmetric in matter and reciprocal in custody.

Maelin opened a new run sheet. “That can work if South Anchor commits within twelve microseconds.”

“If it misses?”

“Orison keeps its carbon. Cargo waits. Kest retains its local state.”

“No Pelagos discharge?”

“None.”

Talia entered the agreed abort condition and sent it back through the next maintenance packet. It would take hours to reach Orison. Confirmation would take hours more. No real-time conversation could save them from wording chosen now.

She wrote each obligation as if someone’s life would eventually depend on a gatekeeper reading it under alarm, because that outcome no longer felt hypothetical.

At 23:51, Orison’s commitment cell changed from conditional amber to test green. Forty-six others remained unresolved.

One inhabited gatekeeper had agreed to risk a micro-transfer, one cargo delay, and a public record of whatever broke.

Chapter 19 — The Keeper of the Present

Jun arrested Talia beside a carbon wafer smaller than her thumbnail.

Orison’s micro-transfer test glowed on the Pelagos control wall. One sterile grain had appeared at a working gate without adding exclusion load to the reserve. The result carried two gatekeeper seals, raw clock traces, an eight-hour cargo delay, and a public abort record. It proved less than its supporters claimed. It proved more than Jun could allow to remain operational while Final Fall crews worked under the same field.

Sal Varo read the custody order aloud.

“Under continuity law, emergency article twelve, Talia Sorn and Maelin Drave are remanded for interference with a protected transit system, unauthorized gate commitment, and material support of resistance to a lawful Council order.”

Talia stood at the main display with her hands visible. She wore a carrier crew token on a cord where her audit key once hung. Fatigue had narrowed her face without making it uncertain.

Maelin stayed seated. “Material support sounds flattering. We moved one grain.”

“You changed custody rules at two gates,” Jun said.

“Ah. Immaterial support, then.”

Sal’s officers approached her with restraints. She looked at their hands, then at the field console. “If you disconnect my left lead before closing the lower reference loop, Kest-Blue loses a district.”

Jun checked the instrument status. He spoke accurately.

“Give Commander Varo the shutdown sequence,” she said.

“There is no shutdown sequence while Final Fall charges hold calibration tension.”

“You designed both systems.”

“I designed the receiver. Your technicians attached weapons to it.”

Across the control glass, warning lights traced the seven black petals around the red dwarf. Jun had authorized dry calibration, not active decoherence. Yet the installed charges narrowed Maelin’s safe options and made her custody operationally difficult. Dependency always converted clean commands into negotiations with hardware.

Sal ordered one officer to take Maelin’s place at the reference console. The physicist dictated each transfer in clipped steps: close southern phase trim, verify district census, move left lead to automatic custody, wait through one full field oscillation. The officer repeated every instruction before touching anything.

Talia watched Jun rather than the console. “You came in person.”

“The next Arclight window would not carry sufficient authority.”

“It would carry an order.”

“It would not carry me.”

For sixteen years, Talia had understood that distinction as respect. Now suspicion entered her expression before any older loyalty could claim it.

The transfer completed. Kest-Blue’s census held. Sal secured Maelin’s wrists in front because her balance suffered after two days without proper sleep. Talia offered her hands before anyone asked.

Jun signed custody herself.

By 10:20, Pelagos control belonged to Final Fall technicians. They isolated the Reciprocal Routing code from operational systems and preserved a copy for evidence. Jun ordered all gate-test credentials revoked. She could prevent another live experiment under Ministry jurisdiction.

She could not withdraw the public model.

Thirty-seven civic mirrors held complete copies. Eleven system assemblies had entered it into emergency debate. Cargo unions had attached independent mass budgets. Gatekeepers were reproducing the inert-grain runs with local emulators that required no Ministry access. Every attempt to classify a mirror generated two more verification receipts.

Jun instructed legal staff to challenge operational use, not possession. Calling public arithmetic contraband would turn uncertainty into faith.

Talia and Maelin went into separate custody rooms on the station’s inner ring. Sal permitted paper, counsel packets during open relay windows, and recorded exercise. He denied Jun’s request to suspend Talia’s public correspondence because the arrest order named interference, not speech.

“You serve under my emergency command,” Jun told her.

“I serve the command as written.”

His answer should have reassured her. Instead, it exposed how much Jun wanted loyalty to fill gaps where law resisted speed.

On Day 21 evening, she brought Talia the Sable Famine archive.

The custody interview room had no exterior wall. Pelagos rotation supplied a weak downward pull that made the old paper files slide unless Jun set a hand on them. She had chosen paper because no display should summarize those nine days. The first folder held route closure notices. The second contained allocation calls from systems losing power, water treatment, temperature control, or food storage. The third listed deaths verified after routes reopened.

Talia sat across the narrow table without restraints. A camera recorded both of them.

“You released your eleven-year warning,” Jun said.

“I released my signature beneath the secrecy order too.”

“You signed because you accepted that unstable evidence could close routes.”

“I accepted ninety days of protected review.”

“You renewed twice.”

“Then objected.”

“Privately.”

Talia looked at the folders. “You did not bring me here to establish that I failed.”

“No. You have established it publicly.” Jun opened the first Sable file. “I brought the case you once understood.”

Day one of the closure showed three gates dark after a synchronization defect propagated through shared commitment software. The network safety board halted accordant transit. Officials predicted thirty-six hours. Jun, twenty-one and six weeks into a logistics appointment, received a temporary desk in a cargo annex because senior coordinators occupied emergency control.

She placed the original allocation sheet before Talia.

No eloquence survived its columns. Sable Two needed coolant catalyst for atmospheric pumps. Nareth requested insulin cultures. Orel’s orbital farms required nitrogen stock before their reserve tanks fell below root pressure. One courier could cross if engineers opened a manual corridor. Its mass allowance could carry only one full request.

“You chose Sable Two,” Talia said.

“Their pumps supported eight million people.”

“Nareth lost cultures.”

“Their remaining stock covered four days under normal dosing.”

“The closure lasted nine.”

“We did not know that on Day one.”

Jun remembered the cargo annex heat. Transit shutdown had stopped replacement coolant for the building itself. Forty people worked under emergency fans while the white star struck the station hull. A senior coordinator had vomited into a waste bin, wiped his face, and continued reading casualty forecasts.

“We performed triage under a bounded emergency,” Jun said. “No option preserved everyone.”

Talia turned the allocation sheet over. On the back, junior Jun had written the courier’s departure mass by hand after the manifest system failed. The numbers leaned uphill. She had not slept for twenty-seven hours.

“Who reviewed your choice?” Talia asked.

“Three coordinators.”

“Publicly?”

“After routes reopened.”

“Did you hide Nareth’s request?”

“No.”

“Did you tell them Sable Two received the corridor?”

“Yes.”

“They knew who chose.”

Jun closed the folder harder than intended. “Knowing did not save their dead.”

“It limited what you could pretend later.”

The camera light remained steady between them.

Jun opened Day four. Nareth’s cultures had failed during an attempted local split. Dose rationing began. Sable Two’s pumps recovered after receiving catalyst, then an unrelated seal fracture disabled one district anyway. The chosen shipment saved millions and failed thousands. Triage had never promised clean causation.

The next folder carried a grease mark across its seal. Jun remembered making it with her own hand.

On Day five, engineers offered a second manual corridor. Radiation drift limited the crossing to a light courier. Emergency control assigned the mass to insulin cultures for Nareth. Forty minutes before commitment, Sable Three reported a cooling cascade in an orbital shelter holding sixty-one thousand people. Its replacement valve weighed less than the culture cabinet and could fit beside the courier’s clock shield.

Senior staff rejected a combined manifest. The valve’s irregular alloy might distort manual geometry. Nareth’s culture cabinet already held certification.

Jun had approved Nareth.

A maintenance engineer at Sable Three heard the allocation over an open emergency channel. He demanded the raw geometry limit. Control first refused because the corridor parameters carried security classification. The shelter engineer kept transmitting temperature readings every three minutes, each one higher than the last. Families behind him struck tools against bulkheads so control could hear how many people remained inside.

Talia read the transcript. “You released the limit.”

“After thirteen minutes.”

“Why?”

“Because he challenged the alloy assumption with a local test.”

The engineer cut a sliver from an identical valve, placed it beside a field calibrator, and showed that its magnetic signature stayed below the corridor’s actual tolerance. Certification tables had listed the whole valve assembly, including a motor that would not travel. The courier could carry cultures and valve if crews removed two shield panels and accepted higher exposure on an unoccupied cargo bay.

Emergency control changed the manifest.

The courier crossed. Nareth received enough culture to restart local growth. Sable Three replaced the valve with eleven minutes left before shelter temperatures exceeded recovery range. Three crew members absorbed radiation while stripping the shield panels; all survived with marrow treatment after routes reopened.

Jun had kept the grease-marked file because it recorded an overturned decision. Public pressure had complicated control. It had also supplied local knowledge her desk lacked.

“You could not have found that option alone,” Talia said.

“No.”

“Did the challenge cause delay?”

“Twenty-two minutes.”

“Could delay have killed both groups?”

“Yes.”

“Yet you opened the parameters.”

“I opened a narrow operational limit to one engineer under emergency authentication.”

“His family could hear.”

“So could everyone crowding the channel.” Jun took back the transcript. “Three false technical claims followed within an hour. Staff spent time disproving each one while other allocations waited.”

“One correct challenge changed two outcomes.”

“That ratio cannot govern a network.”

“Then publish the cost of review and set a queue. Do not remove review because truth arrives mixed with panic.”

Jun turned to Day seven. A food depot on Orel had broadcast its inventory after local officials concealed spoilage. Crowds seized two warehouses. One refrigeration unit failed during the occupation, destroying grain that might have fed a district for a month. Open information had not produced wise collective action there.

Talia studied those photographs as carefully as she had studied the successful challenge. “Did secrecy preserve the grain?”

“No.”

“Did disclosure destroy all of it?”

“No. The other depot distributed stock before power failed.”

“Then the record contains both.”

“You keep wanting records to absolve decisions.”

“I want them to prevent absolution.”

Jun felt the old annex around her again: sweat caught beneath her collar, fans grinding hot air, a hundred voices asking which shipment mattered most. She had not emerged believing authorities always chose correctly. She had emerged believing choices required someone who could still act while every answer produced a funeral.

“Triage needs a decision before agreement,” she said.

“Agreed.”

“It needs protected channels where incomplete forecasts do not trigger competing seizures.”

“Sometimes.”

“It needs authority to hold dangerous facts until action is possible.”

“For hours, perhaps days. With recorded criteria, independent review, and an ending condition.”

“And if the danger has no ending condition?”

“Then you do not have triage. You have government by permanent emergency.”

The phrase irritated Jun because it drew a clean border through years that had contained no clean day. Kest-Blue had not announced a moment when uncertainty became personhood. Route dependency had not paused while cabinet scientists argued thresholds. Each review had inherited the risks of the previous one.

Yet the grease-marked folder remained under her hand. During Sable, emergency authority had exposed itself to a shelter engineer because sixty-one thousand lives supplied a reason to challenge. Under Pelagos classification, nine million voices had received no channel at all until Maelin broke one open.

“What would your public ledger have done during those nine days?” Jun asked.

“Shown the same allocation.”

“To a population already attacking gate offices?”

“Yes.”

“Crowds blocked two emergency depots. A false opening report killed forty-three people in a docking crush.”

“Then publish the crowd risk too.”

“You treat information as if naming a consequence contains it.”

“No. I treat concealment as another intervention with casualties.”

Jun slid the verified mortality file across the table. 6,104,000 deaths across three systems. Some came from missed medicine, some from failed environmental systems, some from hunger and violence after local administration collapsed. The number had built Jun’s career because she refused to let anyone discuss transit as convenience afterward.

“The present Concord depends on gates,” she said. “Reciprocal Routing removes nearly half of safe capacity for at least a decade. Not nine days. Ten years. Your own model predicts shortages in every outer system.”

“It predicts shortages under current allocation.”

“Changing allocation does not manufacture food.”

“It changes who carries the shortage.”

“People still die.”

“Yes.” Talia did not look away. “Your argument remains partly correct.”

The concession hurt more than dismissal.

“Then why proceed?” Jun asked.

“Because Sable required choices inside an emergency that ended. You turned emergency custody into permanent permission. Eleven years of traffic followed the first structured signal. Every crossing increased the branch load because secrecy protected throughput from review.”

“We lacked proof of personhood.”

“You prevented the tests that could supply it.”

“Maelin’s receiver risked reserve stability.”

“So did continued exclusion.”

Jun knew each cabinet date. AY 132, recurring biological patterns. AY 135, probable language compression. AY 139, Maelin’s unauthorized receiver proposal. AY 141, a cultural sequence that repeated with correction after deliberate interference. At every stage, confidence remained below the threshold cabinet rules required for intervention. At every stage, Jun had approved another monitoring interval while traffic continued.

Thresholds had protected the present from panic. They had also ensured evidence arrived only after Kest-Blue could speak for itself.

“You signed the warning closed,” Jun said.

Talia leaned forward. “I signed because you showed me Sable and asked for ninety days. When the review found error, preference, and memory, I asked you to reopen it. You transferred my team. I remained. That failure belongs to me. It does not turn your order into wisdom.”

Jun saw her younger subordinate outside an Arclight review chamber, carrying a folder against her chest. Talia had argued for another personhood test. Jun had called her brilliant, thanked her, and assigned her to route casualty standards. The praise had functioned as a lock neither woman named.

“What do you believe I wanted?” Jun asked.

“A survivable present.”

“And now?”

“You want the authority to define survival without witnesses.”

The answer stayed between them after speech ended.

A security tone interrupted the interview. Sal’s message reported that two outer-system assemblies had demanded a pause in Final Fall preparation. Neither held Council authority. Both controlled local transit keys.

Jun closed the Sable archive. “Your model cannot save Kest-Blue before Pelagos loses coherence.”

“Not without gate participation.”

“Which you do not have.”

“Then why arrest us?”

“To prevent tests from destabilizing the field.”

Talia’s expression changed by a fraction. Auditor’s recognition. A stated reason that did not cover every action beneath it.

Jun gathered the files and left before her former student could ask the next question.

Final Fall command occupied the outer operations ring. Technicians had completed charge calibration twelve days ahead of the original schedule. The Council order allowed forced decoherence within thirty days, but Jun could advance technical readiness without changing the authorized deadline. She approved continuous charge coupling, doubled security at field junctions, and ordered a full evacuation plan for Pelagos staff.

Maelin’s arrest complicated the phase reference. Ministry physicists could maintain it for routine contact, though none understood the receiver’s improvised lower loop as well as she did. Jun authorized supervised consultation from custody. She would not let pride turn lawful detention into avoidable loss.

At 23:30, a projected calibration pulse removed one day from the worst-case failure window.

Jun approved it.

Kest-Blue’s southern districts reported longer visual gaps. Isca’s monitored complaint arrived after a five-minute field delay. Jun read it, logged the effect, and approved the next pulse at lower amplitude.

Nothing about command felt like victory. It felt like choosing which dependency could still be honored before another failed.

Day 22 opened with public hearings across nineteen systems. Jun watched extracts while eating at the operations console. Cargo workers described empty habitats. Clinic directors described route mortality under half capacity. A Kest-Blue councilor condemned overwrite and endorsed distributed reception despite having lost a brother during phase failure. That testimony weakened Jun’s public argument more effectively than any accusation. The branch had rejected an easy rescue on moral grounds while the Ministry prepared to erase it for practical ones.

Legal staff proposed discrediting Reciprocal Routing through Maelin’s unauthorized work history.

“No,” Jun said. “Challenge its capacity assumptions.”

“They are holding under independent review.”

“Then challenge synchronization.”

“Orison reproduced the abort behavior.”

“Find a factual weakness.”

Silence crossed the staff channel.

Talia’s public model remained available. Arrest notices appeared beside it, but the source chains stayed valid. Someone had added the Sable allocation sheets after Talia’s counsel requested their release from historical classification. Jun could have blocked the request. She let it pass because using Sable in private while hiding its record would prove Talia’s charge.

Near midday, Sal entered operations with a sealed packet carried through the latest Kest window.

“Outer transit status,” he said.

Jun opened it. Five agricultural systems had suspended discretionary gate commitments. Three mining systems held their physical authorization rods under assembly custody. Lorn Reach continued medical arrivals but refused any Pelagos-linked routing order without public buffer proof.

“These are delays,” Jun said.

“Read the attached operator notices.”

The first came from South Anchor. Its gatekeeper would not release commitment authority for Final Fall support. The second named the same refusal at Sable Four. The third invoked local emergency law to place both cryptographic and mechanical controls under civic seal.

Jun read all eight notices. No system had seceded from the Concord. No gate had closed survival traffic. Operators continued committed food, medicine, and water routes where local assemblies allowed them. They had drawn a narrower line: Ministry security could not compel their gates to support Pelagos decoherence or seize reciprocal buffers.

“Order Continuity teams to recover control,” her deputy said.

“By what route?” Jun asked.

He stopped.

Personnel transfer required the same local commitments now withheld. Remote override required both operator authentication and the physical authorization rod. The network Jun had spent her life protecting contained no central switch. Its dependency created power in every place capable of saying not yet.

A ninth notice entered the packet queue while they watched.

Jun looked through operations glass toward Pelagos’s black petals. Final Fall charges shone along them in perfect readiness. Talia and Maelin remained in custody. Ministry technicians controlled the receiver. Every local objective she had set for the day had been achieved.

Across the outer systems, gate keys were leaving Ministry reach one lawful seal at a time.

Chapter 20 — The Worlds That Wait

The medicine queue had outgrown Kest North Anchor before Morrow Glass cleared its cradle.

Rhen saw it through the command blister: six feeder craft holding beyond the red approach lamps, each with a sickbay priority code and barely enough propellant to keep station. Two had painted their destinations across their hulls in work light. HARBOR COAST. BURIED NORTH. The third carried no message, only a repeated request for insulin cultures from cargo truss four.

“Control wants us to discharge by district mortality,” Saye said from the cargo board.

“Control has five versions of district mortality.” Rhen eased the carrier against its aft clamps as the cradle released. “Use the clinic receipt times.”

“That puts Harbor Coast first.”

“It asked first.”

A Ministry allocator cut into the channel. “Captain Tal, your manifest has been amended. Biological stock remains under continuity reserve until the Kest assembly certifies its gate contribution.”

Rhen kept one hand on the translation grip. North Anchor drifted past their starboard windows, its graphite ring marked by emergency lamps. Beyond it, Kest turned beneath a bank of northern cloud. Thousands of people down there had learned to measure politics in spoiled doses.

“The cultures were loaded before your amendment,” Rhen said. “They are addressed cargo.”

“They crossed under a network guarantee that no longer applies.”

“Then invoice the guarantee.”

“Do not release truss four.”

Saye muted the allocator. “Valeh says we have twenty minutes before the Harbor Coast transfer loses its clean thermal window.”

Rhen looked across the queue. Every feeder carried enough empty volume to make a poor reception room. Every crew had signed the Empty Rooms compact, yet none could offer bunks to people who had not arrived while their own districts waited for medicine. A compact looked generous in a council file. In orbit it occupied the same cold lockers as insulin.

“Cycle truss four to local custody,” Rhen said. “Put the Ministry seal in the receipt notes.”

Saye gave them a sharp glance. “That is a seizure charge.”

“No. A seizure means we keep it.”
