The first feeder matched velocity against their cargo spine. Its pilot approached too fast, corrected late, and struck the capture rail hard enough to shake the blister. Rhen held Morrow Glass steady while Saye talked the pilot through the second latch. Cargo handlers in pressure cloth crossed the truss hand over hand. They passed silver culture canisters along a tether, one person to the next, because North Anchor had denied use of its powered loader.

A carrier captain learned early that authority often hid in machinery. Whoever controlled the loader could make a lawful transfer impossible without issuing an unlawful order.

Rhen opened the external work channel. “Harbor Coast, report custody at each canister.”

The pilot’s breathing rasped over the reply. “Receiving one. Receiving two. Three. Captain, I have forty-seven patients on substitutes.”

“Then stop telling me and cool the box.”

The feeder backed away with nine canisters and two pallets of antivenom substrate. Buried North took the immune seed. The remaining craft divided the stock by clinic receipts until truss four showed bare restraints and condensation where the cold containers had rested.

North Anchor Control revoked Morrow Glass’s departure assistance.

Rhen smiled without pleasure. “Saye, bring up our own tugs.”

“We sold the port tug on Pelagos.”

“We sold the good one.”

The maintenance crew woke two scarred maneuver pods from their housings. One coughed propellant into vacuum before settling. Rhen worked them as separate limbs, pushing the carrier clear of Kest without touching the station’s guidance field. The medicine feeders peeled away below, silver points descending toward weather and need.

Their next alignment opened forty-three minutes late.

By then, Valeh had converted the emptied cold lockers into six narrow berths.

On Day 24 they reached Torren’s agricultural spindle with half their food allotment missing.

The gate had cut reception windows to protect its exclusion margin. Produce trains waited in the spindle’s dark. Rhen brought Morrow Glass alongside a seed cooperative whose docking collar had frozen shut, then crossed through the service throat in a pressure suit because the cooperative’s crew lacked a second qualified rigger.

Heat from the crop drums pressed through their suit before the inner hatch fully opened. The air smelled of damp fiber and sweet rot. Crates filled the central passage, leaving a shoulder-wide path beneath amber lamps. Each held seed tubers for three outer settlements. A cooling fault had warmed the first rank to germination, then starved the shoots when power rationing shut the lamps.

Cooperative chief Daro Pell knelt beside an opened crate. He lifted a tuber whose pale sprout had browned at the tip.

“Twenty years of blight resistance,” he said. “Now it is spoiled seed.”

“How much survived?” Rhen asked.

“Enough for one settlement if they plant thin. Not enough for three.”

Saye came through the hatch behind them carrying a thermal probe. She checked the next crate, then another. “The inner stacks are cooler.”

Daro did not look up. “Those belong to the ministry allotment.”

“Food ministry?”

“Reception reserve.” He finally faced Rhen. “Empty ground for Kest-Blue gets protected cooling. Existing settlements get a brown sprout.”

The accusation had traveled ahead of them. Every system now held some version of it: rooms for people not yet present meant rooms denied to citizens already breathing; prepared mass meant machine parts left uncast; reception grain meant smaller current meals. Rhen could answer with nine million names and still fail to cool one seed crate.

They removed their helmet. Sweat cooled across their scalp. “Which settlement has the shortest growing season?”

“Vey Field.”

“Send the sound inner stock there. Divide the next grade between the other two.”

Daro’s mouth tightened. “That uses the reserve.”

“The reserve needs living growers when it opens.”

“You can write that into a receipt?”

“I can write that I ordered it.”

He studied the old pressure scars on their suit. “You own a ship. Orders sit lightly on you.”

Rhen crouched beside the crate. The spoiled tubers had produced a little warmth of their own, futile growth trapped inside darkness. “My sister’s district took culture stock yesterday. If another district died waiting, the order would not sit lightly. It would still have to sit somewhere.”

Daro turned the tuber between his fingers. “Captains are meeting on Lumen Cross. They want out of the compact.”

“I know.”

“They say the Ministry will close us before Pelagos falls.”

“I know that too.”

“What do you say?”

Rhen put the damaged seed back in its crate. “I say we load what can grow.”

For seven hours, Morrow Glass supplied power to the cooperative while both crews sorted the stock. Workers marked every viable crate with destination and thermal history. No one erased the reception designation. They amended it, signed the amendment, and copied Daro’s objection beneath Rhen’s order.

When the spindle’s gate offered a short outbound window, Rhen left with three agricultural technicians asleep in the new berths and a hold full of cracked pump assemblies bound for Lumen Cross.

The compact captains had gathered inside an unfinished habitat cylinder where no one had yet paid for gravity.

Twenty-three vessels floated outside the cylinder on Day 25. Their cargo lamps made a rough belt around its bare hull. A week earlier those lights had meant pledged cabins, prepared mass, and routes volunteered for reception. Now eight captains had set their lamps to yellow withdrawal status.

Rhen pulled themselves through the habitat’s central chamber on a guide line. The cylinder smelled of sealant and cold metal. Floor panels had been strapped in temporary stacks along one wall. Through the unfinished windows, carrier silhouettes crossed the white glare of Lumen’s star.

Captain Osei Neral waited near the construction hub with the compact ledger tied to his wrist. “We delayed for you.”

“Bad habit.”

“We need Kest’s vote.”

“Kest did not elect me.”

“You signed for its carrier unions.”

“Then ask what their ships can do, not what their world believes.”

The captains gathered in a ring around a projector clipped to the hub. Without gravity, no one could claim the head of the table. Rhen suspected that explained why Osei had chosen the place.

A route map opened between them. Red closures spread across the Concord. Nine systems had suspended nonmedical passenger transit. Four food gates refused prepared mass unless the Council delayed Final Fall. Arclight had priority claims on machine components. Outer assemblies had begun impounding them anyway.

Captain Jori Venn, whose ore carrier could house six hundred people if stripped, touched the yellow mark beside her name. “My home gate has twelve days of bearing stock. If I carry habitat frames to Pelagos, I strand three refinery stations.”

Across the circle, Bram Keh said, “If Pelagos falls, the frames accomplish nothing.”

“They become housing.”

“Without air plants?”

“We carry air plants.”

“Then a clinic loses bioreactors.”

Their words never rose into a speech. Each captain had a component, a route, and a constituency. Together they formed the machinery of refusal.

Rhen listened until the map had more annotations than routes. They pictured Jun Orra receiving the same data inside Continuity House. Dependency gave the director her strongest argument because dependency could not be shamed away. Close one crossing and some unseen room lost heat. Protect one reception habitat and a seed cooperative opened a crate to rot.

Osei caught their sleeve when they drifted past. “You have said nothing.”

“I was counting.”

“What?”

“Which withdrawals still leave their cargo moving.”

The room quieted by degrees.

Rhen enlarged the yellow vessels. “Jori can leave the compact and still bring bearings to the refinery. Bram can refuse Pelagos but carry air plants to Orison. None of you gain safety by tying up your ships. So the question concerns empty volume, not whether we keep working.”

Jori folded her arms. “Empty volume costs fuel.”

“Yes.”

“And reception mass costs cargo.”

“Yes.”

“You have an answer besides yes?”

“No.” Rhen pushed away from the hub, caught a strap, and stopped themselves beside the map. “I came through a medicine queue six craft long. Yesterday I held spoiled seed grown for settlements that may miss planting. If you want me to tell you scarcity proves the branch can be abandoned, ask plainly.”

Bram looked toward the unfinished cylinder wall. “Nine million people remain a number to my crew.”

“Then show them the bunks they signed.”

“They know the bunks.”

“Make them assign each one. No branch name if you do not have one. Write teacher, dock worker, difficult uncle, child who hates soup. Leave the berth empty, but stop calling the emptiness free.”

Osei rubbed his thumb along the compact ledger. “That sounds like theater.”

“Cargo labels are theater until the wrong crate reaches the wrong moon.”

A few captains laughed. Jori did not.

She said, “My assembly will revoke my gate bond.”

“Then withdraw.”

The answer moved through the ring harder than an appeal would have.

Rhen continued. “The compact cannot require captains to ruin their systems. It can require honest capacity reports. If you have six hundred berths and can now offer sixty, write sixty. If you have none, write none. Do not promise six hundred because you liked yourselves last week.”

Jori changed her status from yellow to white conditional. Bram reduced his pledged air plants by half and retained his cabins. Three captains withdrew. Their vessels stayed on the supply map.

By the end of the meeting, prepared reception capacity had fallen by nineteen percent. The compact survived because its members stopped describing the reduction as betrayal.

Day 26 began with a loading arm jammed across Morrow Glass’s aft truss.

Lumen traffic control had promised four pump assemblies and two pallets of bearing stock for refinery stations beyond the next gate. The loading arm reached the carrier with the first assembly, lost hydraulic pressure, and stopped with six tonnes of machinery hanging between hulls. Traffic control ordered Rhen to wait for a repair crew. Its estimate started at two hours and increased each time Saye asked.

Rhen suited up again.

They crossed the truss with a manual winch clipped at their hip. Lumen’s unfinished habitat filled the sky above them, its bare cylinder striped by work lamps. Below, the white star turned every edge hard enough to hurt. They reached the suspended assembly by crawling along the loader’s shadow, then fixed the winch cable through its shipping frame.

Jori Venn came out from her ore carrier carrying a second cable. Her helmet lamp flashed once in greeting.

“Your assembly called my bearings optional,” Jori said over suit radio.

“My assembly called my departure assistance optional.”

“Good. We have achieved equality.”

They worked without traffic control. Jori stabilized the load while Rhen released the loader claw one tooth at a time. Each release sent a vibration through the frame. If the last tooth slipped, the pump would rotate into the truss and crush both their lines.

“Hold two degrees port,” Rhen said.

“I am holding one.”

“You are drifting.”

“Your ship is drifting.”

Rhen checked the stars against the truss rail. Morrow Glass had moved by less than a hand’s width, enough to turn accusation into fact. Saye corrected from inside. The cable softened, then pulled true.

Jori laughed once. “Captains.”

“Machines.”

Together they lowered the assembly into its restraints. Cargo handlers locked it down by hand. The bearing pallets followed through a service cradle while the failed arm remained frozen beside them, an expensive obstacle defeated by old cable and patient bodies.

At the inner hatch, Jori caught Rhen’s shoulder ring. “My crew assigned the bunks.”

Rhen waited.

“Two mechanics. A grandmother who complains about the air. Four children, no soup opinions recorded.”

“How many?”

“Ninety-eight berths. Down from six hundred.”

“Ninety-eight is not nothing.”

“It feels like confession.”

“Then stop feeling and check your air reserve.”

They touched helmets before separating, the carrier version of an embrace when neither person trusted a suit joint under pressure.

Inside, Saye had laid the refinery destinations over the compact routes. The efficient path sent both pump assemblies through Arclight, where an inner-system allocation office could divide them. The reliable path sent one directly to a refinery gate running near failure. It cost eleven hours and a transit margin they had promised to reception work.

Rhen studied the machinery through the cargo camera. One pump for people already waiting. One margin for people trapped at Pelagos. The ledger offered no column for a clean decision.

“Send the first pump direct,” they said. “Arclight can divide the second.”

“We lose our reception slot.”

“We move to Osei’s later slot.”

“That puts us near the phase limit.”

“Mark it provisional.”

Saye entered the course. “You know Jun will use this circuit as proof that voluntary routing cannot sustain capacity.”

“She can use my maintenance history if she likes.”

“Your maintenance history proves nothing should sustain.”

The direct refinery gate accepted their commitment with twenty seconds remaining. Passage pressure squeezed the blister until the old seals whistled. Rhen heard the aft truss answer through its bolts, every loaded component declaring its mass in a different pitch. They arrived beside a refinery station with half its windows dark.

No receiving tug met them. Four workers came out on hand jets and guided the pump into a cargo throat whose powered clamps had been stripped for parts. One worker raised a glove toward the command blister after custody cleared. Behind that figure, station lights came on deck by deck as the old circulation plant surrendered power to the new unit.

Rhen logged the delivery before sleep could make the details generous. Four workers. Manual capture. Six dark decks restored to schedule. Eleven hours spent. One reception slot delayed, not abandoned.

Facts made poor comfort. They made better cargo.

When Morrow Glass rejoined the compact at Lumen, Rhen returned through a utility lock that required two people to close. Saye waited inside with a dispatch from Kest.

“The assembly held an open allocation session,” she said.

“Did they charge me with the cultures?”

“Later agenda.”

Rhen pulled off their gloves. Their fingertips carried black sealant from the habitat line. “What did they allocate?”

Saye sent the record to the blister. Kest had reviewed its medical deficit, delayed agricultural shipments, feeder propellant, and unfinished orbital shelters. The northern districts objected first. Harbor Coast objected second. Then the clinic unions had introduced a joint amendment.

Kest would volunteer a half allotment from its own prepared mass for reception habitats.

Rhen read the line twice. “Who demanded this?”

“No one. The vote cleared by seven points.”

“After yesterday’s queue?”

“Because of it, according to the clinic statement. They said a queue did not grant priority over people trapped outside it.”

Saye opened the attached district comments. A Harbor Coast nurse had listed the patients now receiving the cultures, not to claim exemption but to show what Kest meant by cost. A northern grower answered with projected winter shortages. The final entry came from a shelter engineer who had slept in an unfinished reception ring for two nights and certified that its water lines held pressure. None of them called the offer painless. They had measured the injury before voting to accept it.

The carrier received its next route window while they read. Lumen Cross opened a narrow accordant solution toward Pelagos supply space. Beyond the glass, the compact’s remaining ships changed their cargo lamps from private codes to common white. Each light corresponded to reduced cargo, tired crews, and a promise revised under pressure rather than discarded by frightened systems seeking private exemptions.

Rhen keyed Kest’s reduced allotment into the ledger. Every promised berth recalculated against current mass. The total remained insufficient. The engines beneath their feet shifted to commitment pitch anyway, carrying less than hope had offered and more than fear had left.

Chapter 21 — Seventy-Two Hours

Sal Varo searched Isca’s ceramic ring before he let her enter the custody drum.

He held it beneath a white scanner while she stood between two security officers in Pelagos Station’s inner corridor. The ring had rested against her skin since childhood. Nemi had fired it in a school kiln from clay taken beside their first apartment. Under the scanner, its green glaze turned gray.

“No active elements,” the technician said.

“I told you that,” Isca said.

Sal returned the ring on its cord. “You also told me you would ask Talia about legal representation.”

“I will.”

“And nothing else.”

“I did not tell you that.”

He had removed his command jacket. The dark work shirt beneath it showed damp marks at the collar where Pelagos cooling had begun to fail. Every few minutes the station gave a shallow tremor. Not enough to threaten the orbit, Maelin had said before the arrests. Enough to tell anyone with feet that two geometries continued arguing inside the structure.

Sal opened the first custody seal. “The visit remains monitored. No data instruments, no physical exchange, twenty minutes.”

“You left out no weather.”

“Is weather an instrument?”

“Usually.”

“Then no weather.”

The attempt at humor died between them. Sal had enforced Talia’s arrest under continuity law and refused Jun’s demand for solitary sensory restriction. Both decisions came from the same place. Isca understood consistency in weather models; she found it harder to forgive in a jailer.

They passed through three doors. Each closed before the next opened. The final chamber had a transparent wall cut with narrow ventilation slots, a bolted table, and two stools on opposite sides of a red custody line. Talia sat with her left wrist secured to a floor cable. Her white audit collar had been taken. Without it she looked smaller, though the old stiffness in her shoulders remained.

“Legal representation,” Talia said as Isca entered.

“Sal asked me to mention it.”

“I have mentioned it every six hours.”

“Then my work here has concluded.”

Sal stayed beside the door. One officer watched from the corridor window. The other carried a portable field suppressor against her chest, its charge lamps moving from blue to amber.

Isca sat. “How long?”

“Twelve minutes before Sal ends the visit for discussing the wrong subject.”

“Twenty,” Sal said.

“His generosity has increased.”

A tremor passed through the table. In its polished edge, Isca saw two versions of Talia’s restrained hand. One lay flat. The other had already curled into a fist. The images joined when the station settled.

Talia’s gaze fixed on the reflection. “How frequent?”

“Every four to seven minutes. Nemi says their southern districts have started seeing power interruptions.”

Isca placed both hands on the table where Sal could see them. “Maelin?”

“Separate cell. She has been writing equations on meal wrappers.”

“With what?”

“Berry concentrate.”

“That sounds like her.”

“They took the wrappers this morning.” Talia glanced toward the corridor officer. “Sal photographed them first.”

Sal did not deny it.

Isca studied the room’s fixed camera. “Gatekeepers are still withholding keys. Thirty-one have sent private assurances through the assembly network. None will call the assurances commitments while you remain charged with continuity sabotage.”

“They should not.”

“You would prefer fewer allies?”

“I would prefer gate operators who understand the procedure. Reciprocal Routing fails if anyone confuses political consent with a timing commitment.”

The answer irritated Isca because it sounded like Talia before North Anchor: accuracy offered as shelter against urgency. She leaned closer across the red line. “My city lost two relay substations last night. Nemi moved Aru into the school shelter, then moved him out when the shelter’s western wall began reflecting the wrong street. We do not have time for every gatekeeper to become a physicist.”

“No. We need them to trust one.”

“Maelin sits in another cell.”

“So they need the model and its test record.” Talia lifted her secured wrist until the cable clicked. “Not my innocence.”

Outside, a security worker rolled a cart of seized instruments past the observation window. Each case carried a white evidence tag. Isca recognized the field camera used for her first visual contact with Nemi. Another box held the damaged receiver core from Eren’s sabotage. Pelagos had turned its own means of perception into contraband.

Sal followed her gaze. “The equipment remains on station.”

“In boxes.”

“Boxes open under authorization.”

“People die under authorization too.”

His expression hardened. “Six million died when authorization failed during Sable.”

Talia interrupted before Isca could answer. “He knows both statements can be true.”

Sal looked at her restraint. “Knowing does not select a lawful action.”

“No,” Talia said. “It identifies when law has selected harm without admitting the choice.”

A low alarm sounded somewhere behind the custody wall. Three pulses, pause, three more. Sal checked his wrist panel. A station technician had reported phase distortion in the inner electrical bus. He dismissed the alert only after assigning a response team.

Isca watched him complete the procedure. “If overlap opens in this room, will you classify what comes through?”

“I will secure it.”

“That is not what I asked.”

“Nothing crosses custody unexamined.”

Talia lowered her arm. “Then examine quickly.”

A sharper tremor shifted the table against its damaged fastener.

“Jun began the outer-petal sequence,” Talia said.

“You can tell from a table?”

“From the interval. Final Fall drives load inward before release.”

Sal moved away from the wall. “Director Orra certified that no irreversible stage has begun.”

“True,” Talia said. “The sequence can stop. It also makes Pelagos less stable while it runs.”

“You have thirteen days under the Council order.”

Isca watched Sal’s face. “Do we?”

His jaw changed before his answer. “The order remains in force.”

“That was not my question.”

Another vibration traveled through the floor. This one carried a higher note, close to a glass rim touched by a wet finger. The field suppressor in the officer’s arms flashed amber twice. At the same instant, the corridor window reflected a room that did not exist on their side: green-painted walls, a crowded civic relay desk, someone leaning over a weather plot.

Nemi.

The image lasted less than a second. Her hair had been tied with a red cloth. One hand rested on a transmitter frame. She looked not toward Isca but toward a point beside her, perhaps a district coordinator, perhaps Aru asking a question after being told not to interrupt.

Isca stood so quickly her stool struck the wall.

The officer raised the suppressor. “Sit down.”

“I saw my wife.”

“Sit.”

Talia had seen something else. Her attention stayed on the suppressor’s lamp. “Your local field is coupling to the custody grid.”

Sal glanced at the device. “Explain.”

“The suppressor creates a clean reference around the room. Pelagos keeps trying to phase-lock against clean references.”

Isca understood before Sal did. “It can carry a narrow packet.”

“No,” Sal said.

“We have done it through dirtier equipment.”

“You will not use a custody device as a relay.”

Talia leaned back, the cable at her wrist drawing tight. “Then turn it off. The next overlap could burn out its control layer.”

The officer looked to Sal.

He said, “Power it down.”

She touched the switch. Nothing changed. The lamps moved from amber to white.

Pelagos shook sideways.

The bolted table tore one fastener from the floor. Isca caught its edge and felt the stool leave her knees. Sal hit the door frame. The officer carrying the suppressor fell across the red line, and the device slid beneath Talia’s stool with its power cell whining.

“Do not touch the casing,” Talia said.

Isca dropped to one knee. A seam along the suppressor had opened, exposing a row of control wafers. White light moved beneath them in pulses that matched the counting interval from the first contact at North Anchor.

Sal pushed himself upright. Blood darkened one sleeve at the elbow. “Officer, clear the room.”

“The door has lost acknowledgment,” the corridor guard said through the window.

He struck the manual release. The first lever came down. The second remained locked.

Nemi’s relay room appeared across every reflective surface.

This time sound followed. Not words yet, only overlapping breaths and a child crying somewhere beyond the transmitter. Talia reached as far as her wrist cable allowed and used her shoe to pin the suppressor against the floor.

“Isca, the top wafer has a maintenance contact on its left edge.”

Sal stepped across the custody line. “Do not instruct her.”

“The power cell will rupture if the coupling closes.”

“Then tell my officer how to stop it.”

“She cannot reach the contact from there.”

Isca lay flat and pushed two fingers through the opened seam. Heat bit her knuckles. She found a recessed metal square.

“Press once,” Talia said. “Do not hold it.”

Isca pressed.

The whine stopped. The white pulses remained.

Nemi’s voice arrived through the suppressor speaker. “Isca?”

Sal closed his eyes for one brief count. When he opened them, duty had returned to his face.

“I am here,” Isca said.

“I have a bridge. It will not last.”

Behind Nemi, civic relay workers moved paper records away from a leaking ceiling. Their room tilted by several degrees relative to Isca’s floor. Pelagos had joined places without making their gravity agree.

Talia spoke quietly. “Ask for channel width.”

Nemi heard her. “Fourteen kilobits stable, perhaps twenty on the peaks. We have six district relays stacked behind us.”

“Enough,” Talia said.

Sal looked from her to the suppressor. “Enough for what?”

“The complete personhood proof.”

The phrase altered the room more than the tremor had. Talia and Maelin had built the proof from Kest-Blue census continuity, independent weather history, private challenge responses, birth and debt records, and the branch’s own asylum petition. Jun had blocked the certified file from public gate relays by classifying its authentication chain. Fragments had escaped. The whole argument, with reproducible tests and Reciprocal Routing’s operational plan, remained trapped behind Ministry custody.

Isca had carried one half since Day 20.

Not in the ceramic ring. Not in clothing or an implant. Maelin had divided the transmission into verbal reconstruction keys, each shaped as a forecast sequence Isca already knew. Talia held the other half as audit exceptions memorized during the derivation. Separately, neither could rebuild the file. Sal’s search had found exactly what it expected: no instrument.

Talia met Isca’s eyes. “Are your northern storm tables current?”

“Through yesterday.”

Sal understood. His hand went to the room alarm.

Isca said, “If you end this visit, the suppressor loses its clean reference when the door opens. You may lose the bridge.”

“You planned this.”

“We hoped Pelagos would overlap,” Talia said. “Planning requires greater confidence.”

“Director Orra prohibited transmission of classified operational models.”

“The routing model has circulated publicly for seven days.”

“Not with cabinet authentication.”

Talia pulled once against the floor cable, not to escape but to make the restraint visible. “That authentication establishes what the Ministry knew. It belongs with the proof.”

Sal’s thumb rested above the alarm. Through the wall, his corridor officer continued working the failed door. Nemi waited inside the reflected room while water ran down a green wall behind her.

Isca said, “You allow lawful personal custody. Fine. Keep our bodies. The evidence concerns nine million other people.”

His gaze moved to Nemi’s image. “If the bridge destabilizes, I terminate it.”

“Agreed,” Talia said.

“I did not authorize the broadcast.”

“No,” Isca answered. “You authorized a visit.”

Sal lowered his hand.

They began with weather.

Isca recited the AY 61 coronal sequence backward by observatory time, using pressure deviations as separators. Nemi’s relay translated each sequence into file blocks and sent checksums through six Kest-Blue districts. Talia added the audit exceptions: early reserve receipts, persistent biological structure, continuity cabinet review dates, traffic increases after the first warnings. Each fact entered the channel beside its source and test procedure.

The custody room turned into a relay by physical necessity. Isca kept one hand on the suppressor’s maintenance contact to prevent the power cell from reconnecting. Talia braced the device with her foot. Sal’s officer fed them a cooling line from the wall emergency kit when the casing began to smoke.

Nemi did not let the proof become only Ministry history. She brought in a registrar from Kest-Blue’s southern coast, who read three births entered after the branches diverged. A mechanic presented repair records from a transit line current Kest had never built. An old man named Jalen gave his debt code and complained that recognition should not cancel what his neighbor owed him.

“Keep that complaint,” Nemi said. “It proves civic continuity better than our anthem.”

The old man told her what to do with the anthem and signed his record.

Aru appeared once at the edge of the relay image, carrying a towel toward the leaking wall. Isca saw him before he saw her. He looked thinner than on Day 10. His shirt bore a cloud forest beetle printed in yellow, the same design Nemi had described buying at a neighborhood market during their last ordinary week.

Isca missed one forecast interval.

Talia supplied it without turning her head. “Northern pressure rise, point eight.”

Aru looked toward the transmitter. “Mama?”

“Take the towels to Lio,” Nemi said.

“I heard her.”

“I know.”

Isca kept her fingers on the hot contact. “Do what Nemi says, Aru.”

He stared at the reflection that held her. “When are you coming home?”

No answer could fit the bridge. She chose the one that did not steal Nemi’s work or promise a world.

“We are building somewhere for home to arrive.”

Aru considered this with the grave suspicion he reserved for adult grammar. Then the relay room lurched. Nemi caught him by the shoulder and pushed him toward the corridor.

“Go.”

He went.

The personhood file reached completeness in nineteen minutes. Talia appended Reciprocal Routing beneath it: prepared-mass requirements, synchronized commitments, temporary shutdown, failure boundaries, and the permanent cost to capacity. No claim promised painless rescue. The plan asked every system to accept slower transit and immediate scarcity in exchange for preserving both populations.

Nemi opened the civic relay to external receivers.

Sal said, “Jun will block Pelagos output.”

“She already has,” Nemi replied. “We are not sending from Pelagos alone.”

The six district relays transmitted through the exclusion field. On the present side, every gate running even a diagnostic arrival pair received a fragment. Civilian arrays captured the pieces and rebuilt them in public. The proof traveled inside operations the Ministry could not stop without closing the network at once.

For thirty-seven seconds, the suppressor speaker filled with receipt codes.

Kest Assembly received complete.

Torren received complete.

Lumen Cross received complete.

Outer Accord Forum received complete.

Arclight public archive received partial, requested repair, then received complete.

The corridor door finally released. Security officers entered with insulated hooks and a containment box. Sal ordered them not to break Isca’s contact until the last receipt cleared. They obeyed him because the device might rupture. They may also have heard the old man arguing about his debt through the speaker.

When the forty-seventh system acknowledged at least one full public copy, Talia lifted her foot.

“Now,” she said.

Isca released the maintenance contact. Two officers pulled the suppressor into the box. Its casing split with a dry snap, and white light vanished from the custody room. Nemi’s image collapsed into ordinary reflections.

The absence hurt with physical precision.

Sal secured the containment lid. “Visit concluded.”

Before anyone moved, every station panel displayed a continuity alert.

Director Jun Orra had accelerated Final Fall.

Pelagos instability provided the stated cause. The outer-petal sequence would proceed directly to forced decoherence unless the reserve returned to safe limits. The order cited civilian interference with a classified field device as evidence that station control had failed.

Isca read the timestamp. “She issued this nine minutes ago.”

“During the broadcast,” Sal said.

“She did not wait to know whether we succeeded.”

Talia stood carefully, her wrist still cabled to the floor. “Success made no difference to her risk calculation. Instability did.”

A second wave of notices covered the first. System assemblies had begun filing emergency demands with the Concord Council. Kest called for suspension. Torren joined. Lumen Cross certified the proof and requested independent review. Outer systems added their names faster than the panel could sort them.

Sal’s corridor officer counted under his breath.

At twenty-four assemblies, the display marked a majority of system assemblies demanding delay.

The Council convened by priority relay. Its procedural channel remained visible for eleven minutes. No debate reached Pelagos, only motions and certifications. The emergency suspension failed because Final Fall authority belonged to the Council’s continuity committee, not to a simple assembly majority. The committee refused transfer.

Talia watched the refusal settle into the public record. “They cannot claim they lacked an alternative now.”

“That helps at a trial,” Isca said.

“Yes.”

“We need it to help before one.”

Sal received a private order. His face tightened as he read. “Talia returns to isolation. Isca goes to monitored quarters. All Pelagos field equipment enters security custody.”

“You cannot put the star in a locker,” Isca said.

“No. I can keep people away from its controls.”

The station shook again. Somewhere beyond the custody drum, a pressure alarm began and did not stop.

Sal detached Talia’s floor cable from its bolt but left the cuff locked. He took the line himself rather than handing it to an officer. That small custody remained lawful, personal, and entirely inadequate.

The public panel cleared its flood of assembly demands. Jun’s accelerated schedule replaced them with a red operational clock.

Isca read the interval aloud because Nemi could no longer hear it and because every person in the room needed the same measure.

“Seventy-two hours remain.”

Interlude III — The Gatekeepers’ Ballot

RECIPROCAL ROUTING COMMITMENT REGISTER

Source: Gate operations logs, assembly escrow files, local custody records.

Authentication window: Day 27, 14:00–18:00 Concord Mean.

Question submitted: Will local gate control accept synchronized Reciprocal Routing commitment, temporary shutdown, public veil accounting, and prepared-mass release under the published Pelagos procedure?

01. KEST NORTH ANCHOR | YES | 14:03 Operator key entered after open assembly instruction. Medical feeder queue remained active during filing. Local note: “We know the immediate cost.” Timing staff assigned.

02. TORREN AGRICULTURAL SPINDLE | YES | 14:07 Cooperative observers witnessed commitment. Cooling reserve reduced to sustain synchronized field preparation. Viable seed stock moved before gate draw began.

03. LUMEN CROSS | YES | 14:09 Compact captains supplied independent mass audit. Gatekeeper accepted revised reception capacity, nineteen percent below original pledge. No substitution of promised volume permitted.

04. ARCLIGHT CENTRAL | YES | 14:14 Public gallery excluded from control floor under existing safety code. Operator streamed every key action to civic archive. Continuity House filed objection.

05. ORISON CONSTRUCTION GATE | YES | 14:16 Unfinished habitat ring designated primary reception site. Water, air, and rotation checks attached. Gate crews requested no ceremonial naming during operations.

06. VEY FIELD | YES | 14:20 Planting schedule amended by grower vote. Gate load accepted despite short-season transfer losses. Two technicians signed from a tractor cab.

07. HARBOR COAST | YES | 14:24 Clinic union countersigned. Insulin culture deliveries continued through preparation. Gatekeeper logged forty-seven patients on substitutes, then committed without qualification.

08. SABLE REACH | YES | 14:29 Descendants’ council read names from the famine closure before authorizing. Entry notes risk of invoking Sable to forbid all future moral choice.

09. MERROW DEEP | YES | 14:33 Volatile pumps placed on autonomous cycle. Local miners demanded public access to exclusion receipts from all prior crossings. Requirement accepted as political instruction, not operational condition.

10. CALDER STATION | YES | 14:37 Gate crew voted by maintenance shift. Senior keeper entered the minority view beside the commitment and preserved both records under equal seal.

11. NORTH LANTERN | YES | 14:41 Passenger departures halted six hours early. Families remained in terminal berths. Operator report includes food inventory, heating margin, and expected missed funerals.

12. PELD ORBITAL | YES | 14:46 Machine shops released prepared alloy. Apprentices hand-carried timing wafers after freight control refused priority. All wafers passed local verification.

13. CROWNLESS | YES | 14:51 Assembly instruction arrived incomplete. Keeper reconstructed the missing page from three public mirrors, compared signatures, and accepted commitment after a nine-minute delay.

14. ILYA’S REST | YES | 14:56 Reserve beds reassigned from expansion ward to reception triage. Hospital director protested loss of future capacity and signed the objection before gate authorization.

15. BRIGHTWATER | YES | 15:02 Ocean habitat reduced desalination reserve for field draw. Gate staff rotated control teams every twenty minutes because local gravity faults had induced nausea.

16. NARROW HOME | YES | 15:07 Smallest inhabited gate in the Concord. Commitment entered by one keeper, one deputy, and a public recorder sharing the same pressure shelter.

17. TESSERACT QUAY | YES | 15:11 Freight syndicate withdrew legal challenge. Operator preserved the withdrawal timestamp. Cargo owners retained damage claims against the Council and Ministry.

18. AFTON BLUE | YES | 15:15 Local weather closed surface launch sites. Timing crew worked from orbital isolation. Prepared mass consisted chiefly of unused storm shielding and vacant ferry tanks.

19. RILL | YES | 15:21 Gatekeeper required the branch census to remain visible on every control panel. Software team added a live total beside phase margin. No predictive losses displayed.

20. OLD SUNWARD | YES | 15:26 Retired operators joined the active shift. Manual clock comparison replaced a damaged network service. Four independent readings agreed within published tolerance.

21. GARDEN NINE | YES | 15:31 Agricultural council split evenly until a nursery cooperative released its own prepared matter. Keeper accepted the contribution and recorded the tie-breaking civic instruction.

22. FAR ARDEN | YES | 15:36 Six-day supply circuit interrupted. Local log lists each delayed destination and responsible officer. Commitment followed without a claim that rescue erased those costs.

23. HELIOT ROOK | YES | 15:43 Security commander requested control authority. Gatekeeper refused and retained technical command under assembly law. Security personnel guarded the chamber doors without entering.

24. PLAINSONG | YES | 15:49 Communication fault removed all live Council traffic. Operator used the signed procedure already received, then transmitted commitment through three civilian arrays.

25. CINDER MARCH | YES | 15:55 Habitat residents observed Pelagos reflections in polished water tanks. Gate team isolated reflective surfaces from control optics and continued preparation.

26. WESTERLY | YES | 16:02 Passenger union supplied volunteer stewards for shutdown shelters. Keeper logged consent after confirming that volunteers held no access to timing controls.

27. ASH VAULT | YES | 16:09 Manufacturing draw cut by one quarter. Furnace crews completed safe cooling before commitment. No emergency exemption requested.

28. KORU LINE | YES | 16:15 Operator’s spouse led the local opposition petition. Both signatures appear in the public record. Keeper entered approval and requested independent review after operations.

29. SEL NARROWS | YES | 16:22 Food stores projected eleven days under closure assumptions. Assembly authorized ration release. Gate control committed after confirming direct shipments could resume on abort.

30. DAWN ARCHIVE | YES | 16:29 Civic archivists mirrored the personhood proof across surface libraries. Gatekeeper attached checksum, operating oath, and a handwritten refusal to classify citizens as residuals.

31. KEST SOUTH RING | YES | 16:35 Southern crews matched North Anchor’s clock. Two districts objected to mass division. Keeper accepted the majority instruction and left objections readable.

32. IRON CHOIR | NO | 16:42 Gate council cited refinery dependence and rejected temporary shutdown. Operator locked commitment hardware, then published the lock code’s custody chain.

33. ARCLIGHT EAST | NO | 16:47 Continuity directive retained local force. Keeper refused assembly petition pending Council authorization. Public observers recorded the control chamber from outside its sealed doors.

34. MERE OUTPOST | NO | 16:53 Medical stores projected collapse under delayed transit. Gatekeeper entered refusal beside named clinics and requested evacuation priority if other systems proceeded.

35. VANTAGE | NO | 17:00 Operator challenged the published phase tolerance. Local simulation produced two unresolved edge cases. No independent physicist cleared them before deadline.

36. HALCYON MILL | NO | 17:06 Workers’ assembly supported rescue. Senior keeper declined personal execution authority and surrendered keys to sealed civic custody. No qualified deputy available.

37. RED WAKE | NO | 17:12 Security forces occupied the gate floor without discharge of weapons. Keeper’s refusal followed private questioning. Record preserves visible bruising; coercion finding remains pending.

38. PAVO’S STAR | NO | 17:18 Local assembly rejected prepared-mass release after housing loss projections. Gatekeeper certified the vote and included minority reception plans.

39. KETTLE MOON | NO | 17:24 Timing spine failed calibration twice. Operator refused commitment rather than submit a false ready state. Repair estimate exceeded the ballot window.

40. GLASSHOLD | NO | 17:31 Residents voted against any protocol that preserved future exclusions. Keeper interpreted Reciprocal Routing as insufficient abolition and withheld the gate.

41. SEVEN FALLS | CONDITIONAL | 17:37 Commitment activates after verified Pelagos command transfer to joint control. Clock synchronized. Prepared mass secured. Local key remains in operator custody.

42. MIRALE | CONDITIONAL | 17:42 Approval requires proof that current Kest cannot face overwrite. Published model accepted; independent matter audit still running. Completion expected before Day 29.

43. OCHRE PORT | CONDITIONAL | 17:48 Gate will join if total ready commitments reach forty. Operator rejected symbolic participation below a viable synchronization threshold.

44. STILL HARBOR | CONDITIONAL | 17:53 Local hospitals demand protected emergency packet windows during shutdown. Technical review found one narrow channel possible. Council waiver pending.

45. OUTER LACE | CONDITIONAL | 17:58 Commitment depends on civilian observers remaining inside the control gallery. Security order requires their removal. Keeper requested immediate judicial ruling.

46. WINTERSPAN | SILENT | 18:00 No ballot packet received. Gate telemetry shows active calibration until 17:46, followed by local power loss. Civilian arrays report no control-floor traffic.

47. GREY PERIHELION | SILENT | 18:00 No response to assembly, Council, or operator channels. Gate continues routine holding pattern. Custody of the senior key cannot be verified.

FIRST TALLY CLOSED | 18:00

31 yes. 9 no. 5 conditional. 2 silent.

Total commitments recorded: 47.

Operational note: A vote does not complete a route. Every affirmative key still requires synchronized control, prepared mass, a common clock, and a Pelagos command willing to receive it.

Part IV — The Falling

Chapter 22 — The Blockade at Pelagos

Day 28 opened with thirty-one civilian carriers holding their engines cold outside Pelagos Reserve.

Rhen counted them from Morrow Glass by cargo light, not registry. White marked prepared habitat volume. Green marked biological support. Amber marked industrial mass that could be converted during instantiation. Against the red dwarf, the lights formed a loose constellation no navigation chart recognized.

Between them and Pelagos Station, Ministry security had placed twelve cutters across the approach plane.

The cutters held close intervals, noses turned outward, drive plumes low and steady. None displayed attack locks. They did not need to. Their formation covered every safe route through the exclusion petals, and a carrier that tried to pass would have to force a cutter aside or cross the station’s unstable field at a bad angle.

“Pelagos blockade confirms weapons free under continuity defense,” Saye said.

The words came from the intercepted command band. Rhen looked at the tactical pane. Red range circles spread from each cutter until they overlapped around the reserve like scales.

“Any fire-control illumination?”

“Not yet.”

“Then they are free to use weapons they have not aimed.”

Osei Neral’s face appeared on the compact channel. Lumen Cross turned the skin beneath his eyes gray, or perhaps four days without sleep had done it. “Three captains want a breach on vector nine.”

“Vector nine passes through petal five.”

“They say the cutter gap gives us fourteen seconds.”

“Petal five has been slipping phase since yesterday.”

“They know.”

“No, they know the sentence. Knowing requires not flying into it.”

A second face joined them. Jori Venn had strapped herself into her ore carrier’s cargo station rather than its bridge. Behind her, prepared alloy blocks filled the camera view in dark ranks.

“Captain Tal, my assembly did not send this mass to wait outside a legal argument.”

“Good. We are waiting outside a firing solution.”

“The Council gave Jun seventy-two hours. We have fewer now.”

“Then we cannot waste them scraping survivors off a reserve petal.”

Jori’s jaw set. “Your ship has crossed unstable fields before.”

“And it came back with a woman from another world in an empty bay. That does not qualify me to recommend it.”

A proximity alarm sounded. One of the compact ships, a narrow passenger ferry called Lark Ascending, had moved ahead of the holding line. Its pilot drove toward vector nine with two habitat drums attached along the spine.

Rhen opened direct channel. “Lark, cut thrust.”

No response.

“Captain Hes, your port drum has an unsecured radiator hinge. Petal five will tear it off before security has to.”

The ferry continued accelerating.

Rhen brought Morrow Glass’s maneuver pods online. Saye caught the movement on her board.

“If we leave formation, cutters track us.”

“They already track us.”

Rhen rolled the carrier thirty degrees and burned across the ferry’s projected path. Morrow Glass moved slowly compared with a cutter. Compared with a loaded ferry, it carried enough mass to make refusal physical.

“Rhen,” Osei said, “you will not clear that crossing in time.”

“I do not need to clear it.”

The ferry filled the forward glass. Its two habitat drums showed fresh white paint and exposed cooling lines, unfinished rooms wrapped around a passenger engine. Rhen waited until the pilot could see every scar on Morrow Glass’s bow, then fired the starboard pod in three short bursts.

Their carrier slid into vector nine.

“Collision warning,” Saye said.

“I have it.”

“Range eight hundred.”

“Hes has it too.”

At five hundred meters, Lark Ascending cut thrust. Its drive plume collapsed. The ferry yawed as the habitat drums tried to keep moving around its spine.

Rhen fired both aft pods, matched the yaw, and placed Morrow Glass broadside between the ferry and the blockade. The carrier’s old frame groaned under lateral load.

Hes came on channel furious. “Move your ship.”

“Secure your radiator hinge.”

“You do not command this fleet.”

“Correct.”

“Then move.”

Rhen watched the Ministry cutters hold station. One had turned its weapons spine toward them. “You signed a compact for empty rooms, not dead crews. Return to line.”

“The line will still be here when Pelagos falls.”

“So will your drums if you stop trying to scatter them through the reserve.”

For five seconds, neither captain changed thrust. Then Jori’s voice entered the direct channel.

“Hes, your starboard drum has begun flexing at collar two. I can see it from here.”

The ferry pilot cursed. Attitude jets flashed. Lark Ascending backed away.

Rhen held broadside until the ferry cleared, then guided Morrow Glass toward the compact. Their hands had begun to tremble against the grips after the danger passed. They tightened them until the motion stopped.

Saye said, “Cutter six painted us during the turn.”

“I noticed.”

“You looked pleased.”

“I have a bad face for fear.”

“That explains several years.”

The compact channel filled with demands. Some captains wanted a new leader. Others wanted immediate retreat. Osei called for silence and failed to get it.

Rhen looked past the tactical pane to the carriers themselves. Their cargo made them slow, vulnerable, and useful. Habitat drums. Water tanks. Shield blocks. Agricultural substrate. Empty passenger decks with names assigned to bunks. They had approached as ships, allowing security to answer with ships.

“Put every cargo manifest on common display,” they said.

Osei frowned. “To whom?”

“Everyone.”

“The Ministry has our manifests.”

“It has numbers. Give it shapes.”

Jori understood first. “External work lights.”

“Yes. Unship safe cargo where they can see it.”

Saye turned from her board. “Here?”

“Here.”

“Loose mass inside a blockade gives every cutter commander a reason to call us hazardous.”

“Not loose. Cargo formation.”

Rhen marked a plane perpendicular to Pelagos approach. Carriers would separate their prepared containers and habitat drums, tether them in ordered rows, and hold the loads between the compact and security. No object would enter the exclusion field. No ship would close distance. The result would resemble a construction yard rather than an attack wing.

Osei studied the geometry. “It takes three hours.”

“Two if ore carriers handle the anchor blocks.”

“And if security advances while our trusses are open?”

“We retreat around the cargo.”

“Leaving it to seizure.”

“That still places prepared mass closer to Pelagos than it sits now.”

Captain Hes broke in. “You want us to unpack under guns.”

“I want those guns to aim through housing and medical stock before they reach us.”

“That uses civilians as shields.”

“No civilians occupy the drums. We use cargo as cargo.” Rhen enlarged the safe lanes. “Security can inspect it, seize it, or negotiate around it. It cannot pretend we brought an assault force.”

Jori changed her ore carrier’s status to maneuver ready. “My blocks can hold the lower row.”

Osei followed. One by one, compact captains accepted the geometry. Hes waited until twenty ships had committed, then added Lark Ascending with a note demanding that Rhen never cross his bow again.

“Agreed,” Rhen said. “If he never points it at a reserve petal.”

The carriers moved.

Morrow Glass took the central row because its replaceable trusses could release cargo without changing the ship’s balance too quickly. Rhen matched rotation with a pair of Orison habitat drums. Saye extended the loading arms while crew crossed outside to attach eighty-meter tethers.

Every action appeared on open channel. Range. Mass. Tether tension. Thruster impulse. If security wanted evidence of an attack, it would have to edit three hundred civilian feeds.

The first habitat drum left Lark Ascending under gentle push. Its work lights revealed rows of dark windows and unfinished docking mouths. Jori’s alloy blocks followed, each riding a service sled. Green-lit biological tanks came from Harbor Coast with clinic seals still visible.

Ministry cutters shifted to maintain range.

“Security command requests purpose,” Saye said.

Rhen opened the reply to all bands. “Prepared reception inventory. No occupied craft entering your line. Request inspection corridor and station negotiation.”

The answer came from a commander identified only by service key. “Civilian fleet will withdraw to outer holding distance. Cargo deployment will cease.”

“Negative.”

“You are creating navigational hazards within a continuity exclusion zone.”

“Hazards have coordinates. We transmitted ours.”

“Withdraw.”

“Inspect.”

No reply.

Rhen left the channel open. Around Morrow Glass, cargo moved into rows. The work demanded enough concentration to suppress argument. One water tank began rolling after a tug released too early. Saye caught it with a loading arm, but the impact pushed the carrier off line.

“Port pod, two percent,” Rhen said.

“Pod is under the tether cone.”

“Then use dorsal.”

“Dorsal pushes the medical tanks.”

Rhen examined the local geometry. The rolling tank crossed beneath their aft truss, pulling its cable toward a sharp brace.

“Release tether three.”

A crew chief shouted from the exterior channel. “We lose the tank.”

“We lose the ship if it cuts.”

Saye released. The cable snapped away one meter before reaching the brace. The water tank drifted free toward the security line.

Three cutters illuminated it.

“It carries water,” Rhen said on open band. “Mass twelve tonnes. No drive. Spin point four.”

The nearest cutter sent out a capture drone. Its claws closed around the tank’s service ring and arrested the drift. For the first time since arrival, Ministry security performed an action that preserved compact cargo.

Rhen recorded the custody transfer. “Thank you, cutter three.”

No one answered, but the tank’s work light stayed green.

Two hours into deployment, the rows took form. Habitat shells occupied the center. Water, shielding, and agricultural stock flanked them. Alloy anchors fixed the corners. Civilian carriers moved back five kilometers, leaving their contribution suspended between fleets.

Pelagos Station lay beyond, small against the red star. Its seven black exclusion petals appeared uneven. Petal five had shifted inward by several degrees. Reflections moved along its surface despite the absence of direct light.

The cargo formation made its argument through mass and distance. Security could not call it a breach while every carrier withdrew. It could not destroy the inventory without destroying visible rescue capacity. Retreat would abandon the route to civilians. Advance required weaving cutters through the very habitat hardware Final Fall meant to make unnecessary.

Cutter three requested permission to inspect the recovered water tank.

Rhen accepted before Osei could call a compact vote. They sent the tank’s fabrication record, pressure history, and custody seals on open band. The cutter answered with a demand for a physical sample.

“They already hold the tank,” Saye said.

“They want to know whether we booby-trapped it.”

“Did we?”

“Only with drinking water.”

The cutter’s capture drone pulled the tank toward an airless inspection frame mounted beneath its hull. Two security technicians emerged in hard suits. Their shoulder jets moved them carefully around the container. One held a scanner against its seams while the other connected a tap to the service valve.

Every compact channel watched.

Captain Hes said, “If they seize the stock, we advance.”

Rhen keyed over him. “No movement without common assent.”

“I am done accepting your assent.”

“Then accept your own collision alarms.”

On the cutter feed, clear water entered a sample bulb. The security technician held it beneath a spectrometer. Results appeared publicly because Rhen had required open telemetry as a condition of inspection.

Potable. Standard mineral balance. No active components.

The technician rotated the bulb in one glove. A small bubble moved through it. Behind the visor, the inspector looked younger than the service rank attached to the feed suggested.

“Cutter three,” Rhen said, “return the tank to the marked coordinate after inspection.”

A new voice answered. “Cargo inside a restricted approach zone enters continuity custody.”

“Then issue a receipt.”

The voice paused. “For water?”

“For twelve tonnes of it.”

“We are not cargo handlers.”

“You became cargo handlers when you caught it.”

Saye covered her mouth, poorly hiding a smile.

The cutter transmitted a provisional receipt. Rhen checked mass, timestamp, seal number, and condition. The condition field read INTACT, CONTENTS POTABLE. They countersigned.

“Thank you,” they said. “You now hold enough water to support four hundred reception berths for nineteen days.”

“We hold a navigation hazard.”

“Your receipt says potable.”

Compact captains copied the exchange to their assemblies. Civilian feeds gave the water tank a name within minutes: Receipt One. Rhen disliked the sentiment and preserved the custody record anyway.

Security could no longer treat every object in the cargo plane as an unidentified threat. Cutter five asked for the manifest of a green-lit biological tank. Cutter nine requested structural scans of an Orison habitat drum. Each request opened a narrow procedural channel. Each answer required cutters to acknowledge the cargo’s intended use.

Rhen assigned compact crews to respond by specialty. Harbor Coast clinics answered the biological tank. Orison engineers explained the drum’s unfinished pressure walls. Jori supplied alloy composition for the anchor blocks and invited security to scan every one.

The Pelagos blockade changed without moving. Red tactical marks acquired inventory labels. Weapons circles remained. So did the prepared rooms between them.

A security lieutenant appeared on Rhen’s private channel. The identifier named him Kale Vent, cutter nine. His helmet rested against his acceleration couch, and sweat darkened his close-cut hair.

“Captain Tal, withdraw the habitat drums fifty kilometers. Leave tagged mass for Ministry custody.”

“The drums cannot tolerate acceleration while untended.”

“Your engineers claim they can survive transit.”

“Transit under a carrier truss. Not a cutter tow.”

“We can provide tugs.”

“You have two qualified heavy tugs. I counted.”

Kale looked off-screen, likely at the same fleet inventory. “You counted our equipment?”

“I count anything that might have to move cargo.”

“We are not here to move your cargo.”

“No. You are here to keep us from moving it.”

His jaw flexed. “My cutter carries thirty-two people. Some have families on systems your shutdown will deprive of medicine.”

“So does my ship.”

“Then take it away.”

“Pelagos holds people with no ship to take them anywhere.”

“Final Fall authority has been settled.”

“Authority has been settled. People have not.”

Kale glanced down as a message reached him. Rhen saw his eyes stop on it. “Your civilian record lists ninety-eight berths aboard Venn Prospect.”

“Correct.”

“Yesterday it listed six hundred.”

“Yesterday its assembly still had bearing stock.”

“You reduced the pledge.”

“We made it true.”

The lieutenant sat back. Security training gave him no clean answer to a smaller promise. “If station authority accepts negotiators, cutter nine can clear a shuttle lane. The blockade commander has not approved one.”

“Will you clear it if Sal Varo asks?”

“If the order reaches me through station custody.”

“That sounds conditional.”

“It sounds like my oath.”

“Keep your receivers open.”

Kale ended the channel.

Rhen entered his statement into the compact log without calling it support. An operational possibility differed from allegiance. Talia had taught them that distinction, usually while making a room wish she had not attended.

Twenty minutes later, one of the habitat drums reported falling pressure. A seam seal had chilled below its installation tolerance while detached from carrier power.

Rhen sent Morrow Glass’s utility launch with a repair team. Cutter nine tracked the launch but did not illuminate it. Two compact mechanics reached the drum, clipped themselves to its unfinished hull, and spread a thermal blanket across the seam. Their hand lamps moved over the curved white surface while the civilian fleets waited behind them and security held ahead.

The repair took twelve minutes. No ship advanced. No weapon acquired a target. When pressure stabilized, cutter nine transmitted a safe-lane acknowledgment.

Only then did a new channel open from Pelagos Station. Sal Varo’s service signature authenticated it, though his face did not appear.

“Captain Tal, station security will accept two civilian negotiators through cutter corridor six. No carrier approach. Shuttle mass limit four tonnes.”

Osei let out a breath. “He moved.”

“Half a step,” Rhen said.

The blockade commander entered before they could answer Sal. “Station authority does not supersede continuity defense.”

Sal’s reply stayed flat. “Pelagos local custody includes station approach safety.”

“You are ordered to maintain isolation.”

“I am maintaining it. Two negotiators, inspected shuttle, no cargo.”

Rhen watched the two service signatures oppose each other on the channel. No speech, no threat, only overlapping claims to the same corridor.

“Accept,” Osei said.

Rhen selected Jori as second negotiator. Ore carriers had contributed the most prepared mass and carried the greatest reason to leave. If they reached Sal, the blockade might become a supervised delivery instead of a siege.

Saye prepared the ship’s utility launch. Rhen released their command harness and pulled themselves toward the rear hatch. Halfway there, a warning rolled across every panel.

PELAGOS FIELD DEVIATION.

The red star doubled in the forward glass.

One image remained centered behind the station. The other shifted nine degrees left, carrying a second arrangement of cargo and cutters with it. In that reflection, several ships occupied positions they had not yet reached.

“Hold all thrust,” Rhen ordered.

A carrier at the upper edge of the formation fired its correction jets anyway. Its future image moved first.

The exclusion petals flashed white.

A phase surge struck the cargo plane.

The first habitat drum vanished from Rhen’s sight and reappeared twenty meters away. Its tethers pulled taut across two water tanks. Jori’s anchor blocks collided without sound, throwing fragments through the formation. Cutter three’s capture drone folded around the tank it held.

Morrow Glass lost power.

Emergency lamps came up along the blister. Rhen hit the manual release on their harness as gravity failed. Saye floated above the cargo board, one hand bleeding where a panel edge had caught it.

“Report.”

“No main bus. Maneuver pods unresponsive. Pressure holding.”

Outside, security cutters tumbled among dark civilian ships. One cutter spun nose over tail with its drive still firing. Its exhaust swept toward a disabled ferry.

Rhen pushed off the command chair and caught the manual thruster cabinet. “Cold-gas controls.”

“They cannot stop that cutter.”

“They can move us out of its plume.”

They tore open the cabinet. Mechanical valves waited behind the dead electronics, each labeled in faded handwriting from a refit twelve years earlier. Saye pulled herself beside Rhen and took the starboard bank.

Through the glass, the prepared rooms drifted between both fleets while Pelagos Station disappeared behind a spreading field of white.

Chapter 23 — The Mercy of Interception

The first collision existed only as a line across Talia’s custody wall.

Emergency power had stripped the room of its camera feed, clock, and door acknowledgment. One display remained alive because custody law required a visible station alarm path. It showed Pelagos approach space in broken segments. Cargo drifted through one segment. Ministry cutters crossed another. Civilian transponders appeared, vanished, then returned with positions displaced by the phase surge.

Talia stood against the wall with her restrained wrist pulled as close to the display as the floor cable allowed.

Cutter six rotated through the projected course of a habitat drum. No impact yet. The field system alternated between two solutions and assigned both a fifty percent confidence mark. A navigation computer would reject them as unusable.

An auditor saw an omitted decision.

She touched the screen. The controls ignored her. “Corridor guard.”

No answer came through the door.

“Sal.”

The station rolled. Her shoulder struck the display. Outside the custody room, something heavy scraped along the corridor and hit a bulkhead. Red emergency light replaced white for three counts.

A second line joined the first.

Cutter six would miss the habitat drum in one solution. In the other, its hot drive crossed a disabled passenger ferry. The ferry’s automatic avoidance had already fired once against false geometry. Its remaining gas could move it out of either danger, not both.

Talia pulled the cable until the wrist cuff cut skin. The floor bolt held.

“Guard, open this door.”

A face appeared at the narrow observation pane. The corridor officer from Isca’s visit had blood along one temple. He worked the manual wheel from outside.

“Pressure seal jammed,” he said through the glass.

“Give me station tactical on the door panel.”

“Custody panels do not receive tactical.”

“Then route approach telemetry as an evacuation hazard.”

His expression said the distinction offended him. Training overcame offense. He opened a maintenance cover beside the door, found the hardline selector, and changed two pins.

The custody display filled with raw approach data.

Talia had requested a map. Instead she received thousands of measurements: inertial readings, drive states, fragment tracks, pressure alarms, field confidence, and carrier cargo coordinates. The abundance concealed danger more efficiently than secrecy could.

“Can you hear me through local intercom?” she asked.

The officer pressed a wall switch. “Yes.”

“Find Sal. Tell him approach traffic has entered a collision cascade.”

“Station command already knows ships are disabled.”

“They do not know the cascade.”

“What difference?”

“One disabled ship drifts. A cascade makes each rescue maneuver endanger the next ship.”

He started to leave.

“Also tell him cutter six has ninety seconds before its drive plume reaches civilian ferry Lark Ascending.”

The officer ran.

Talia sorted the telemetry by consequence. Remove ships with no relative motion. Remove fragments too small to alter a crewed hull before local systems recovered. Flag live drives, pressurized craft, tethered mass, and any object whose position differed across the two field solutions.

Seventeen hazards remained.

A rotating cutter crossed a water tank. The tank struck an alloy anchor in one solution and a medical support vessel in the other. A habitat drum had two taut tethers pulling it toward separate civilian ships. Morrow Glass lacked main power but showed cold-gas pressure. Jori Venn’s ore carrier still had one maneuver bank and too much mass to use it near the loose cargo plane.

No single command network reached them all.

Talia opened the custody intercom. “Any station operator, identify.”

Static answered. Then a maintenance dispatcher said, “Custody drum, clear this channel.”

“Route auditor Talia Sorn. I need external transmission.”

“You are not an auditor.”

“Correct. I need it anyway.”

The dispatcher heard the alarms behind her or saw the tactical designation attached to the data. “External arrays have no synchronized timing.”

“Use local narrowband. Address ships by transponder when they appear.”

“Packets may arrive out of order.”

“Number every instruction. Tell recipients to execute by observed geometry, not receipt time.”

The dispatcher hesitated. “Under whose authority?”

“Yours, until Sal reaches you.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It is the only authority currently connected.”

A channel opened.

Talia sent the first correction to cutter six: kill main drive, vent port maneuver tank, accept stern-first rotation. The order would save Lark Ascending and place the cutter near a habitat drum, but the drum moved slowly enough for later interception.

The cutter rejected her identity key.

“Transmit the geometry without my name,” Talia said.

The dispatcher did.

Cutter six shut down its drive.

Its rotation continued. White exhaust burst from the port side as the maneuver tank vented. The cutter’s nose swung away from the ferry. Lark Ascending drifted through the cooling plume and remained intact.

The new course altered four projections.

Talia rebuilt the hazard list. “Instruction two, Venn Prospect: no thrust. Release alloy tether seven.”

Jori’s carrier answered directly. “Tether seven holds our outer anchor row.”

“It also pulls a habitat shell into cutter six.”

“Release loses eighty tonnes.”

“Release it.”

“Who authorized you?”

“No one.”

A beat passed. Then Jori said, “That sounds familiar.”

Tether seven separated. The alloy block drifted free. Without its pull, the habitat shell rotated above cutter six instead of through it.

Pelagos shook again. The tactical display divided into three star positions, two false and one current, but no instrument identified which one. Talia anchored the geometry to inertial motions measured before the surge. Objects could jump between phase solutions. Momentum still constrained what happened after each jump.

Her old warning to Jun had used that exact principle eleven years earlier. Preserve raw motion before classification. Do not normalize contradictions before their consequence becomes measurable. Talia had signed the warning, then accepted its burial when Jun argued that publication could close the routes.

Now the rule survived only because a custody officer had routed unclean data to a prisoner.

The door wheel moved half a turn.

Sal appeared beyond the glass with two technicians and a hydraulic spreader. His left sleeve had been cut away. A pressure wrap covered his elbow.

“Talia,” he said through the pane, “step back.”

“I cannot.” She lifted the cable.

He looked down, swore, and told a technician to isolate the floor circuit. The spreader’s jaws entered the door seam. Metal bowed inward. Air hissed through the frame.

Talia continued transmitting.

Instruction three sent a compact tug toward cutter three’s disabled capture drone. Instruction four ordered the cutter crew to release the water tank before the drone pulled it into their own antenna spine. Instruction five paired a Ministry rescue pod with Harbor Coast’s medical vessel because neither had enough maneuver gas to reach the injured ferry alone.

The fleets complied unevenly. Security rejected civilian commands. Compact captains distrusted Ministry vectors. Talia stripped source labels from every correction and attached only observations, actions, and measured consequence.

The door opened thirty centimeters.

Sal squeezed through sideways. He carried a manual cuff key in his teeth and a portable command board under one arm. The station lurched while he crossed the room. He caught the table bolt, steadied himself, and unlocked Talia’s wrist.

“You are leaving custody under emergency extraction,” he said.

“For what purpose?”

“Command the rescue.”

“That purpose exceeds your authority.”

“Then file an objection while moving.”

The red cuff mark around her wrist had started bleeding. Sal wrapped it once with gauze from his pocket, handed her the command board, and pushed the bent door wider.

The corridor beyond had lost gravity and half its ceiling. A ventilation panel floated near the inner curve. One technician tied the panel to a rail. The other led them toward station control using a cable clipped along the wall.

Talia hooked her forearm through the cable and pulled herself after Sal. “Where is Isca?”

“Monitored quarters, pressure intact.”

“Maelin?”

“Alive. His cell door opened during the surge. He refused evacuation until someone brought his wrappers.”

“That also sounds familiar.”

They crossed an intersection where two station workers were sealing a coolant line with a clamp meant for a smaller pipe. Talia took the handle long enough for one worker to reset the clamp jaws, then followed Sal. Rescue authority did not make the physical corridor less damaged.

Pelagos control occupied a circular room around the station’s inertial core. Six consoles had gone dark. Operators shared the remaining four. The main star display showed a red disk spread across several incompatible coordinates.

Sal secured Talia beside the tactical console. “You have local approach transmission. Ministry cutters still answer blockade command. Civilian ships answer their captains.”

“Then I have a speaker, not command.”

“You saved three ships with a custody intercom.”

“People followed corrections. That will stop when a correction costs someone more than trust covers.”

As if summoned, cutter nine refused instruction six.

Lieutenant Kale Vent appeared on the tactical channel. “Your vector sends us across compact cargo.”

“It sends you beside a biological tank,” Talia said. “Your current rotation takes your weapons spine through it.”

“We can fire the spine clear.”

“Fragments strike Morrow Glass.”

“Then move the carrier.”

“It has no main bus.”

Kale looked off-screen. “Who placed you in command?”

“Sal extracted me to coordinate rescue.”

“That is not Ministry authority.”

“No.”

He did not execute.

The tactical board updated. Cutter nine’s spine would hit the biological tank in forty-one seconds. If it fired clear, fragments crossed Morrow Glass’s pressure hull. If it held course, the tank ruptured and pushed the cutter toward a tethered drum.

A priority call arrived from Continuity House.

Jun Orra appeared on the only clear screen. Artificial rain crossed the court behind her. Continuity House still possessed gravity, reliable power, and windows that showed one world at a time.

“Commander Varo,” Jun said, “report custody status.”

“Talia Sorn is under my direct control in Pelagos command.”

“You removed her from detention.”

“A collision emergency required her expertise.”

“Return her after stabilization.”

Talia leaned into the camera. “Stabilization requires common command across the blockade.”

Jun’s gaze moved to her. “You have no office and no operational rank.”

“Correct.”

“Yet you are issuing instructions to Ministry vessels.”

“They can reject me. Cutter nine is doing so now.”

Sal put the cutter’s projection beside Jun’s image. Twenty-nine seconds remained.

Jun read it. “Lieutenant Vent, destroy the biological tank before contact.”

“Fragments reach a civilian carrier,” Talia said.

“The carrier entered a prohibited zone.”

“Its crew still requires rescue.”

“You can compel civilian withdrawal if I transfer security authority. All compact vessels to outer holding range, surrender their gate and cargo controls, then cutters follow your vectors.”

Talia saw the offer’s structure at once. Jun would grant command only if rescue delivered the blockade’s original demand. Civilian captains would have to surrender before Ministry crews received Talia’s corrections. The condition converted danger into leverage.

Cutter nine reached twenty seconds.

“No,” Talia said.

Jun’s face remained still. “No to which term?”

“I will not condition rescue on surrender.”

“The civilian fleet created this hazard.”

“The phase surge disabled both fleets.”

“They deployed loose mass inside the exclusion zone.”

“Your cutters held weapons free inside the same zone. Responsibility can wait. Collision cannot.”

Jun leaned toward the screen. “You once signed a warning that recommended sealed handling because uncontrolled action could close the network.”

“I signed the warning. I also signed the burial. Try me for both after we stop ships from hitting each other.”

Ten seconds.

Talia switched to Kale’s channel. “Vent, vent your weapons coolant through the aft purge. It changes your center of rotation by point six degrees. Do not fire the spine.”

“That purge disables us.”

“You are already disabled.”

“Director?” Kale asked.

Jun looked at the projection. For a moment Talia saw the logistics coordinator who had survived Sable inside the director who had built Final Fall: a woman able to count deaths before anyone else accepted the count.

“Vent aft coolant,” Jun said.

Kale executed.

A silver cloud burst from cutter nine. Its rotation changed slowly, then enough. The weapons spine passed beneath the biological tank with less than four meters clearance. The tank’s green work light traveled along the cutter hull and cleared.

Jun did not end the channel.

“Talia Sorn,” she said, “I transfer limited emergency authority for interception, stabilization, and crew recovery within Pelagos approach space. No authority over Final Fall, gate commitment, or civilian cargo disposition. Commander Varo will hold joint custody of each order.”

Sal answered, “Accepted.”

Talia said, “Add no surrender condition.”

Jun’s eyes narrowed.

“Put it in the transfer,” Talia continued. “Every captain needs to know rescue does not purchase their ship.”

The tactical display showed twelve unresolved hazards. Jun could revoke the offer, issue direct commands, and trust obedience to outrun phase distortion. Instead she amended the text.

“Rescue authority carries no surrender condition,” she said. “Transfer authenticated.”

The command board unlocked.

Ministry cutter telemetry aligned with Pelagos local control. Compact captains still required separate consent, so Rhen Tal opened their common channel from Morrow Glass on emergency battery.

“Talia,” Rhen said, “if this ends with my cargo impounded, I will object.”

“Your objections usually arrive complete.”

“What do you need?”

“Cold-gas reserve and the state of your aft truss.”

“Enough gas for three corrections. Truss intact. Saye has a cut hand and strong opinions.”

“Preserve both.”

Rhen relayed civilian assent ship by ship. Sal authenticated Ministry vectors. Talia placed rescue pairs across the two fleets, each pairing a craft with maneuver power to one without it. Cutter nine towed a compact ferry. Jori’s ore carrier used its remaining bank to intercept cutter six after civilians released two more alloy anchors. Harbor Coast took injured security crew because its medical vessel still held pressure.

No symbol on the board retained a pure side.
