A distress packet arrived from cutter three. The capture frame beneath its hull had collapsed during the surge, trapping two security technicians outside. One suit had lost maneuver gas. The cutter could not rotate without crushing them against the water tank still held by its drone.

Harbor Coast’s medical vessel lay closest, but a broken tether crossed its path. Talia assigned Lark Ascending to pull the tether clear.

Hes answered, “My port drum has a failed hinge.”

“You will not move the drum. Release your utility skiff.”

“The skiff carries our only pressure welder.”

“Bring it back after the rescue.”

“If it survives.”

Talia looked at the suit telemetry. One technician had six minutes of reserve gas and a falling temperature. “That condition applies to the technician too.”

Hes launched the skiff.

Its pilot approached the broken tether from below the cargo plane, caught it with a magnetic hook, and pulled until the line cleared Harbor Coast’s bow. The medical vessel fired one short burn. Its nose passed beneath an alloy block with eight meters to spare.

Cutter three’s commander tried to rotate early.

“Hold attitude,” Talia said.

“My crew are outside.”

“Your rotation kills them.”

“The water tank blocks our recovery lock.”

“Release the drone.”

“That transfers twelve tonnes into the medical approach.”

Talia moved the projected tank forward. Its momentum carried it away from Harbor Coast if released within the next eleven seconds. After that, field drift turned it back across the vessel’s path.

“Release on my count. Three. Two. One.”

The drone opened its claws. Receipt One moved free, green light turning slowly across its surface. Harbor Coast slid through the gap. Two medics came outside on lines while the cutter technicians pushed away from the collapsed frame.

The first technician reached a medic. The second spun beyond the line, one arm unresponsive. Harbor Coast could not fire without throwing all four people apart.

“Lark skiff, intercept,” Talia said.

Hes did not argue. The skiff released the broken tether and used its remaining gas in a hard lateral burst. Its pilot caught the spinning technician against the welder rack. Both bodies struck the frame, rebounded, and held because the pilot had wrapped one leg through a restraint before contact.

Suit pressure remained stable.

Cutter three transmitted thanks on the open rescue band. Hes answered, “Return the tank when convenient.”

For several seconds, no one spoke. Then the cutter commander issued a second provisional receipt, this one for custody of an injured civilian skiff pilot until Harbor Coast could bring everyone inside.

Talia added both records to the rescue log. Mercy needed no sentimental label. It needed names, pressures, injuries, and a chain of custody that did not stop at the uniform.

The cascade shrank from twelve hazards to seven, then four. One fragment struck an empty habitat drum and opened its wall. A cutter lost its antenna spine. Morrow Glass spent the last of its cold gas moving a disabled Ministry pod away from an anchor block.

The final danger involved the station itself.

Pelagos had drifted thirty meters from its declared orbit during the surge. A compact service tug approached from one side. A Ministry tug approached from the other. Their separate controllers issued opposite corrections and nearly drove the station harder across its own phase reference.

Talia canceled both burns.

“Link tug controls through local custody,” she said.

The Ministry operator objected. “Civilian keys cannot enter security hardware.”

“Then put both sets in Sal’s board.”

Rhen said, “Compact accepts if the combined vector stays public.”

Sal opened a clean control partition. One Ministry pilot entered thrust limits. The civilian pilot entered tether load. Talia supplied direction. Sal authenticated the burn.

Two tugs fired together.

The station moved through the white field by centimeters. Its false reflections drew closer. One vanished. Then another. The remaining red star settled behind the exclusion petals while pressure alarms across Pelagos changed from continuous to intermittent.

On the approach board, cutters and carriers held a common safe geometry around damaged cargo.

Sal sent the combined control partition from rescue command into the station’s primary system. Civilian assent traveled with Ministry authentication. Neither could overwrite the other.

The console accepted joint control.

Chapter 24 — When the Other Sky Dims

The first district vanished while Isca still had its weather open on her screen.

North Adar had reported a dry dawn, high cloud over the ridge farms, and a pressure drop that would bring rain by evening. Isca had corrected the station model because its borrowed archive still assumed the coronal scar across the continent. She drew the front north by forty kilometers. A green confirmation line came back from Kest-Blue, carrying Nemi’s annotation about school roofs and field cisterns.

Then the map lost North Adar.

No flare crossed the display. No warning color filled the district. Roads, rivers, atmospheric towers, civic relays, homes, and 18,406 registered residents flattened into the neutral gray reserved for areas without coherent return. The weather front continued across the absence for three seconds before Pelagos understood that it no longer had ground beneath the forecast.

Isca pressed her hand to the glass. “Requery every local relay.”

Maelin answered from the phase pit below her. “Already moving.”

Seven black petals of the exclusion array stood beyond the operations windows, each one cutting a wedge from the red dwarf. Their alignment had survived the blockade surge, but their edges trembled against the star. Reflections no longer showed a clean second sky. Kest-Blue constellations arrived in torn segments, bright enough to give Isca a horizon and too unstable to hold it.

A response climbed through the receiver stack. It carried no words, only municipal identifiers. North Adar’s relays answered with impossible variation: present, absent, present, three thousand citizens, no citizens, a reservoir without a town. Maelin killed the requery before its demand dragged more phase load through the district.

“Don’t ask it again,” she said. Her voice came over the local circuit, clipped by static. “We have the answer we can afford.”

Sal Varo’s joint-control officers had turned the old custody gallery into a reception command floor. Ministry uniforms worked beside carrier crews and Pelagos researchers. Red security bands had been stripped from the doors, though their adhesive outlines remained. Everyone could cross the room now. No one could leave the clock.

Isca pulled North Adar from the active queue. The action required her civic relay signature and a receiving-site witness. A gatekeeper from Ossa Habitat supplied the second key with a hand that shook once above the console.

“What does removal mean?” he asked.

“It means we stop spending coherence on addresses that no longer answer.”

“You’re certain none remain?”

“No.” Isca made herself look at him. “I’m certain repeated queries endanger districts that still answer.”

He signed.

Before the queue advanced, Isca copied North Adar’s final weather packet into the civic loss file. The forecast carried no scientific value now, but its municipal signatures identified who had worked the morning shift. She attached the names as witnesses to their own district rather than leaving only a vanished population total.

The loss entered the live census. The total at the top of the room began falling faster than any person could read individual changes. It had started at 9,412,806. For weeks that number had served as proof against ministers who preferred uncertainty. Now proof offered no protection. Each subtraction represented a citizen who had already endured childhood, work, argument, illness, repair, affection, boredom, and weather. The receiver could count what coherence released. It could not tell Isca who had been holding a cup when their kitchen ceased.

Nemi’s channel opened with a six-minute delay.

Her image assembled from horizontal bands. She sat in the emergency classroom at Ilex North, still wearing the yellow neighborhood vest she had used during the southern plague. Behind her, children’s desks had been pushed against the walls. Evacuation numbers covered the lesson board. Someone had drawn both versions of Kest’s moons in blue chalk above the columns.

“Isca,” Nemi said from six minutes ago, “Adar has gone dark. We have moved the east wards into the school shelter. Aru is with me. Tell me which list you trust.”

The channel held her face while Pelagos waited for words Nemi had already finished speaking. Isca hated the delay because it converted every exchange into an argument with the past. She could not interrupt. She could not ask Nemi to move her camera. Every answer arrived after the danger had changed.

Isca keyed her reply. “Trust your paper roster over the municipal feed. Mark each person physically seen. Send children and critical-care cases through Group Orison-Seven. Do not wait for family consolidation. Reception can reunite households after instantiation.”

She almost added that Aru should go first. Nemi had refused that privilege before, and the refusal had carried political sense. One family could not define the rescue. Yet a mother could still want her son alive while she honored everyone else’s children.

Instead Isca said, “Use the same rule for Aru.”

Her message entered the exclusion field. Estimated receipt: five minutes, forty-two seconds.

Talia crossed the command floor carrying three tablets beneath one arm. The white audit collar had gone with her office, replaced by a pressure jacket stained during the blockade rescue. She put the tablets beside Isca without touching the active screen.

“Habitat allocations,” Talia said. “Orison can accept another eighty-two thousand in the first material window if the local gate holds. Kest has released the second half-allotment. Veyr and Lorn are still arguing over atmospheric mass.”

“People first. Infrastructure later.”

“That creates water and sanitation failure inside nine hours.”

“Then send the treatment patterns with the people who know how to run them.”

Talia glanced at North Adar’s gray outline. “Agreed. I needed you to say which expertise travels with which district.”

Isca opened the social continuity register. The Ministry had once asked her to prove personhood through memories, as if a marriage or private joke carried more reality than a municipal pump schedule. Now the rescue needed every kind of continuity. Midwives traveled with clinic patterns. Mechanics accompanied transit diagrams. Teachers carried child rosters whose guardians might arrive through another gate. Nobody fit cleanly inside a cargo category, and the system had no time to invent dignified labels.

She marked Ilex North for Orison because its teachers had plague-triage experience. Nemi would object to being classified as useful. Isca allowed herself one breath of bitter amusement and continued.

Across the room, a warning tone descended by half steps. Pelagos Array Four had lost another fraction of lock. Maelin climbed the ladder from the pit, moving with more caution than usual. Blood darkened one sleeve where the blockade surge had thrown her against a bus rail. She refused the medic who followed her.

“The automatic phase reference is drifting with the branch load,” Maelin said. “It keeps averaging present Pelagos with Kest-Blue. That made sense when we believed one side contained noise.”

Talia looked toward the black petals. “Can you separate them?”

“Software will keep choosing the larger stable solution. That means us.”

“Can you stop it?” Isca asked.

Maelin gave her a narrow look. “I can give it a reference that refuses to average.”

She laid a physical control wafer beside Isca’s hand. Its casing had been opened so many times that no two screws matched. Four fiber leads trailed from one edge.

“Manual?” Talia said.

“Pelagos manual. Not committee manual.”

Sal approached from the security tier. “State the risk in words my officers can enforce.”

“If anyone disconnects me after I enter the pit, the reference collapses. If the pit floods, burns, or loses pressure, the same result follows. If I become confused, someone up here must compare my calls against Isca’s branch clock.”

“You should not be the sole point.”

“I designed the receiver’s oldest layer. Nobody else can feel where its compensators lie to themselves.”

Isca heard the pride and the shame bound inside that sentence. “You said feel.”

“I said it for speed. The instruments show it. My hands know which instrument lies first.” Maelin pushed the wafer closer. “Your clock supplies the external check. Mine holds the local line.”

Another district on the south coast began flickering. The live census shed sixty-two names, recovered forty, then lost nine hundred at once.

Arguments ended around them.

Maelin took the ladder down. Sal assigned two officers to the pit access with orders to obey Maelin’s safety technician, not Ministry command. Talia placed the manual hold in the joint-control record. Isca kept working because witnessing preparation could not substitute for rescue.

The next message from Nemi arrived eight minutes after the prior image. This time the classroom windows showed noon-darkness. Kest-Blue’s sun still stood above the horizon, yet its light had thinned to the color of old metal. Children moved behind her in paired lines. Adults held paper cards over their heads so cameras could read household groups when the municipal network failed.

“We received your rule,” Nemi said. “You always choose the list with handwriting.”

Her smile lasted less than a second.

“Aru has Group Seven on his coat. He wants to know whether your Orison has rain.”

Isca looked toward Talia.

“Young park,” Talia said quietly. “Wind system unfinished. They seeded cloud water last week, but no one can promise rain.”

Isca opened the reply. “Tell him Orison is still learning. He can help them measure it.”

She stopped recording, erased the sentence, and began again.

“Aru, Orison has young trees and bad wind controls. Its first rain may go sideways. Take your moon card. I will find you there.”

For Nemi she added, “Your group follows him.”

The answer would reach a woman already making another decision.

Reception windows began opening across the Concord. Each appeared on the wall as a narrow white bracket beside a site name. Ossa received forty-seven water engineers and their households. Aster Deep received the first southern-plague clinic, equipment patterns arriving ahead of patients by fourteen seconds. Orison Habitat accepted three hundred children from a coastal ward whose foundations had begun alternating with empty rock.

Instantiation did not resemble arrival at North Anchor. Prepared matter rose inside reception frames as colorless vapor, found edges, then acquired weight. Medical teams confirmed breath before names. The process moved too slowly for spectacle and too fast for grief. Isca watched remote feeds only when a civic dispute required her. Her task involved deciding who entered which bracket while the brackets still existed.

A representative from current Kest called to protest that Orison-Seven had exceeded its assigned mass.

“You have six minutes to release more,” Isca told him.

“We already surrendered half our allotment.”

“Then choose which prepared machine waits. I have a school shelter with eleven hundred people and a failing southern feeder.”

“The machine produces insulin cultures.”

Isca closed her eyes once. Current lives stood behind that answer. The old habit of exclusion would have made the choice by hiding one side.

“Delay the decorative structural print at Orison’s west park,” she said. “Keep the culture unit. Strip three unpressurized service cars for equal margin.”

The representative checked the numbers. “That leaves the west park unreachable.”

“For tonight.”

He released the cars.

A distress marker appeared inside Ossa’s first reception group before Isca could return to the Ilex roster. Forty-one people had resolved in the correct frame, but their clinic core had arrived as disconnected pattern blocks. The receiving medics could identify pumps, sterile tubing, and sealed drug stocks. None of them knew which pieces belonged together.

“Ossa requests a Kest-Blue clinical engineer,” Talia said.

Isca searched the active civic register. The nearest qualified engineer waited in a coastal group assigned to Veyr. Moving her would split a household of five across systems.

“Ask her,” Isca said.

“We have ninety seconds before Ossa opens the next frame.”

“Then give her ninety seconds to decide.”

The engineer appeared through a damaged branch packet. Her name was Dera Osra. Two children crowded beside her while an older man held the household’s paper identifiers. Isca explained the equipment failure without calling it duty.

“If you divert to Ossa, your household keeps the Veyr assignment,” she said. “Earliest reunion depends on slow transit after the network stabilizes. We cannot promise a date.”

Dera looked at the older man. The field delay let Isca watch their mouths move without sound before the return circuit caught up.

“My father cannot manage both children through instantiation,” Dera said.

Ossa’s controller cut in. “We can receive the household together. I will release staff quarters for five.”

“You do not have authorization to change civic allocation,” Talia warned.

“I have a broken clinic and forty-one people breathing beside equipment we cannot assemble.”

Isca checked Ossa’s mass. Five additional bodies fit. Their household patterns did not include the workshop equipment assigned to Veyr, but that loss could wait.

“Keep them together,” she said. “Move the entire Osra household to Ossa. Mark the workshop claim for later review.”

Dera gave no grateful speech. She put one hand on each child, told her father to hold the paper cards high, and stepped into the coastal frame.

At Ossa, she resolved on her side with one knee against the floor. Her younger child appeared several meters away and screamed until the older man formed between them. Dera found the clinic core before reception medics finished checking her pulse. She opened the first pattern block, rejected two tools they offered, and made them rotate the pump housing so she could see its branch-standard fittings.

The clinic came online seven minutes later. Its first patient belonged to current Ossa, a gate worker burned during the rehearsal. Its second had arrived from Kest-Blue with a fever. Both entered the same treatment queue.

Talia added the changed household allocation to the public record. “Veyr will object that we took skilled labor promised to its workshop.”

“They should object,” Isca said. “Dera should be able to answer after her family is safe.”

That difference mattered. Emergency triage could move a citizen without converting the movement into permanent ownership. Every reception decision created a claim that would outlive the collapsing field. Isca refused to let urgency close those claims in silence.

By the middle of Pelagos night, the second sky dimmed enough that no reflection held a full constellation. Maelin’s calls came from the pit at regular intervals.

“Reference plus point zero two.”

A technician answered, “Confirmed against branch clock.”

“Array Four compensator falling behind.”

“Drive raised two percent.”

No poetry entered the exchange. Maelin named deviations. The room corrected what machinery could still correct. Every call bought time measured in people rather than minutes.

Nemi’s third image arrived with damage across one side. She had moved into the shelter corridor. Concrete walls pressed close behind her, painted with handprints from generations of children. Aru stood at her shoulder wearing a red coat, his moon card tied to the front with string. He tried to appear solemn and failed when he saw Isca’s frozen image on the return monitor.

“Mama says you’re six minutes away,” he said.

Nemi pulled him close before the transmitter could rotate away. “Group Seven loads now. They have one adult for every twelve children. I am assigning myself to Group Eight.”

Isca leaned toward a conversation that had already happened. “No.”

On screen, Nemi continued without hearing her.

“The east stair has families below it, and the local count dropped their block. Someone has to close the school shelter after we clear it. If I leave with Seven, those people miss the next bracket.”

Aru looked from Nemi to the transmitter. “Are you coming after?”

“Yes,” Nemi said.

She did not promise how soon. Isca knew the distinction and loved her for refusing the lie.

Nemi fastened the child’s transit tab at his collar. “Go with Teacher Seli. Find the place where the rain goes sideways.”

Aru pressed his palm to the camera. The image held his hand larger than his face.

Isca placed her palm against the Pelagos glass. Six minutes and a failing star separated the surfaces.

“Group Seven entering reception frame,” an Orison controller announced.

The remote feed showed children forming inside gray vapor. Some arrived standing. Others collapsed as local gravity took them. Teacher Seli appeared with one arm around nothing, cried out, then found the child who resolved beside her. Names rolled down the civic register.

Aru Ren appeared eighty-third.

His red coat acquired color. The moon card hung from its string. He stumbled into a medic, twisted free, and demanded the transmitter before his lungs had finished their first breath in the present.

Isca could not speak to him yet. Orison’s route had to close before a narrow packet could travel. She marked him received with her own key.

Behind the relief came the empty space where Nemi’s name should have stood.

Group Eight lost its alignment.

Maelin called from the pit. “I can hold Ilex for one more reception bracket. Not two.”

Isca checked the shelter roster as Nemi’s next delayed packet began assembling. East-stair names filled the lower half. Nemi had added each by hand.

Talia stood ready at the allocation controls. “Which site?”

“Orison,” Isca said. “Keep the family route together.”

“Orison needs equal margin.”

Sal’s voice came from the security board. “We still have two disabled cutters in the outer cradle. Strip them.”

“They belong to Ministry custody,” an officer said.

Sal removed their claim with his command key. “Not anymore.”

The final Ilex bracket opened.

Nemi’s image cleared. Dust streaked her yellow vest. The shelter corridor behind her stood empty, doors open to each room. She held the paper roster against the camera.

“East stair accounted for,” she said. “School shelter closing now.”

A light failed above her. Farther down the corridor, daylight turned gray.

Nemi folded the roster into her vest, took the manual seal in both hands, and pulled the shelter door shut between Kest-Blue and the dimming sky.

Chapter 25 — Every Gate at Once

Forty-seven gates had never obeyed the same clock.

Talia saw the disagreement spread across the joint-control wall in amber fractions. Arclight ran nine microseconds ahead of Pelagos. Ossa Habitat corrected too aggressively, crossed the reference, and came back three microseconds late. Kest North Anchor held within tolerance until an arriving medical train heated its timing racks. Two outer gates still displayed conditional authority. A third remained silent.

Any ordinary crossing could tolerate those differences. Reciprocal Routing could not. At commitment, every gate had to accept a share of exclusion load, preserve it through the rotating veil ledger, and release prepared margin to the reception sites. A late gate would push its burden into its neighbors. An early one would instantiate matter against a history the rest of the network had not yet agreed to keep.

The result would not merely fail. It would choose victims by clock error.

“Reset the rehearsal,” Talia said.

A controller beside her did not move. “We have twenty-eight minutes before Pelagos loses the next branch band.”

“I can see the loss forecast.”

“Then we cannot afford another rehearsal.”

“We cannot afford a commitment that breaks at Gate Thirty-Nine.”

Talia enlarged the operational trace. She had no Ministry collar, office, or statutory power. Limited emergency authority gave her command only over the rescue opened by the blockade surge. Thirty-one gatekeepers had signed the first ballot without conditions. Five had attached local protections. Nine had refused, and two remained silent. Since that tally, local mandates had turned all nine refusals into operational assent, three conditional gates had cleared, and Grey Perihelion’s missing key had been recovered. Two conditional gates and silent Hadran remained outside the sequence. The plan still named all forty-seven because physics did not recognize abstention.

Sal Varo stood at the security tier with Jun’s transfer order visible above his station. “Which gate can we prove without it?”

“None,” Talia said. “We prove what happens with everyone inside.”

He accepted the answer without liking it. “What do you need?”

“A load small enough to reverse. Something the conditional keepers can watch cross their own instruments.”

Maelin’s voice rose from the phase pit. Static roughened every third word. “You have nine hundred kilograms of unassigned prepared mass at Veyr. Send it around the veil rotation without instantiation.”

“That consumes our reserve margin.”

“For ninety seconds.”

“If a keeper blocks release, Veyr loses it.”

“Then Veyr learns what conditional means under load.”

Talia looked at the gatekeeper window. Veyr’s senior operator, Damos Kesh, had agreed only if Reciprocal Routing showed no measurable reduction in local food traffic during the first week. His condition could not survive the protocol’s premise. Everyone would lose capacity.

“Damos,” she said, “I need your prepared margin for a closed rotation.”

His image appeared beneath a hard white star. “Our assembly prohibited transfer to branch reception until local guarantees hold.”

“This will not instantiate anyone.”

“Then it proves nothing about reception.”

“It proves whether your gate can preserve an exclusion burden without dumping it into Pelagos.”

Damos glanced off screen. Advisors moved as reflections in his console. “What risk reaches Veyr?”

“Nine hundred kilograms become unavailable for ninety seconds. If your release fails, the mass remains in your frame until we unwind it manually.”

“You said unavailable. Not lost.”

“I said what the procedure supports. I cannot promise the unwind succeeds against a failed gate.”

Silence crossed the channel. Talia did not fill it with persuasion.

Damos entered his key.

The rehearsal began at low power. Forty-five gates accepted the rotating load. Veyr’s margin vanished from its local account and appeared as a moving white segment across the public veil ledger. Ossa held it. Arclight held it. Kest took the burden while still feeding current-world cargo through a separate lane. The segment reached Gate Thirty-Nine, where the conditional keeper had capped reciprocal intake at half the required value.

Pressure rose across the neighboring ledgers.

“Do not compensate,” Talia ordered.

A former Ministry controller stared at her. “We can carry the excess at Pelagos.”

“That recreates the dump.”

“For four seconds.”

“The old system began with exceptions measured in seconds.”

Gate Thirty-Nine’s operator heard her. His local trace showed the unaccepted burden pushing backward toward a water convoy already in arrival preparation. If he held the cap, the convoy would miss its window. No moral argument had moved him during the ballot. A queue of four hundred thousand liters did.

He released the limit.

The white segment passed. Veyr recovered its mass at ninety-three seconds, three seconds late and intact.

Damos looked down at the returned account. “Our food lane stayed open.”

“At half safe capacity,” Talia said. “That will not improve because we want it to.”

His expression tightened. “Veyr commits for the emergency operation. Permanent authority still belongs to our assembly.”

“Recorded.”

One conditional marker turned green. Its raw trace remained visible beneath the color, available to every assembly watching.

The silent gate belonged to a mining system called Hadran Reach. Its local authority had fractured between a gate union and an appointed keeper who had locked himself out of public channels. Talia had no image, only raw clock telemetry and an intake shutter refusing remote command.

Rhen’s voice came from Morrow Glass, berthed against Pelagos with its crew aboard. “Hadran loads forty percent of Arclight’s industrial catalyst. They go dark whenever the capital asks whether their equipment meets code.”

“Can you reach their union circuit?”

“I can reach a mechanic who owes me money.”

“Does the mechanic hold gate authority?”

“No. She holds the cooling bypass. Authority gets conversational when racks heat.”

Talia turned to Sal. “Is that coercion?”

“If you order sabotage, yes.”

Rhen gave a short laugh over the channel. “Nobody said sabotage. Hadran’s cooling plant has a public test mode. It displays every sealed gate process to the local union board before cycling. Their keeper used secrecy to keep his shutter closed. We make the closed process visible.”

Talia considered the line between operational proof and threat. The old Ministry had treated visibility as a hazard. Yet forcing a cooling cycle during commitment could kill people if the bypass failed.

“Run the display test only,” she said. “No change to flow. No remote actuation.”

Rhen contacted the mechanic.

Hadran Reach appeared twelve seconds later as an industrial diagnostic. Coolant pressure, shutter position, reciprocal intake readiness, and the appointed keeper’s unsigned refusal filled the union channel. The refusal cited contamination by anomalous persons. Beneath it, the gate model showed what would happen if Hadran stayed outside: industrial catalyst traffic stopped in both directions because no legal route could isolate one system from the rotation.

The gate union voted on the same screen. Talia watched authorization build from maintenance keys, cargo keys, habitat safety keys, and finally the emergency civic key held by Hadran’s elected forewoman.

The appointed keeper tried to revoke the display. His command failed because he had no union maintenance signature.

Hadran’s shutter opened.

Forty-seven green markers stood on the wall.

Talia did not trust their color. She ordered each gate to expose the instrument beneath its readiness signal. The wall changed from forty-seven simple confirmations into a field of oscillator traces, intake temperatures, shutter latencies, and local authority chains. Three sites had rounded their timing difference before publication. Arclight had hidden a thermal correction inside an approved maintenance envelope. Kest North Anchor reported a cradle vibration that repeated every seventh cycle.

“Green means within tolerance,” an Arclight controller said.

“Green means your display chose a category,” Talia replied. “I need the measurements that created it.”

Opening those measurements cost another minute. It also showed that Hadran’s shutter reached full travel slower than its model predicted. Under branch load, that lag would arrive at neighboring gates as a false reduction in reciprocal intake.

Rhen recognized the shape first. “Worn drive screw. Their display test measured command time, not physical opening.”

The Hadran forewoman called to someone off screen. A mechanic entered the gate housing with a vibration probe strapped to her wrist. She placed it against the shutter rail while the union cycled the mechanism at one-tenth power.

“Twenty-one milliseconds from command to motion,” she reported. “Model says eight.”

“Can you replace the drive?” Talia asked.

“Not before Pelagos loses another district.”

“Can you publish the lag as part of your clock?”

The mechanic looked offended. “I can measure it better than your capital model did.”

She ran three cycles. The first started at twenty-one milliseconds, the second at nineteen, the third at twenty-three after the housing warmed. Rather than averaging them into a reassuring value, she entered the full range. Neighboring gates adjusted their hold windows around the worst result.

Kest’s repeated vibration required a different correction. North Anchor Control wanted to filter it as known cradle wear. Talia refused. A cargo worker carried a portable sensor onto the graphite ring, knelt beside the bearing that had predicted itself during Morrow Glass’s first arrival, and recorded the flaw directly. The timing spine learned its seven-cycle rhythm as measured noise rather than pretending the gate ran clean.

At Ossa, intake temperature had climbed because reception crews parked medical heaters beside a timing cabinet. A local nurse objected when gate staff ordered the heaters moved.

“We have branch children coming through that frame,” she said. “The hall is nine degrees.”

“Your heaters pull our oscillator off reference.”

“Then warm the children somewhere else before you take the only heat.”

The dispute reached Talia because neither side held authority over the other. She asked Orison’s engineers for the arrangement they used around Frame Seven. They had routed warm air through a fabric duct from outside the timing floor. Ossa’s cargo union cut two clean grain sleeves into an improvised duct and moved the heaters behind a shielding wall. Intake temperature fell without leaving the hall cold.

Operational proof changed more than gate votes. It forced each system to reveal the maintenance, labor, and local judgment hidden beneath a synchronized line. Reciprocal Routing could not make those differences disappear. It could only keep one authority from concealing them until failure chose the cost.

Talia froze the instrument wall into the public record. “These traces govern commitment. If a local display disagrees, raw measurement wins and the disagreement stays visible.”

One by one, the gatekeepers signed that rule. Hadran’s civic key arrived last, assembled from the same union authorities that had opened its shutter.

No one cheered. The branch census had lost another two thousand citizens during the rehearsal.

“Commitment in six minutes,” Talia said. “Publish every local condition. No hidden override authority. If a gate leaves tolerance, its neighbors hold load rather than diverting to Pelagos. Reception sites confirm medical floor, atmosphere, and mass.”

Names answered around the Concord.

Orison Habitat reported first. Its unfinished ring filled three remote windows: reception halls built from cargo membranes, young parkland under work lights, clinics arranged beside frames that had never held a person. Aru Ren sat on an examination bench wrapped in a silver blanket. A technician tried to move him from the control view. He held up his moon card until she let him stay.

“Orison ready for primary civic groups,” the habitat controller said. “Local atmosphere stable. West park transit closed. Water reserve at nineteen hours under maximum intake.”

“Confirm no ornamental printing.”

“Confirmed. All prepared matter assigned to citizens, clinical infrastructure, sanitation, and food systems.”

Other sites followed. Ossa offered its unbuilt third ring. Veyr opened two agricultural cylinders intended for later settlement. Kest had stripped service craft and postponed reconstruction to create equal margin. Across the network, empty rooms ceased to count as speculative capacity. They had floors, pressures, doors, and people waiting with blankets.

Maelin called from below. “Reference stable within point zero four. Do not ask me for point zero three.”

Talia did not. “Branch civic authority?”

Isca answered from the opposite console. Fatigue had hollowed her face, but her voice remained precise. “Kest-Blue authorizes distributed instantiation under the public allocation record. No overwrite. No involuntary household sorting except immediate safety. Local councils retain claims after reception.”

“Present civic authority?”

A chorus of assembly signatures entered the ledger. Jun’s Final Fall order remained active above them, a red command waiting on a clock. Jun herself had not entered joint control.

Talia opened a direct channel to Continuity House. “Director, reciprocal commitment will conflict with Final Fall in four minutes.”

Jun appeared in the rain court chamber. Artificial rain struck the dark windows behind her. “I know.”

“Will Ministry systems interfere?”

“I have issued no new interference order.”

“That leaves the standing one.”

“It remains lawful.”

“Law cannot execute both.”

Jun’s eyes moved toward a display Talia could not see. “Then your operation will expose which authority the gates obey.”

It sounded like refusal. It also sounded like a door left unlocked. Talia wanted certainty from the woman who had taught her to demand it from everyone else.

“You can cancel Final Fall,” she said.

“Not before I know your synchronization holds.”

“By then the conflict may decide for you.”

“Yes.” Jun did not look away. “That consequence belongs in my record.”

The channel closed.

Commitment fell under one minute.

Every gate shifted from local time to the Pelagos branch clock. Figures that had wandered by microseconds drew toward a common line. The timing spine distributed corrections through physical oscillators at each gate. It did not send one command and assume obedience. Each site measured its neighbor, published the difference, and adjusted only after the difference appeared in the common ledger.

Observable effect first, Talia thought. No office got to hide the contradiction inside a model.

“Thirty seconds,” Sal announced.

Reception frames darkened. Cargo traffic across the Concord entered temporary hold. Ships already in arrival preparation backed out or committed to slow local berths. On Kest, an insulin shipment cleared its lane five seconds before shutdown. At Ossa, a food convoy missed the boundary and remained safely at origin.

“Ten.”

Talia placed both hands on the audit rail.

At zero, forty-seven gates accepted reciprocal load.

Pelagos changed color.

The red dwarf remained red, but the exclusion petals no longer cut pure black shapes across it. Lines of branch information moved outward through them, divided among gates instead of compressed into the reserve. The operation wall filled with white routes. Each route carried a clock trace, a veil share, a receiving-site bracket, and a live census.

Orison’s first large frame activated.

Prepared vapor climbed in ranks. A transit worker formed from boots upward, fell to both knees, and reached for the woman resolving beside him. A clinic bed arrived occupied. Two municipal water cores appeared with technicians holding their manual checks against their chests. Medical teams moved only after civic registrars called names.

Isca listened to the delayed branch roster and answered each dispute. “That household belongs in Ossa, not Orison. Keep the southern dialysis group with its plant. Do not separate the Ilex teachers from their paper census.”

The network held for eleven seconds.

At twelve, Gate Eighteen reported a frequency slip.

At thirteen, the timing spine between Pelagos and Morrow Route K-19 went dark.

No explosion marked the failure. One line disappeared from the wall. Neighboring clocks began separating at less than a microsecond per cycle, a change too small for any human sense and large enough to make the next instantiation bracket lethal.

“Freeze reception,” Talia said.

Orison stopped midway through a group. The people already coherent remained. Prepared vapor collapsed back into holding tanks before incomplete bodies could form. Across other sites, controllers closed brackets in ordered sequence.

“What failed?” Sal asked.

A timing engineer expanded the route. “Physical carrier oscillator at K-19. Blockade surge damaged the remote coupling. We had continuity during rehearsal because no branch mass crossed that spine.”

“Replace it.”

“The spare cannot acquire the live reference without a transit event.”

Talia looked toward Maelin’s pit. “Can Pelagos inject the clock through the field?”

“No,” Maelin said. Her breath came ragged through the circuit. “A packet carries the value after measurement. We need an oscillator that experiences both sides of commitment. Physical clock, live crossing.”

Every ordinary route sat closed under Reciprocal Routing. Reopening one through standard authorization would require hours and a Council vote. Kest-Blue did not have hours. The gray districts on Isca’s map continued spreading while reception waited.

Rhen had already opened Morrow Glass’s raw clock file.

The thirty-year-old carrier had preserved the double arrival instead of allowing Ministry normalization. Its oscillator still carried the eleven-second fracture as a physical calibration scar. Talia saw the answer before the engineers finished modeling it.

“No branch dump,” she said. “The crossing must use the accordant pair and preserve both exclusion states.”

“That route has never been flown,” the timing engineer said.

“Neither had Reciprocal Routing.”

Sal looked from Talia to the carrier feed. “Crew risk?”

Rhen answered. “Too high for passengers. Manageable through commitment if we evacuate before terminal lock.”

“You cannot evacuate during a crossing.”

“We can leave after the clock takes origin state and before the carrier enters full mass commitment. The ship goes on without us.”

Talia studied the route geometry. A physical clock could ride the prepared history through the failed link, but someone had to hold the carrier on line until origin commitment. Pelagos then had to catch the new reference while Maelin maintained the branch.

Rhen touched the worn console of Morrow Glass. “Stop auditing my face, Talia. Give me the route.”

“I am auditing the procedure.”

“Then write fast.”

She created an emergency crossing record in the public veil ledger. It named the ship, the risk, the absent branch dump, the evacuation boundary, and the authority nobody fully possessed.

Only Morrow Glass could carry a live clock through the failed route. Beyond the operations glass, its scarred cargo spine released from Pelagos and turned toward the dark arrival ring.

Chapter 26 — The Last Honest Crossing

Morrow Glass left Pelagos with no cargo except a clock and thirty years of repairs.

Rhen felt each repair as the carrier cleared the station cradle. Port truss complained through a welded collar replaced at Kest six winters ago. The command blister seal whistled near the lower hinge. An old thermal patch behind the navigation rack gave off the hot-metal smell it produced whenever drive output passed sixty percent. Those faults belonged to one ship. They did not need a Ministry certificate to agree on their history.

The raw clock occupied the center of the cargo spine inside a shock cage. It had survived the first double arrival because Rhen had hidden its storage crystal from normalization. Now Maelin’s technicians had opened the casing and connected its oscillator directly to the carrier’s mass commitment bus. The clock would not receive a timestamp through a packet. It would measure origin, crossing, and arrival with the same piece of vibrating quartz.

“Range to commitment?” Rhen asked.

Sava, their engineer, checked the line. “Eleven minutes. Evacuation window closes at three twenty.”

“Say it as a ship fact.”

“We have seven minutes and forty seconds to get everybody except you into Pelagos cutters.”

“Better.”

Sava did not smile. Eight crew remained aboard, each one required to bring a stubborn system through origin-state capture. Rhen had cut the roster from twenty-six by force of captain’s authority and several insults. Nobody needed to ride a scarred carrier into an untested route merely because it had once carried their bunk.

Outside the forward glass, the arrival ring looked black against the red dwarf. This crossing began at Pelagos, ran through the failed K-19 timing link, and committed toward North Anchor at Kest. No schedule supported it. No destination cargo request justified its mass. Reciprocal Routing had prepared two honest states: one where Morrow Glass arrived and another where it did not. The protocol would preserve the unchosen state instead of grinding it into the reserve.

That distinction comforted physicists. Rhen still had to fly the ship.

“Clock cage locked,” Sava said.

“Show me the physical catches.”

“We checked them twice.”

“Show me.”

They left the blister together. The central passage carried handprints in grease, handwritten maintenance tags, and the shallow deck dents made by cargo boots over three decades. Empty crew compartments stood open on both sides. Personal kits had gone to the cutters. A paper drawing from Rhen’s niece remained taped beside the galley vent because nobody had remembered it until the ship began moving.

At the cargo spine, Sava pulled each cage handle under Rhen’s eyes. Four catches held. The clock’s service light blinked at a rate almost identical to the pulse in Rhen’s wrist. A fiber ran from the casing to the commitment bus. Another returned raw frequency to Pelagos. Nothing about it looked worth a carrier.

“Arrival pickup knows where to find it?” Rhen asked.

“North Anchor has an armored extraction rig on cradle two. If the ship resolves outside half a meter, they lose the rig.”

“Then we arrive inside half a meter.”

“If it resolves inside the cradle wall?”

Rhen tapped the cage. “Then the clock still experiences arrival before the wall breaks it. Timing gets the reference.”

Sava studied their face. “You say that as if it makes the rest acceptable.”

“No. I say it because the job has an order.”

A Pelagos cutter matched velocity alongside the starboard lock. Rhen sent the two cargo hands across first, followed by the medic and the timing technician. Each transfer altered the carrier’s balance. Morrow Glass had hauled grain, pressure tanks, seed vaults, machine parts, and medical cultures. It disliked emptiness. Rhen compensated with water ballast until the drive vibration settled underfoot.

Talia’s voice entered the command channel. “Public crossing record is open. Forty-seven gatekeepers acknowledge the route. Hadran Reach entered three seconds late but has accepted correction.”

“Any hidden override?”

“No.”

“Final Fall?”

“Still active. Jun has not executed its terminal key.”

Rhen looked at the black ring ahead. “I asked about things that can hit my ship.”

“If the order executes during commitment, Pelagos decoheres the branch reference. Your arrival pair loses accord.”

“There it is.”

“I am working on authority.”

“You do that. I’ll work on mass.”

At six minutes, the carrier’s origin field touched the ring. Frost spread across the outer trusses even though no atmosphere surrounded them. Rhen felt the first pull as pressure behind the eyes. The ship remained physically at Pelagos, but the gate had begun preparing Kest histories around its declared mass.

Sava returned to the blister after sending the other engineer across. “Drive can hold without me until release.”

“Go.”

“I can stay through clock capture.”

“No.”

“You stayed for my brother’s feeder craft.”

“That gave you a debt, not a vote.” Rhen pointed toward the lock status. “Take the cutter.”

Sava unhooked their maintenance key from the console. For years it had hung on a blue carabiner beside the thermal controls. They put it down instead of taking it.

“You’ll need manual access when the bus drops.”

Rhen closed a hand around the key. “I’ll give it back.”

“Liar.”

“Engineer.”

Sava went.

Before sealing the starboard lock, Rhen walked the evacuation list against each departing beacon. The medic had reached Cutter One with the portable trauma rack. Two cargo hands occupied Cutter Two beside the timing technician. Both engineers registered in Cutter Three. Pelagos control tried to count Sava twice because their suit tag and maintenance key still reported from different places.

“Delete the key from personnel tracking,” Rhen said.

A security clerk answered, “Ministry inventory binds tools to assigned crew.”

“Your Ministry no longer owns the ship, the tool, or my engineer.”

Sal came onto the circuit. “Key reclassified as carrier equipment. Count corrected.”

Eight evacuation beacons moved beyond the truss line. Rhen compared faces against names anyway. Software had once called Isca excess mass. They would not let a clean total replace looking.

Cutter Two reported an imbalance. One cargo hand had loaded three personal cases despite the one-bag rule, and the cutter pilot could not trim without wasting fuel needed for rescue. Rhen opened the case manifest. Clothes filled the first. Family keepsakes filled the second. The third held handwritten maintenance books from Morrow Glass, every pressure test and field repair going back to the prior captain.

“Keep the books,” Rhen said. “Dump my locker instead.”

The cargo hand protested. “Your locker has the ownership papers.”

“The registry has copies.”

“It has Ministry copies.”

Rhen looked toward the paper drawing beside the galley vent. A ship existed in more than its title, yet paper could not justify risking the rescue cutter. They emptied the locker remotely. Spare clothes, two bottles saved for a route anniversary, and a carved Kest navigation piece tumbled into the disposal mesh. The ownership envelope weighed almost nothing. Rhen told the hand to fold it inside the first maintenance book.

Cutter Two balanced within tolerance.

The drawing presented a smaller problem. Rhen pulled it from the wall, creased it once, and slid it into the emergency suit pocket. The picture showed Morrow Glass with four wings and windows along a truss that held no atmosphere. Their niece had given every crew member a different colored face. No gate mathematics could use it. That made it easier to keep.

Pelagos requested confirmation that no recoverable cargo remained aboard.

“Clock only,” Rhen said.

“What about the ship’s reactor stock?”

“Required for crossing.”

“Water ballast?”

“Required for trim.”

“Habitable equipment?”

Rhen looked through empty compartments. Bunks, galley heaters, sanitation loops, and pressure membranes could have served Orison. Removing them now would change declared mass after origin preparation and invalidate the physical clock relation.

“Recoverable after commitment means not recoverable,” they said. “Record the equipment as sacrificed to route synchronization.”

The clerk paused before entering the loss. Old transit accounting treated a carrier as a productive asset until salvage declared otherwise. This ship had not yet broken. Everyone simply knew the procedure required leaving it inside a crossing no hull had been designed to survive.

Rhen signed the sacrifice line with a grease-stained thumb. The public record accepted it beside prepared mass, crew risk, and clock custody.

North Anchor publicly and formally acknowledged the loss before the crossing began. Its dockmaster reserved a disposal orbit and assigned no salvage crew until the physical clock cleared the cradle. That order removed one final temptation to save the hull before the route.

Crew count reached one.

The empty blister amplified every relay click. Rhen strapped into the command cradle and moved control to the right-hand board. The evacuation spindle waited behind them: a blunt pressure pod designed to carry two injured crew from a dead ship. Pelagos had fitted it with a drive pack and none of the shielding Rhen would have chosen. To make the clock cross, Rhen had to remain aboard through origin-state capture, let automatic commitment take the carrier, then eject before its mass entered the arrival pair. Leave too early, and the clock lost its live relation to the ship’s navigation oscillator. Leave too late, and the spindle joined the crossing as undeclared mass.

There would be forty-eight milliseconds between those states.

“Maelin,” Rhen said, “tell me your reference survives the ejection transient.”

Her reply came from the phase pit with machinery striking in the background. “It survives if your spindle clears the hull by three meters before terminal commit.”

“My spindle manual says two point four.”

“Then improve on the manual.”

“Nice to hear you too.”

A warning light announced coolant loss in the phase pit. Rhen heard a technician call for pit evacuation.

Maelin answered away from the channel. “Seal line three. Keep the pumps on reference bus.”

Talia broke in. “Maelin, report.”

“Array Four shed a coolant jacket. The pit can hold local phase or local temperature. I chose phase.”

“How long?”

“Long enough if Rhen stops talking.”

Rhen placed one hand on the release paddle. “That remains our shared plan.”

At four minutes, North Anchor acquired their test carrier. Its signal arrived as a faint second horizon across the forward glass. Kest appeared where Pelagos should have remained, green south, burned north, two small moons set against the carrier’s present view of a red star. The old injury inside the raw clock woke. Its trace split by eleven seconds and then drew toward Maelin’s branch reference.

The first double arrival had thrown Rhen across this same blister while twenty-six people shouted damage reports. They remembered rescuing Valeh’s cargo technician from a folded truss. They remembered the unfamiliar woman in observation bay three. Isca had entered their ship as mass excess, and Rhen had worried about cultures warming while a civilization pressed unseen against the hull.

This time no one had hidden the excluded state.

The public veil ledger showed both arrival histories. One carried Morrow Glass to Kest. One left North Anchor empty. Reciprocal Routing assigned preservation shares across every gate. Nothing fell toward Pelagos as waste.

“Three minutes thirty,” Talia said.

The last cutter detached from starboard. Sava’s face appeared in its tiny forward window, then vanished behind the carrier truss.

Rhen sealed the lock remotely. Pressure remained stable. The command blister became a closed room inside a ship already preparing to exist elsewhere.

“Evacuation boundary in ten seconds,” the timing system announced.

Rhen checked the clock cage through an internal camera. Each catch stayed seated. The fiber trace remained green.

At three minutes twenty, Pelagos cutters crossed the safety line.

“Crew evacuated,” Rhen said.

Sal confirmed every beacon. “All eight clear. Rhen, proceed to spindle.”

“Not yet.”

“Your procedure says now.”

“My oscillator says origin capture is incomplete.”

Talia looked at the same trace. “Four seconds behind model.”

“The model doesn’t own this bearing.” Rhen felt the cradle vibration through the seat frame. A worn compensator in the carrier’s lower spine had not accepted the gate frequency. Automatic control wanted to mask it. Rhen disabled the mask.

The ship lurched starboard.

North Anchor’s horizon doubled. One Kest carried the brown northern scar. The other showed cloud forest along the same continent, impossible in the present and physically coherent inside the branch field.

“Origin capture now,” Rhen said.

The clock trace closed around both horizons.

They released the command cradle, caught the rail, and pulled toward the evacuation spindle. Gravity had become unreliable. Deck and wall traded authority every few steps. A maintenance cabinet opened and scattered ceramic fuses through the passage.

Pelagos called thirty seconds to terminal commitment.

Rhen reached the spindle, climbed inside, and clipped Sava’s key to the manual release. The hatch resisted closure because the frame had twisted under the origin field. They kicked the lower corner until the seal light changed from red to amber.

“Amber seal,” Sal said. “Abort ejection.”

“Abort means ship and clock fall out before destination.”

“It means you live.”

“No. It means I remain aboard for an unshielded arrival.”

Rhen armed the spindle.

Maelin counted reference corrections from the pit. Her voice slowed between numbers. “Plus point zero six. Hold. Minus point zero one. Hold.”

A technician shouted that temperature had crossed the suit limit.

“Maelin,” Talia said, “transfer local hold.”

“No transfer available while the route locks.”

“You wrote a manual handoff.”

“For stable load.” Maelin coughed once. “This is not stable load.”

Rhen could do nothing for her from the spindle. The knowledge sat badly in the body. A captain expected every danger to admit a rope, hatch, pressure margin, or person sent with tools. Pelagos offered none. Maelin had made herself part of a machine that needed her hands longer than those hands could survive.

“Twenty seconds,” Talia said.

Rhen watched the physical clock through its camera. Quartz frequency matched Pelagos on one line and Kest on another. The timing spine had not yet accepted the bridge.

“Come on,” they told the ship.

Not magic. Not friendship. Just a request to machinery whose bad habits they knew.

At twelve seconds, the lower-spine compensator slipped again. Rhen used Sava’s maintenance key to open manual access from the spindle. They drove the field trim two marks past safe. The carrier shuddered. One truss brace tore free and spun toward the ring.

At nine seconds, the two clock lines touched.

“Reference acquired at North Anchor,” Talia said.

Rhen pulled the ejection handle.

Nothing happened.

The twisted hatch frame had blocked the mechanical release. Sava’s key still hung in the panel. Rhen turned it hard enough to break the plastic grip, opened the explosive circuit, and struck both contacts with the metal shaft.

The spindle left Morrow Glass sideways.

Acceleration drove Rhen’s head against the restraint. Pelagos, Kest, and the black ring traded places across the small port. The spindle cleared one meter. Two. Its drive pack fired late, pushing it through the carrier’s forward field edge.

For an interval too short to measure by pain, Rhen occupied both motion solutions. Their inner ears reported rotation in opposite directions. The restraint held the chest while the skull insisted that down had split.

Then the spindle cleared three meters.

Morrow Glass committed without them.

The carrier vanished from Pelagos in sections. Cargo trusses went first, followed by the scarred spine and warm mechanical rooms. The command blister held longest. Through its glass, Rhen saw the empty cradle where they had sat. Then Kest took the ship.

North Anchor reported arrival within forty-one centimeters of target. The extraction rig caught the clock cage before the port truss struck the cradle wall. Timing data flooded the failed spine. Forty-seven gate clocks corrected toward the physical bridge.

The carrier did not survive extraction. Its forward truss folded. The command blister split along the line that had appeared in the discarded arrival during the first crossing. Cradle clamps failed under the lateral load, and North Anchor pushed the wreck into a safe disposal orbit before it could damage the ring.

Rhen saw none of that directly. The spindle tumbled above Pelagos while their body argued with every direction. They fired the attitude jets and made the spin worse.

“Hands off,” Sava said through the rescue channel. “Your telemetry shows vestibular conflict. Let the cutter catch you.”

“I can arrest it.”

“You are feeding the wrong axis.”

Rhen opened their hands.

The cutter matched the tumble, caught the spindle in a flexible collar, and pulled it away from the ring. Motion continued inside Rhen after the stars stopped. They vomited against the suit seal and could not tell whether they lay faceup.

Talia’s voice returned, distant through medical traffic. “The timing spine is stable. Reception restarting.”

“Maelin?” Rhen asked.

No one answered immediately.

On the open phase circuit, Maelin called one final deviation. “Reference level. Lock it there.”

A technician confirmed the lock.

Metal struck metal below her microphone. The coolant alarm continued. Maelin did not speak again.

Sal sent officers into the pit once the automatic reference accepted the physical clock. They reached her after the branch load transferred. The medical channel used exact terms: thermal injury, inhalation, no pulse, resuscitation stopped under mass-casualty protocol.

Rhen closed their eyes. The sensation of falling intensified.

At Orison Habitat, reception frames opened again. Kest-Blue’s last coherent districts entered the queue while its census continued to drop. Names moved across the cutter’s medical display beside Rhen’s vital signs.

Sava climbed through the collar and found the broken maintenance key still clenched in Rhen’s hand.

“You owe me a ship,” Rhen said.

Sava secured their head between padded blocks. “You owe me a key.”

Beyond the spindle port, Pelagos held one sky. Far across the network, North Anchor’s disposal thrusters touched the broken hull of Morrow Glass and pushed it clear of the gate.

Chapter 27 — The Common Future

Jun watched Final Fall wait for her hand.

The terminal key remained red on the Continuity House table. Its authority reached Pelagos through a protected command route that Reciprocal Routing had not closed. One touch would force the reserve petals inward, break Maelin’s phase reference, and end the conflict between standing law and emergency rescue.

It would also kill everyone still coherent in Kest-Blue.

Rain crossed the chamber windows in measured lines. The court pumps had begun their morning cycle despite the capital’s all-night emergency. Maintenance kept its schedule because someone had inspected the filters, replaced a valve, and assigned enough water to an indoor sky. Jun had spent her life protecting systems whose reliability permitted citizens to mistake dependence for nature.

The wall display showed those systems under half load. Cargo held at origins. Clinics counted remaining stocks. Food routes had shifted to local slow transfer. At Orison Habitat, branch citizens emerged into unfinished halls while reception workers recorded names. Pelagos carried Maelin Drave’s death notice beside an active phase lock.

A cabinet officer stood behind Jun. “Director, the route has exceeded statutory instability limits.”

“I can read the limits.”

“Final Fall now has mandatory force.”

“Mandatory upon whose execution?”

The officer hesitated. “Yours.”

There lay the institution’s oldest convenience. A procedure could carry obligation across a hundred desks, then place the final harm beneath one finger and call it necessity. Jun had used that structure after Sable. Six million dead had given every later caution a face. She had believed that if she accepted the last decision, secrecy became a burden she carried rather than power she exercised.

Eleven years of evidence had disproved her.

“Open the full cabinet record,” she said.

“Continuity Black prevents public release during network instability.”

“I am revoking the classification.”

“You no longer hold sole command over Pelagos security.”

“I retain classification authority until the Council dissolves it. Open the record.”

The officer entered the command reluctantly. Cabinet minutes, structured-signal reports, traffic increases, suppressed casualty models, and Talia’s old warning moved into the public archive. Jun added her own orders rather than letting staff summarize them. She had continued traffic after biological structure appeared. She had approved language calling cultural returns nonperson residuals. She had accelerated Final Fall when public assemblies demanded delay.

None of those decisions began with a wish to kill. The record did not need motive to soften action.

A Council channel opened across the rain court. Representatives appeared in stacked windows, some inside emergency chambers and others on gate floors. They spoke over one another until Jun muted the general circuit.

“I am canceling Final Fall,” she said.

The terminal demanded a reason code. Existing options included technical resolution, false alarm, superseding continuity threat, and enemy interference. None described an order that had become unlawful through knowledge its authors suppressed.

Jun selected OTHER and typed: ORDER WOULD DESTROY A VERIFIED CIVIL POPULATION. DIRECTOR ACCEPTS RESPONSIBILITY FOR DELAYED RECOGNITION AND ATTEMPTED EXECUTION.

She pressed the key.

Red changed to gray.

At Pelagos, Final Fall actuators withdrew from ready state. Security systems released their automatic conflict with joint control. The Council windows filled with demands.

“You have exceeded cabinet authority.”

“You concealed personhood findings from this body.”

“Network shutdown has already produced fatalities.”

“Who governs routing now?”

Jun unmuted them. “For the emergency interval, the joint gatekeepers’ command governs only the active reception and network stabilization. Local assemblies retain civil authority. No Ministry officer may restore Final Fall or reclaim Pelagos without a recorded Council vote.”

A representative from Arclight leaned toward his camera. “And afterward?”

“Afterward, this Ministry cannot credibly audit the exclusions it authorized.”

The sentence entered the record before anyone could protect the institution from it.

Jun transmitted transfer orders to Sal Varo, the forty-seven gatekeepers, and the civilian reception compact. She removed the classified command path that had allowed Continuity House to reach reserve systems without public notice. Technical officers protested that an open route could invite sabotage.

“Then publish the danger and guard it lawfully,” Jun said. “Hidden access has already been used against citizens.”

Her deputy asked whether she intended to resign.

“Resignation would let me choose the terms of departure.”

“What do you intend?”

“To remain available for arrest and testimony.”

The word arrest quieted the chamber more effectively than command authority.

Jun ordered security at Continuity House to preserve every office. No private deletion. No cabinet immunity claim. No destruction under instability rules. She surrendered her personal audit key to a clerk who had worked for her six years and had never been invited into the classified meetings that shaped his department.

“Who takes directorship?” he asked.

“No one.”

The Council had not yet voted to dissolve anything. Jun could not declare a replacement by confession. She could, however, refuse the fiction that one new guardian repaired an office built around secret disposal.

“Route questions go to joint command,” she said. “Institutional questions go to the Council in public session.”

A new figure appeared on the wall: the Kest-Blue census.

Instantiation had entered its final civic groups. The number fell when coherence failed at Pelagos and rose nowhere because reception preserved identity rather than creating additional citizens. At first contact, 9,412,806 people had petitioned for recognition. The present register counted those received, those still in process, and those lost.

Jun watched the last category settle.

83,219.

No range followed it. No uncertainty band offered a place to hide. The figure included North Adar, families in failed feeder zones, patients whose infrastructure arrived too late, and citizens lost inside the final Ilex bracket before Morrow Glass restored the clock. It did not include current-world deaths from rationing and delayed cargo. Those required another ledger, another audit, and names of their own.

Jun ordered the census frozen as a verified closing figure, not a memorial estimate. Civic registrars could still correct names, household links, and duplicate reports, but any change to the total would require public evidence from both Pelagos and a reception site. A cabinet statistician asked whether that standard would trap known uncertainty inside the count.

“It will display uncertainty beside the count,” Jun said. “It will not let this office revise the dead when a cleaner number becomes convenient.”

She added a field for unresolved identities. Twelve reception records lacked enough continuity to confirm whether a missing citizen had arrived under a damaged name. Those cases remained open. They did not reduce the verified loss without proof.

The cabinet officer behind Jun began to cry without sound.

Jun did not touch him. Comfort from the person who had led the concealment would ask him to care for her remorse.

She opened a testimony statement instead.

“My name is Jun Orra,” she said to the public record. “I directed the Ministry of Stellar Continuity. After the AY 132 findings, I classified evidence that exclusion fields carried structured biological and cultural information. I authorized continued transit. I later signed Final Fall. No subordinate instruction required me to make those choices.”

She described Sable because it had shaped her judgment. She did not offer it as acquittal. Six million dead during a nine-day closure had taught her what networks could cost. It had not granted ownership over every life outside the public account.

When she finished, the rain court pumps stopped for scheduled maintenance. Water slid down the glass and left the artificial garden visible in hard morning light.

Council security entered through the low doors. Jun placed both hands on the table before they asked.

Talia reached Orison Habitat while its new residents still wore reception blankets.

The shuttle docked against an unfinished spoke. Bare structural ribs crossed the ceiling, tagged for panels that had been sacrificed as prepared mass. Air moved in uneven gusts because the wind system had not learned the ring’s open spaces. Every sign carried two dates: Concord standard and the Kest-Blue civic calendar.

Talia stepped from the shuttle into a queue of people trying to find relatives across forty-seven reception sites.

She had expected noise. The reception hall instead held pockets of concentration. Registrars bent over paper rosters when network links failed. Medics examined pupils and lungs. Families repeated names into search terminals. Some people stared through the ring windows at stars arranged exactly as their science predicted and nothing like the sky they had left.

Isca stood near Frame Seven with Aru asleep against her shoulder.

Talia recognized the red coat before Isca’s face. The moon card remained tied to its front, bent at one corner. Isca had one hand under the child’s knees and the other braced against a column whenever Orison’s irregular wind pushed through the hall.

Nemi had not arrived yet.

Talia stopped beyond speaking distance. She would not make Isca receive policy while waiting for her wife.

A reception controller approached with the final Ilex roster. “Group Eight split across Orison and Ossa. Three brackets. We have ninety-four percent name confirmation.”

Isca shifted Aru’s weight. “Where did the shelter closer resolve?”

“Unconfirmed.”

“Search yellow neighborhood vest. Adult woman, thirty-three, old plague mask scar at the left temple.”

“We already sent physical descriptors.”

“Send them again without the civic relay title. She may have removed the vest.”

The controller obeyed.

Aru woke and looked past Isca toward the frame. “Did Mama close it?”

“Yes,” Isca said. “She closed the school shelter.”

“Then why isn’t she here?”

“Because the route put people in more than one place.”

The answer neither promised nor frightened beyond what they knew.

Talia turned toward the arrival board. Ossa’s third bracket showed a delayed group recovering from mass shear. Two names lacked local matches. One image displayed only a woman’s back as she argued with a medic and pointed toward a transmitter.

Yellow cloth showed beneath the silver blanket.

Talia carried the board to Isca. “Ossa feed.”

Isca set Aru down so quickly that the wind nearly took the blanket. She touched the image with two fingers. The woman turned at the far end of the feed. Dust marked her face. A pale scar crossed her left temple.

Nemi saw the Orison return image and stopped arguing.

For several seconds, network delay made both families watch each other discover the connection at different times. Then Aru shouted. Nemi pressed both hands to her mouth. Isca closed her eyes, opened them again, and began asking the controller for the next permitted transfer window.

No ceremonial words survived the practical questions.

“Ossa to Orison civilian packet opens in nineteen hours,” the controller said. “Physical transit cannot resume before synchronization review.”

“Nineteen,” Isca repeated.

“Nemi can remain on continuous local channel after the packet lane opens.”

“Book it.”

Aru put his moon card against the camera so Nemi could see that he still had it.

Talia stepped away.

Beyond the reception hall, Orison’s young park curved uphill with the ring. Newly planted trees leaned under erratic fans. West paths ended at pressure barriers where service cars had been stripped for instantiation margin. Citizens from two Kests sat on supply crates and compared place names. A current-Kest nurse showed a Kest-Blue water engineer how to secure a blanket against the gusts. Neither behaved as if history had reconciled them.

Sal called from Pelagos. “Council emergency session has a dissolution motion.”

“For the Ministry?”

“For the Ministry and Continuity cabinet. Replacement charter uses the working title Commons of Futures.”

Talia disliked the name on first hearing. It sounded broad enough to conceal the same machinery beneath generous language.

“Who controls routing?” she asked.

“Forty-seven gatekeepers under published assembly mandates until charter ratification. Every exclusion allocation enters an open ledger. Independent audit has access but no operational vote.”

“Who appoints the auditor?”

“That argument has occupied forty minutes.”

“Good.”

Sal sent the motion. It barred classified reserve accounts, required a public reason for every delay and closure, established representation for instantiated Kest-Blue councils, and separated audit from routing command. It did not guarantee agreement. It did not create capacity lost to Reciprocal Routing. It could not deliver medicine faster than half-speed gates allowed.

The first amendment came from a current-Kest food assembly. Its delegates wanted emergency priority to remain with populations already dependent on established routes. Kest-Blue councils objected that “established” would turn 143 years of advantage into a permanent legal claim. A clinic coalition proposed priority by predicted deaths. Cargo unions answered that narrow mortality models ignored seed, maintenance, and delayed infrastructure.

Talia listened from Orison while reception crews rolled empty vapor tanks past the registry door.

A Council clerk asked her for neutral wording.

“There is no neutral wording,” she said. “State who gains priority under each version and publish the dependency model.”

The clerk looked annoyed, which reassured her.

Modelers ran the alternatives against the next thirty days. Existing-route priority kept Arclight clinics stable and cut settlement water deliveries. Predicted-death priority moved medicine quickly but allowed three outer-system seed banks to cross irreversible spoilage thresholds. Equal system shares ignored population and travel distance. Every proposal produced losses its authors had described as secondary.

Isca joined the session from the reception hall. Aru slept beneath the table while Nemi waited on a delayed link from Ossa.

“Do not assign Kest-Blue one representative,” Isca said. “We had district councils, labor groups, rural claims, and political opponents before you recognized us. Instantiation did not make us one constituency.”

An Arclight delegate asked how those councils could retain authority after their territory ceased to exist.

“The same way your trade courts retain claims over cargo between systems,” Isca replied. “Through people, records, and consent. Geography does not become your concern only when ours is gone.”

The emergency articles created temporary seats for each reception-site civic assembly, with Kest-Blue districts allowed to reconstitute across sites. No ceremonial branch seat could speak for everyone. Household relocation claims remained reviewable after crisis assignment. Skills used during rescue created no labor debt to the receiving habitat.

A second dispute concerned gatekeepers. Several Council members wanted them to hold exclusive authority because they understood the machinery. Hadran’s union submitted its shutter record as an objection. Technical expertise had detected the drive lag only after civic workers forced the raw process open.

The adopted language required three keys for nonroutine commitment: technical, local civic, and receiving-side safety. An emergency could shorten debate, but it could not erase which key acted or why. Any hidden route rendered the decision presumptively invalid.

Talia added a separate requirement for dissent. “If five gates object, their reasons stay beside the commitment even when the majority proceeds.”

“Would that not weaken public confidence?” a Council member asked.

“Confidence that depends on removing disagreement belongs to the former Ministry.”

They kept the dissent clause.

The Council adopted emergency articles after six hours of public amendments. The Ministry dissolved pending archive transfer and trial review. Continuity House lost its command circuits before local sunset. Workers who had never entered the cabinet retained their jobs under temporary civic service, because accountability did not require destroying every person who knew how to maintain a gate.

Talia received the audit appointment with a restriction she had proposed herself. She could inspect any routing decision and compel a public response. She held no authority to commit a crossing. The person naming hidden cost would not secretly choose whose cargo moved.

Her first task waited inside Orison’s borrowed registry office.

The desk had once belonged to habitat construction. Dust outlined removed tools. One wall remained unfinished, exposing cable runs labeled by hand. Talia connected the new public veil ledger to a display facing the corridor rather than her chair.

People gathered without invitation: gate workers, Kest-Blue registrars, current-Kest cargo families, reporters, two Council clerks, and Isca with Aru beside her. Nemi’s delayed image occupied a tablet propped against the wall from Ossa.

Talia entered the closing figures.

Verified Kest-Blue population at contact: 9,412,806.

Citizens instantiated: 9,329,587.

Citizens lost: 83,219.

She added unresolved name discrepancies beneath the verified total instead of burying them inside it. A second column opened for current-world route deaths, supply injuries, and deferred-care claims. Their count remained incomplete. The ledger displayed that incompleteness in amber.

A reporter asked, “Does this mean the rescue succeeded?”

Talia looked toward Isca.

Isca answered. “It means my son is here. My wife is alive on Ossa. It also means eighty-three thousand two hundred nineteen people did not arrive. Write all of that.”

A cargo representative asked when ordinary transit would resume.

“After gate-by-gate safety review,” Talia said. “Forecast capacity sits near half the old network. Priority rules remain under public assembly control.”

“Near half for how long?”

“No honest model gives a short answer.”
