The representative swore under his breath. He had family waiting on Kest and no reason to find institutional reform comforting. Talia entered his delayed route claim and gave him its public identifier.

At the bottom of the display, a field requested the ledger’s custodian.

Old Ministry practice required an office name and sealed key. Talia entered no custodian. She assigned mirrored copies to all forty-seven gate archives, the Kest-Blue civic councils, and the public network.

Then she opened the first public veil ledger.

The corridor display filled with routes. Each crossing showed its prepared histories, preserved exclusion share, cargo priority, dissent, and measured delay. Nothing in the record promised innocence. It showed who had chosen, what they knew, and who could challenge the choice.

Outside the registry office, Orison’s faulty wind pushed a scatter of paper intake cards along the young park path. Aru ran after his moon card before it reached the pressure barrier. Isca caught one corner, he caught the other, and together they carried it back toward Nemi’s waiting screen.

Epilogue — An Audit of Spring

Three years after Pelagos, Orison’s first spring began with rain going sideways.

Talia watched it strike the registry windows in long diagonal taps. The habitat’s wind system still overcorrected when warm park air reached the unfinished western spoke. Children treated each gust as weather. They ran beneath young trees with their coats open, shouting warnings from one calendar to another.

The spring audit waited on Talia’s desk.

Commons of Futures staff had marked the disputed crossing in amber. A Kest agricultural cooperative wanted two hundred tonnes of cloud-forest seedlings moved to Orison. An Arclight clinic claimed the same route window for surgical cultures. Capacity across the Concord held near half its former throughput. Reciprocal Routing preserved every exclusion share, but preservation demanded time, prepared margin, and forty-seven published commitments.

No option came free of harm. That fact no longer permitted a sealed answer.

Talia opened the hearing channel. Representatives filled the public wall: farmers from both versions of Kest, clinic logisticians, gate engineers, patient advocates, and three citizens who had entered objections without institutional backing. The routing panel sat in a separate column. Talia could question its evidence and require corrections. She had no routing vote.

“Your seed manifest assumes full survival after a twenty-hour hold,” she told the cooperative.

A Kest-Blue forester shook his head. “The manifest assumes sixty-eight percent. The summary converted viable stock into loaded stock.”

Talia highlighted the omitted column. “Commons staff will correct it before deliberation.”

The clinic representative leaned toward her camera. “Our cultures lose all utility after fourteen hours.”

“Your filing lists a substitute source on Veyr.”

“Veyr’s batch serves pediatric cases. Diverting it moves risk rather than removing it.”

“Then the public record needs those cases.”

A clerk added them. Nobody thanked Talia. An auditor who depended on gratitude had chosen the wrong work.

Outside the registry office, a slow-route training carrier crossed Orison’s inner window. It moved on chemical thrust toward the local transfer ring rather than committing at once. Rhen taught from its command blister twice each week.

Their vestibular damage had never settled. They walked with a cane in rotating sections and kept one hand near a fixed rail when the habitat wind shifted. Cadets learned to read thrust from instruments because their captain no longer trusted the body’s report of down. Rhen claimed this made them a better teacher. Sava claimed it made them less able to demonstrate bad habits.

A message from Isca waited beneath the hearing file. She had declined another invitation to serve as ceremonial spokesperson for the instantiated councils. Her reply contained four lines: she worked weather, Nemi taught emergency planning, Aru had school, and none of those facts created a permanent public office.

Attached beneath it, Aru had sent a forecast for the young park. He predicted lateral rain at thirteen hundred and requested better roof drains. Talia forwarded it to habitat maintenance.

The routing panel began deliberation in public. Two members favored the cultures. One proposed splitting the window, which gate engineers rejected because two commitments consumed more preserved margin than either cargo could supply. A patient advocate asked whether Orison could delay seedlings until the next cloud-water cycle. The forester answered with root viability figures rather than an appeal to heritage.

Talia listened for missing consequences.

Across the wall, a smaller feed showed Jun Orra testifying at the final month of her trial. Jun wore no director’s gray. She described the AY 132 classification meeting and named each authority she had used. Her defense had introduced Sable mortality records. Jun agreed they explained her fear, then refused the attorney’s suggestion that fear removed choice.

The court had not yet ruled. Commons procedure required the testimony link because several current routing safeguards depended on failures under review. Accountability moved slower than confession.

The public loss ledger beside the trial feed held 12,441 verified current-world deaths from rationing, deferred care, and route accidents during the changeover. Another 2,106 claims remained disputed. Kest-Blue’s dead did not shrink beside those figures; neither column served as subtraction.

At Orison, the panel chose the surgical cultures. It also ordered a local mass release to preserve half the seed stock until the next window. The cooperative filed an immediate objection. Its text appeared beside the decision, not beneath a closed appeal seal.

Talia found one remaining defect. The panel’s reason cited patient count but omitted the seedlings’ reduced survival after storage.

“Return the decision for correction,” she said.

A panel member frowned. “The omitted figure does not alter our vote.”

“It alters what the public can judge.”

The corrected reason arrived nine minutes later. Talia signed the audit line without endorsing the route choice.

Rain rattled harder against the glass. In the park, maintenance workers opened a drain while children moved the seedlings under a temporary roof. The surgical cultures entered commitment at Kest Gate. Forty-seven exclusion shares appeared across the public veil ledger, each measured and named.

The cooperative’s objection remained visible beside the crossing after the carrier arrived.
